
I
The dust of the summer covered the streets. I walked slowly-
because my heart wasn't in it-toward Montpelier Square.
Francis Farrell, my father-in-law, lived alone in a house off
the square with his son, Roger. They were always in each
other's company; and my wife, Rosie, would have been eter-
nally in her mother's arms if I hadn't put my foot down on
that score already before our marriage. Farrell and his wife,
Hilda, separated when Roger was eight and Rosie, whom they
all called Rosemary because she was christened Rosemary, six
and a half. There was no question of divorce, for they were
Catholics. Hilda brought up Rosie, and Roger became his
father's property; and I kept my distance from Hilda and
Farrell and Roger as much as circumstances allowed. Yet on
that summer evening I was on my way to see Farrell on a
small but for me urgent matter. I had no choice-one more
reason why my heart wasn't in it.
The Farrells weren't only Catholics: they were rich too. I
was neither. They feverishly pretended in my presence that
such matters could be overlooked. Things were also a little
topsy-turvy where age was concerned, for Rosie was twenty-
six years younger than I, which made her twenty-four; Far-rell, my father-in-law, was two years younger than I. He
brought that up whenever the opportunity occurred or was
found, then laughed it off, but was mighty pleased with be-
ing my junior. He was, moreover, of the opinion that people
took him for Roger's elder brother.
I still had a couple of bob on me, so I went into a pub. Far-
rell wasn't expecting me; therefore he could wait. The pub
reeked of gentility; the stale sausages on the counter waited
for flies. Behind the counter was a wide mirror which re-
flected my dirty gray beard. I smoothed down my few tufts
of hair. The beard, the practically bald crown and the dark
glasses made me look quite formidable and a trifle forbidding
in my own eyes.
"Half a bitter," I said to the bespectacled barmaid. In an
hour's time, I hoped, I would be able to afford a more sub-
stantial drink. The bar was quiet, since we were approaching
the holiday season. Beside me stood a sunburned man; at a
table sat a woman proud of her sunburned arms.
It was my chronic penury that brought me together with
the rich man's daughter. A friend of mine, a faithful fellow,
ran an art school. Aware of my persistent lack of money, he
engaged me to give a biweekly lecture on the history of paint-
ing. Of painting I knew no more than the average man who
stares through the window at Zwemmer's in Charing Cross
Road. A number of young ladies attended my lectures.
Frankly, I didn't notice Rosie for several months. When I
did, it was because she persisted. At our first private chat I
said if she were fat, Rubens would have painted her. She
wasn't flattered. Her skin was as white as milk and swans,
her eyes a bit like forget-me-nots, her hair straw colored. It
took me a few more months to notice that the girl was set
on me. I found that surprising. Far bigger was my sur-
prise when one winter afternoon my landlady came into my
bed-sitting room and said a young lady wished to see me.
Then in walked Rosie.
”
"You shouldn't have come, I said.
"I wanted to come," she said.
"Would you like tea?" I asked. "I could get it.""I didn't come for that."
She gave herself to me in fact, threw herself at me in a
grimly determined fashion. On the next day, for still obscure
reasons, I didn't go to Moura, with whom I was having an
affair with mutual lack of enthusiasm. Three days later Rosie
informed me that she would marry me. I laughed. "You
must be out of your head," I said gently. "I'm a man with
no fame and no money. I'm the author of great books that
will never be written. I can't support a wife. I can't sup-
port even myself."
She rode roughshod over my objections. "It was an awful
sin," she said. "It can be redeemed only through marriage."
I observed that I was uninterested in and unmoved by sin.
"I've told mummy," she said. "I told her I might be preg-
nant. That settled it. My father... he doesn't enter into it.
Of course mummy was terribly upset."
"I bet she was," I said, and her mother was far more upset
when we came face to face for the first time. Rosie and I
lunched with her at her club. Hilda was a tall woman with a
flat bosom and a fine aquiline nose, her complexion pale, her
graying hair brushed back. I saw her point and said, to cheer
her up: "Your daughter of course is quite mad. Fancy want-
ing to marry me." She nodded coldly. "Before we go on," I
continued, "I want to make this clear. I was born in genteel
poverty. From genteel poverty I moved into sheer poverty
wherein I still find myself, and honestly I have no desire to
leave it. If I come by a few pennies I blow them; if I don't
have a few pennies, then I look for somebody who is willing to
buy me a drink. When I come by pennies again then I buy
him a drink and so it goes."
"My husband's family is very rich," said Hilda disapprov-
ingly. So we had something in common. "Rosemary," she
went on, "receives a big allowance from the family trust."
"I feared that," I said.
She nodded, then spoke to Rosie of her dogs. Like every-
thing else, that meal came to an end too.
The Farrells made agricultural machinery. They had been
making it for three or four generations. There were fourbrothers; the eldest, Matthew, was the chairman of Farrell &
Sons. My Farrell was the second brother; I didn't retain the
names of the other brothers, though I met them both at the
wedding. One of these brothers was an M.P. The factory was
at Reading. My Farrell ran the London office which was, to
my surprise, in the heart of Mayfair. In my opinion offices
should be in the heart of the City. Hilda lived in Wiltshire.
My first visit to Orchard House, her home on the edge of a
small village which had ceased to pretend to picturesqueness
when a nylon factory and dozens of new government-built
houses went up, was memorable chiefly because of the dogs,
Sealyhams, who jumped all over you and ran the place. There
was a lady-housekeeper called Mrs. Walsh, a widow with an
ample bosom. She giggled a lot. She had been with Hilda for
twelve years and they were past disliking each other. She
came in answer to an advertisement Hilda put into the Times
asking for a Catholic lady-housekeeper. Mrs. Walsh took a
liking to me, which was a pleasant change in that house. In
an aside punctuated by giggles she whispered that Hilda
drank a lot of brandy late at night. "She isn't a secret
drinker, don't you know, Mr. Danesbridge. She's a lone
drinker."
I said I approved of that.
"If I had known that Rosemary wasn't pregnant I'd have
stopped the wedding," Hilda said to me after the wedding.
Just to annoy her, I made her a grandmother in less than
a year.
The clock in the pub said it was ten to seven. I thought
I had better hurry if I wanted to find Farrell at home. Rich
men like him dine on caviar punctually at eight o'clock.
Montpelier Square looked dead tired in the heat. The Far-
rells' house was in a side street. I found it a dismal house
because it was hardly lived in. Once I peeped into the draw-
ing room, which was on the first floor. You saw at once that no
party had been thrown there for donkey's years. I rang the
bell: Roger burst out of the house.
"I thought it was my taxi," he said in an angry, disap-
pointed voice.I was just as disappointed to see him. His presence would
hamper the eloquence my urgent matter needed.
"My father's in the study," he said. "Come in. Where's
that taxi? I'm going to be late."
"It'll come, " I said soothingly.
"It's Jasper," said Roger to his father, opening the study
door. "I told you I'd be late." He spoke reproachfully. One
needed no eagle's eyes to perceive that they had had an un-
pleasant argument.
The doorbell rang. "There's your taxi," said Farrell.
"See you probably when I get back," said Roger. He said
that to his father, not to me. The front door closed behind him.
"Glad to have found you in," I said. A bottle of whisky
stood on a table. "I'll help myself to a whisky, if I may."
Father and son were comparatively tall. But for their po-
tato noses they would have been handsome. They looked alike
except for their eyes. Farrell's were unquiet, questing eyes,
with a touch of melancholia; Roger's eyes were those of a well-
to-do, well-fed young man. The study was crammed with
furniture. When Farrell ceased using the drawing room he
brought his treasures into the study. Above the fireplace hung
a Toulouse-Lautrec, which the grandfather probably brought
back from Paris at the turn of the century as a souvenir of a
night out; the Utrillo above the writing table was probably
bought by his father in memory of a visit to Montmartre be-
fore the First World War.
Drip by drip I filled a small tumbler with whisky. Farrell
looked like one who had come through an ordeal against his
wish and better judgment. I tossed down the whisky, then
sighed. "There's nothing like having a hospitable father-in-
law," I said.
He asked how Rosie was.
"She's very well," I said. "She's dining with some friends
of hers who aren't my friends, so I didn't go with her."
"Pity," said Farrell. Those who make a mess of their own
marriage are always ready to impart the secret of marital
felicity.
I waited; he said nothing. "Because marriage should be acompromise?" I said. "Marriage isn't. It's no good saying
'I like your friends' when you don't like them. It's better to
keep one's friends in separate compartments. Rosie has hers,
I have mine; so we don't have to compromise." I saw he
wasn't listening. To be frank, I wasn't listening either.
"Look here," I said, to get it over, "I want you to do me a
favor. It's really the first one I've ever asked of you. I've
written an article, a short one, for the .
never mind, it's a
small literary magazine you never heard of, but it's solvent,
which is the main thing for you. I won't get their check before
the end of the month, but I repeat: it's perfectly all right.
Could you as a favor advance it to me? I'll give you a post-
dated check."
"Most certainly, my dear fellow. Of course. How much
is it?" He had already brought out his checkbook.
"Three guineas, " I said. He stared at me. "I need it," I said
a little anxiously, "and I'm giving you a postdated check."
He put away his checkbook. My heart went cold. He took a
roll of notes from his pocket, quickly counted three ones and put
them on the writing table.
"Don't you want more?" he asked. "Three sounds very
little to me."
"Plenty for me," I said, and wrote out a check.
"You're a lucky man," he said. "You'll go to Heaven."
I tittered into my beard. "Why?" asked.
"Read the Sermon on the Mount and you'll know why."
That wasn't my line of country, so I laughed again. "Must you
give me a check?" he asked. I told him that was part of the
bargain. "You should ask Rosemary for such small loans,"
he said. "She's your wife."
"That's not part of the bargain." I looked at the clock. “I
ought to be moving. You surely have a dinner date."
"I haven't. Why don't you come and dine with me?" I
declined. He urged me.
"I always plan ahead," I lied. “Dinner in a restaurant
wasn't my plan tonight, so forgive me for saying no." I
couldn't tell him that few things bored me as much as being
taken out to dinner."As you like," he said. "Stay a little longer if you can." I
nodded. That wouldn't involve me.
mary get home?''
"By closing time," I said.
"What time will Rose-
He asked me to help myself to a drink. He hardly drank.
"Rosemary said the other day you're seriously thinking of
writing a novel." That made me laugh.
"Very seriously," I said. He didn't understand, so plied
me with questions which I answered without bothering to lis-
ten to my words. The man was a frustrated dreamer, a neu-
rotic whose neurosis was worsened by his admirable self-
control. I was sure that if he could bring himself to shriek or
bring the house down he would feel better. But self-control
was easy if you had no money troubles. If a woman broke his
heart he could go to Taormina or drink himself to death. I
could never afford either. Suddenly and for the first time I
became interested in him.
"What sort of childhood did you have?" I interrupted him.
"Childhood?" I nodded vigorously. "Quite an ordinary
childhood."
"Was it unhappy?"
"Not in the least."
"Explain," I commanded, pouring out more whisky. He
had something to hide; something worried him-perhaps in-
cest, perhaps some other awful memory.
"A strange request," said Farrell, smiling. "It was a pretty
ordinary childhood."
"Go on," I ordered him.
"It was a completely happy childhood. I'm sorry, Jasper,
but it was humdrum and happy."
"You disappoint me," I said. "Were your schooldays un-
happy?" He assured me they weren't. "Weren't your priests
very harsh there?" He shook his head. I lost interest in his
childhood. "Speaking of priests," I said, "darling Rosie is
trying to convert me in a sweet, underhand manner. She
leaves all sorts of little Catholic tracts all over the place, for
me to pick up and read, I suppose. As man to man, tell me,
Francis, do you really believe in the Real Presence at Mass?""It would be easier for me if I didn't," he said in a low
voice.
"I see," I said, not seeing. I helped myself to more whisky.
The man was the perfect host. "Speaking of compromise,
would it be better for all concerned if I became an R.C. my-
self? It would please Rosie enormously and it wouldn't go
against my convictions, since I have none."
"Don't if you don't believe," he nearly shouted. "Don't.
You hear me? Don't. If you can't accept God's love, if
you..."
"Don't upset yourself. I was only joking," I said quickly,
afraid that he would start stripping his soul. Naked souls
didn't appeal to me. "Let's speak of lighter matters. You
haven't been drinking at all and I'm just swilling it down."
With almost parental care I mixed him a whisky and soda.
Anything to keep his soul buttoned up.
"That's very kind of you," he said. "To speak of lighter
matters, how's my little granddaughter? Fancy being a
grandfather at forty-eight."
"That comes of having so virile a son-in-law." He laughed.
It was better like that. A gold cigarette case and a gold lighter
lay before him on the table. His cuff links were of rubies. The
platinum watch chain was quite a contrast to all that gold.
Heat or no heat, he always wore a waistcoat. "Mary's very
well," I said, "but I just can't stand the sight of nanny. I
can see in her perfidious eyes that she has guessed that I never
had a nanny.... I say, it's getting on. I shouldn't be keep-
ing you."
"Oh, no. I like our chat very much."
"But don't you want to go out to eat caviar?"
"Not at all. I don't want to go out."
He was lonely, so lonely that he didn't want to go out. That
was the result of having married so young. It is better to
marry than to burn; therefore, Farrell first married then
started to burn. With me it had been the other way round.
First I burned, then I married. I laughed out loud. "I'm
getting a bit woozy," I said. "Paupers don't often have the
chance to drink tumblers of whisky." He said he would send
me a case of whisky tomorrow. "You're too good to me, butI won't have it. I have my own tiny integrity, which doesn't
include cases of whisky. A bottle perhaps..." I was warm-
ing to him: it might have been the whisky.
"Let's talk of you," I said. "I've often discussed you with
Rosie, especially your marriage. I'm beginning to know a bit
about you Catholics. I'm convinced old Hilda left you be-
cause she didn't want to have any more children."
"No, old boy, she didn't want to have any more me."
"Poor fellow," I said, patting his shoulder. "Does it still
hurt?"
"I don't think so. I don't know; I never discuss it."
"You ought to." He got up and drew the curtains. It was
now stiflingly hot in the study. He sat down. "We never have
a chance to talk," I said. "Probably we won't ever have one
again. But now that we are talking ... why don't you go
the whole hog?" It was my turn to rise. It was too hot to sit.
"What do you mean?" he asked. "The whole hog? I don't
understand you."
"Divorce," I said.
"There's no divorce for Catholics," he said. He said that a
little shiftily.
"I know you'd be outside your Church," I said, trying to
catch his eye. He was looking at the Toulouse-Lautrec. "I've
known plenty of divorced and remarried Catholics in my
time, here and in France. That's what you should do. Get
Hilda to divorce you, or divorce her, then marry; and then
you won't be lonely. Or if you want to be lonely, don't marry;
but be free of her altogether." I shook my head because I was
truly tipsy. "Of course you are free of her altogether. Still
...
well, you know best. I must really go."
"Have one before you go," he said. Roger appeared while
I had it.
"You come, I go," I said. Still under the influence of
drink, I said to Farrell: "You must come and dine with us
very soon." His eyes lit up, for he seldom saw Rosie.
"When?" I asked.
"Would Wednesday of next week do?" he asked. I wanted
to ask Roger too but decided I wasn't tight enough for that.
"Wednesday will suit us," I said. Farrell said he wouldtelephone for a taxi. "The tube is still running," I said.
"I'll walk with you to the tube," said Roger, much to my
surprise.
It wasn't any cooler in the street; nevertheless, I was fast
sobering up. Roger's presence accounted for that. We walked
in silence to Brompton Road. As we turned into it he said:
"I imagine you know a lot of journalists." I said that was
possible. "Do you know a woman journalist called Virginia
Tilton?" I said I didn't. "She works on a glossy magazine."
I made no comment. "She's rather a friend of mine."
"I think I'm going to walk," I said. "It's too hot to take
the tube."
"May I walk as far as Hyde Park Corner with you?" Roger
asked.
I said: "By all means," and wondered why he wanted to
walk with me. He and Rosie lunched together every three
weeks or so. He was too well-mannered to run me down to
her. Of that I was certain. Besides, he must have recovered
by now from the initial shock of his only sister's marrying
me. I was certain too that he had nothing against me. He
had merely expected something better from his sister. We
met about once every three or four months. Mathematically
speaking, that meant we hadn't met a dozen times in our lives.
"I had a date with Virginia at a pub in Fleet Street,"
Roger said. A cat mewed on top of a trash can in a doorway.
"She was dining with some publicity man. Our date was at
six. But my father detained me. I left when you came. It
was past seven by then. I just managed to catch her. She had
to leave. Her dinner was at eight. Naturally she was annoyed
with me."
"Your father shouldn't have detained you," I said. I had
become stone cold sober and was regretting that I had asked
Farrell to dine with us next week. Rosie and he didn't hit it off.
On the other hand, Roger didn't hit it off with his mother.
However, Roger wasn't as shy as Rosie.
"I have no alternative," said Roger. We were approaching
Hyde Park Corner. "My father is alone; he has been alone
since my mother left him. He knows millions of people, buteven if they're millions they can't be all the time at his beck
and call. Mind you, he's not an insisting sort of person; he's
just a very lonely person."
"Hasn't he got a mistress or a girl friend? If I had his money
I'd have a harem. Let me add, only if I were lonely."
"He has a mistress," said Roger, "but that doesn't help.
Look here, I'm not really entitled to speak of it. I shouldn't
have mentioned her. I had no business to." We were at Hyde
Park Corner. A lonely late bus shot past us like a monster
cannonball. "Please don't say a word about it to Rosie."
"Of course not. She's on her mother's side. You are a
strange family. Mother's side and father's side, and they
parted seventeen years ago."
"Somehow it's still that way," he said.
"It's still that way because they aren't divorced."
"We Catholics..." he began.
"Good night, Roger," I said, and walked away. I hoped he
wasn't annoyed with me for leaving him abruptly; not that it
mattered, but I was sorry for him. The fellow was young, and
his father had no business keeping him from his glossy jour-
nalist. And father and son were no business of mine.
"I went to see your dad," I said to Rosie when I got home.
"I happened to be in Knightsbridge so looked in on him. I
asked him to dine with us next Wednesday."
"Oh," she said.
"I know, darling, he frightens you and makes you fidgety,
but don't forget you frighten him and make him fidgety too."
"I'll ask some bright and clever woman to amuse him on
Wednesday," she said. We went to bed in her vast flat off
Regent's Park. The flat had been bought by her trustees soon
after our wedding. On Saturday mornings I was invariably
convinced that I had forgotten something, till I remembered
that I had no weekly rent to pay for the room I slept in. The
bed was vast too. A big wardrobe with a long glass in the middle
faced the bed. The glass reflected her flaxen hair against the
pillow and a dark patch above the quilt: my beard.
"Did he say anything about mummy?" Rosie asked.
"No. Why?""She phoned just before you came in. She said Roger
called her to say he was coming down for the weekend and
my father will come for the day on Sunday."
"What's wrong with that?" I asked.
"Well, Roger was at Orchard House a month ago and you
know how seldom he goes to see mummy." I said I didn't.
"Yes, you know," she insisted. "But why should my father
go down only for the day? Last time he went to see mummy he
stayed the night. That was just after we got married." I
waited for her to go on. "What are they up to?" she asked.
"What can they be up to?" I said. "Nothing; or probably
they want to discuss with her the extra million they want to
put into some family trust or other. What do I know?"
"Mummy doesn't like it," said Rosie.
She turned on her side. I read for a while, then looked at
her. "You're not sleeping," I said.
"I've been thinking," she said.
"About your father and mother and Roger and the week-
end?"
"I don't like it," she said, turning to me. "I've been always
frightened of the thought of his picking up some adventuress
and then going to tell mummy that he wanted a divorce."
"Why must she be an adventuress and why shouldn't he be
free if he wanted to? Your mother is free, isn't she? So why
can't he be free too?" Her eyes said they didn't follow me.
For Rosie to understand what I meant would have been akin
to being born again, and on the condition that Hilda wasn't
her mother.
"He would be miserable and wretched if he broke away
from the Church," said Rosie. "It would ruin his life. He
couldn't carry the weight of being a lapsed Catholic."
That surprised me. I wouldn't have thought that she ever
gave Farrell a thought. "You're fundamentally one of the
nicest people I know," I said, and switched off the light.
II
On the following Monday Rosie telephoned Roger at the
office. Needless to say, Roger was employed by Farrell & Sonsand worked there with his father. "He's in a rotten mood,"
she said, putting down the receiver. I asked who was in a
rotten mood. "Roger," she said. "He said his father ruined
his weekend, which he wanted to spend in London because his
girl was free over the weekend-she's a journalist-but he
went to Orchard House to please his father, and then it turned
out there had been no need for him to go to Orchard House.
I can't make any sense of it, but he could have been more
polite about the weekend."
Though I couldn't figure out what it was about I felt sorry
for Farrell, who evidently hadn't achieved his purpose. From
Monday until Wednesday morning Rosie hoped that her fa-
ther would telephone to say he couldn't dine with us. On
Tuesday evening happiness came her way, for Hilda rang up to
say she would be passing through London on Wednesday with
her dogs on her way to the Tunbridge Wells dog show. Rosie
wanted her to come and lunch at the flat. I was all for it be-
cause the notion of the mother coming to lunch and the father
to dinner rather appealed to me. It wasn't to be. Hilda said
Regent's Park was too far for her, so Rosie went to lunch
with her at her club, which was near Hyde Park Corner, and
I promised to fetch her after the meal.
I knew quite a lot about Hilda, thanks to Mrs. Walsh. Hilda
abhorred sex, which in the beginning she considered a huge
insipid joke, then sheer dirt; and when in the right mood she
would tell Mrs. Walsh that she had left her husband because
he was keen on that sort of dirty thing. To make things worse,
he wanted that dirty thing from his wife. Mrs. Walsh was of
the opinion that if Farrell had had a mistress or picked up
prostitutes the marriage wouldn't have broken up. "Sounds
very strange to me, Mr. Danesbridge, don't you know," Mrs.
Walsh would say, offering me another glass of sweet sherry.
Mother and daughter were having coffee when I arrived in
the club. It was very much a ladies' club, yet I saw more men
than women. "Oh, Jasper," said IIilda. In her tweedy
fashion she found my name extraordinarily funny. Soon after
I married Rosie, she gave one of her Sealyham puppies the
name of Jasper. Thus I became the other Jasper. But greed
is greed, and she sold the dog Jasper when a handsome sumwas offered for him. "Oh, Jasper," she said again. I grinned:
she had the gift of making you feel an imbecile. Her vast feet
held in by gillie shoes protruded from under the table. "Now
don't stand there, Jasper," she said. I came up. "You've
surely seen a collection of old cows before," she said, dropping
her voice. The other women looked furiously at her. I sat
down. "I hear Francis is dining with you tonight," she said.
I nodded. "Coffee, Jasper?" I nodded again. "And a
brandy?" I said I should like one very much. "Then I'll
have one too," she said, and let out a hoarse guffaw. And that
was the woman whom Francis Farrell had taken into a nuptial
bed.
"Mummy says father turned up for breakfast on Sunday,
went to Mass with her and Roger, then lunched, then had a
little siesta and then left."
"You forgot to tell Jasper," said Hilda, "that we did have
a grand emotional moment. But without it I'd have thought
something had gone wrong with him." Came the guffaw.
"Oh," I said, interested. The brandy was tiny, the coffee
cup a thimble. Undoubtedly little flows where women gather.
"We usually go to the chapel of the Augustinian nuns,"
said Hilda, "to Mass on Sundays. You know that, Jasper."
I did. The good nuns had a nursing home to which a chapel
was attached. Hilda preferred the chapel to the church in
Salisbury. Often I stood outside the chapel waiting for Hilda
and Mrs. Walsh and Rosie to come out, watching other Protes-
tant husbands picking up their wives. They had cars; I, of
course, hadn't. "Francis has been umpteen times to the
chapel," went on Hilda, "and he has seen the nuns umpteen
times too. Yet when we came back from Mass he nearly cried,
saying how beautiful they looked and how ecstatic their ex-
pressions were when they received Communion. 'Those eyes
behind grill and veil,' he said." Hilda shook her head. "I
blushed for him," she said. I was sorry for Farrell again. It
must have been the beatific expression of the nuns which
stopped him from telling her some home truths-at any rate
something she wouldn't have enjoyed. The man had fantasy.
I observed that the first time we met. He was shy and diffident.He didn't cherish the idea of two gentlemen having to meet
over the daughter of one of them. "So I told him to have a
good cry and left him in the drawing room, boomed Hilda,
adding, 'Behind grill and veil,' as if he had seen them for
the first time."

She looked at her watch and said it was time she collected
her dogs from the cloakroom and left. "Good luck, darling, this
evening," she said to Rosie. "Keep your spirits up." Then
to me: "I hope he won't be in one of his emotional moods."
She kissed her daughter. "See you on Friday, Jasper," she
said.
Jasper, I said to myself as Rosie and I left the club. Jasper.
At opening time I looked into the French pub in Dean
Street. Fred was there. He was always there. He cast his
lugubrious shadow on every drink one had. I had half an
hour with him; then came a sound like a distant alarm clock
going off. "Time for me to go," I said.
"What noise was that?" asked Fred, who hated everything
new. I pulled my sleeve back to show him my wristwatch,
Rosie's present.
"It has an alarm," I said. "Frightened you, eh?"
"Yeh," said Fred.
"It's Swiss. I can just see a Swiss cheese manufacturer
drinking kirsch with his friends, the alarm going off in the
middle of weighty repartee. 'Ach,' he says, 'my leetle meestress
who is so fat and vite shee is vateing for mee.'"
"That's not how the Swiss speak," said Fred.
"Just because some tourist agency sent you to Switzerland
once, Fred, and you never wrote the article in which the
agency should have been mentioned in a footnote, you think
you know everything about the Swiss. You truly believe, do
you, that they make such watches for men who have no mis-
tresses? Come, come."
"So you have a mistress now?" he asked.
"I haven't, but my father-in-law is dining with us."
"You've sold yourself to the rich all right," said Fred. "A
wrist alarm watch. Not even a mistress. You've become a
strange fellow, Jasper. You've sold your soul to the rich butnot your flesh." Fred looked around and waited for the reac-
tion; but the two men nearest to us had drifted away to speak
to a girl with flat hair.
"You've wasted your remark, Fred," I said. "Still, you'll
find plenty of opportunities to repeat it with your usual spon-
taneity."
"What's your father-in-law like?" Fred asked, as I bought
him a drink.
"He would have made a bishop or a witty abbé in eigh-
teenth-century France."
"What do you know about eighteenth-century clerics in
France?" asked Fred, then swallowed the drink.
"What indeed? I'm off. See you tomorrow, I expect."
A chap we knew came in. Fred called to him: "The trouble
with Jasper is that he has sold his soul to the rich but not his
flesh."
In Frith Street I hailed a cab. I didn't want Fred to see
me getting into a cab. He would have thought my flesh was in
jeopardy. I sat back in the cab. Fred was right, in a sense.
Farrell wasn't an eighteenth-century figure. He should have
lived before. And he wouldn't have made a successful bishop
or an abbé, though I hadn't a clue what made bishops or abbés
successful. In the back of the taxi I saw Farrell as a pilgrim.
There I was, on unsafe ground again. How did you become a
pilgrim? What made pilgrims tick? How long were you a
pilgrim and how did you cease being a pilgrim when you had
your share of pilgrimages? "That's number twenty-eight,"
I said to the cabbie. He looked surprised. "I'm only a lodger
here," I said, after I paid him.
I didn't take the lift: I went up the marble stairs. In
that house I invariably had the impression that I was living
above my imagination. It gave me a sense of disaster which,
however, disappeared once I was with Rosie. She defended
me from the marble steps and high ceilings.
"You'll have to put on your dinner jacket," said Rosie.
"Why?"
"Because he will be wearing one." Pilgrims seldom wear
dinner jackets.
"I've put it out for you, Mr. Danesbridge," said nanny,who seemed to be continuously around. Had she nothing to do
in the nursery? She was a short, round woman of fifty. I often
remarked to Rosie that Mary would never learn to read or
write while we kept nanny.
"She'll be gone by then," said Rosie, which made me laugh,
because one of Rosie's most endearing qualities was that she
didn't understand. Perhaps that was why I married her.
She came into the bedroom while I tried to tie the black tie.
"It's your idea," I said, "so you tie it."
"I'm too fidgety," she said. "I'm always a nervous wreck
when I have to see him."
"He's uneasy and you're ill at ease.

"I've asked Helen Ford," she said, when she had tied the
tie. "She chatters all the time. That'll make it easier." She
stepped back to examine her work. "Why did you have to
ask him? You knew it would be difficult for both of us."
"I asked him because I rather liked him the other night.
Forget that you're your mother's champion, at least for to-
night. The whole thing is very stale."
"But I know what mummy feels," said Rosie; and I lit a
cigarette, since it was more profitable to smoke than to dig
into their family complexes. Had I been sensible I shouldn't
have asked Farrell. "The tie's straight," said Rosie, and we
went to the drawing room. There was a bottle of Ricard,
which cheered me up at once.
"You're a very good girl," I said. She went off to the
kitchen to see how Mrs. Adams, our daily woman who came in
on such occasions, was faring.
I poured a good measure of Ricard into a glass, contem-
plated the ice, and in it went; then I poured water into the
glass and, as always, it gave me satisfaction to see the liquid
change color. Slowly Helen Ford pushed herself into the
room. I violently disliked her. She stood for all the things
Rosie could have been if she hadn't the saving grace of being
Rosie.
"The lift here reminds me of French lifts," she said. “Ameri-
can lifts are widely different, but French lifts...." On sherattled, and I quite forgot her presence. When I remembered
it she was saying: "You have the impression they only go up
but never come down." So she was still on lifts. After Rosie
appeared I took my drink to an armchair and sat down with
a sigh.
I heard the doorbell and I heard nanny speaking to Farrell;
then he came in, wearing a dinner jacket, the studs shining in
the lamplight. He was no pilgrim. Helen told him she hadn't
seen him since she was as small as that. She showed it. She
was so clumsy that I thought she would drop. Farrell looked
as though he too had waited for a telephone call; and I be-
came angry. Because I had been in momentary need of three
pounds I let myself in for an unpleasant, boring evening. Life
was too short for that. As if she had sensed it, Mrs. Adams
over-roasted the chicken, and the cauliflower was a little
crumbly. Yet I couldn't help feeling sorry for Rosie and Far-
rell. They were uneasy and ill at ease. She smiled a lot, and
he barricaded himself behind his perfect manners. Hilda, and
lunching with Hilda, weren't mentioned. Helen wanted to
know whether Farrell believed that the Olympic Games im-
proved happy relations among nations. At last the ladies left
us to our port.
"Wasn't it Mr. Sponge who stayed on drinking port till the
early hours, and whenever his host suggested they should join
the ladies, he said it was too soon or something?" I said. "I
feel like him."
"What was the name of his host?" Farrell asked. "It's ter-
rible how one forgets."
"It sounds like Huckleberry Finn, in my memory, but of
course it couldn't be Huckleberry Finn. I'm all for forget-
ting, if you get my meaning. One learns to forget, one reads
to forget, one forgets to forget." I emitted a laugh. "But we
don't want to talk like bright women. Oh, Mr. Farrell, should
one or shouldn't one forget? Not for us. Have more port."
He said no, thank you. I was annoyed again. "My poor old
drunken mother used to drink port, but before dinner; as a
matter of fact, any time of the day, if she could lay her hands
on a bottle." I said that to shock him. He examined his gold
lighter. "You find that sordid, don't you?""I think you have very much the wrong opinion of me,"
he said.
"I don't think so," I said.
"Perhaps my own opinion of myself is wrong." He stopped
oh, never
playing with the lighter. "Of course one should
mind."
"Why don't you and Rosie hit it off?" I asked. I saw no
reason why I should hedge.
"I didn't know we didn't hit it off," he said. "I'm sorry
to hear that. Is it your impression, or did she say so?" He
lifted his hand. "This is an unfair question," he said quickly,
"so please don't answer it."
There it was: the man was buttoned up again. The nearness
and hint of intimacy of the other night were only in the eyes
of the beholder: that is, in my imagination. "Rosie is too
loyal a daughter to say a word against her father," I said.
"As I don't want her to say anything against me now,"
said Farrell, rising from the table, 'could we join the ladies?"
"What about Mr. Sponge?"
"His daughter wasn't his hostess. If I remember rightly,
Mr. Sponge had no daughter."
So that was his line of country. I waited till he reached the
door. "I forgot to tell you, " I said, "that Rosie and I lunched
with Hilda today. Or to be accurate, Rosie lunched with her
and I had coffee with her."
"Oh, I see,” he said. His hand was on the doorknob.
"She told us you went to see her last Sunday."
"That's correct," he said, opening the door. "Come,
Jasper."
"I'll finish my port," I said. "You know where to powder
your nose against the wall." He said yes, thank you, and left
the room.
I drank three glasses of port in succession. Well, I could
only blame myself for having asked him to dinner. The man
was a bundle of nerves. Such men should be left to themselves
and to those who can't escape them.
Mrs. Adams looked in. "Pardon," she said, "I didn't know
you was still 'ere, sir."
"You too," I said, and left the dining room to her.It was ten o'clock: an hour to kill. I was wrong; at ten
thirty Farrell took leave of us, saying he had a lot of papers
to go through at home. I left it to Rosie to see him off. "Pa-
pers to go through," I said to Helen, who nodded like some
minor sage. "Papers. I have to write an article for a paper.
Deadline seven a.m. I must rush and write it. If I don't see
you again before you go: it was nice of you to have us... I
beg your pardon. Nice of you to have come."
To go to the bedroom I had to cross a passage leading to
the hall. Since the passage had no door at the end of it, I
could see Rosie and Farrell still in conversation near the front
door. I vanished into the bedroom. If they had anything to
say to each other they could have said it during dinner. I
threw myself on the bed. Rosie came into the bedroom.
"You could have taken your shoes off, darling," she said.
"You look distressed," I said. "Did Helen say I was rude
to her?"
"It isn't Helen," she said. "It's him. I'm so very sorry for
him, poor man."
"That's the one thing he isn't," I said.
"Don't joke," she said. "He was so sad."
"Sad? I thought he was rather unpleasant. The whole eve-
ning was unpleasant, but I can only blame myself for it." I
got off the bed and started to undress.
"Don't say he was unpleasant," said Rosie. "I'll say the
same thing you said to me. Mutual. He said you were very
forbidding." That made me laugh, but it was an uneasy
laugh. "Please don't laugh," she went on. "He was so de-
pressed. You know how I feel about him, but tonight I really
felt desperately sorry for him."
"I've often told you that you Farrells complicate your lives
and emotions too much." I kicked off my left shoe.
"And he wants you to lunch with him tomorrow," said
Rosie. I shook my head. "Please go," she begged. "I prom-
ised him I'd persuade you. You must go. You have to.
Please."Chapter Two
