I
I went.
First I dropped into the French pub, which wasn't yet
crowded, and there at the bar stood Fred. I didn't know
why but I always expected him to drop on the floor. Moura
Atkinson was talking to him. There had been a time when
with many interruptions she could listen to me for hours.
Nowadays Rosie's incomprehension seemed preferable. I or-
dered a glass of white wine; on second thought, however, I
changed my mind and had half a pint of bitter. "You'd better
drink that white wine," I said to Fred.
"You weren't annoyed with me last night?" Fred asked.
"Why should I be annoyed with you?" I said, pretending
I didn't hear or didn't deign to remember-what he had
said about my selling my soul but not my flesh to the rich.
"Because I said you've sold your soul but not your flesh to
the rich," said Fred, after having drunk the wine. Now he
was safe. Moura said it was an excellent remark. That meant
that till she decided some other remark outdid it, she would
repeat it to everybody she met, whether he knew me or not.
"What I meant by that," went on Fred, "is that you like
being with them without benefiting by it.""That's because I'm lazy, indolent," I said. "This is quite
a new departure. You sell your soul because you like it, but
don't sell your flesh because you're lazy. You'll go a long way
yet, Fred. Good-by, Moura."
"He wasn't like that in the old days, at all," I heard Moura
say as I went out.
To please Rosie and not a little my curiosity, I telephoned
Farrell at ten. He sounded eager and relieved, and asked me
to meet him in a restaurant off Covent Garden at a quarter to
one. Rosie was rather anxious, reminding me several times
that I was meeting him at a quarter to one; nanny gave me an
irritating time while with care and tenacity she brushed my
jacket. Before I left Rosie blurted it out. "Don't let him say
anything against mummy," she said.
I walked to the restaurant, stopping at only two pubs on my
way. In the second pub I saw a man whose name I couldn't
remember. He had something to do with television and said I
should ring him up. "We should get together," he said. "Some-
thing might come of it." Though I had listened to such
phrases for decades, and "got together" without anything
ever coming of it, I asked him his number. He said he would
write it down but hadn't paper on him, so I put my hand into
my pocket. Yes, there was some paper. I fished it out. It was
a colored picture of St. Theresa of Lisieux. "The Little Flower"
was the caption. I quickly turned it, not wanting the fellow
to catch sight of it. On the other side was a prayer.
"I can't write on this," I said. "It's a postcard somebody
sent me. Never mind. Just tell me; I won't forget. I'm very
good at remembering numbers." I forgot it by the time I
reached the street.
I was truly annoyed with Rosie for putting that picture into
my pocket. My pocket should be safe from her proselytizing.
Then I couldn't help smiling, because it was sweet, childish
and totally ineffectual. Nevertheless, I was glad that the tele-
vision chap didn't see the Little Flower. I read the prayer on
the other side in St. Martin's Lane, and bumped first into a
woman, then into an Indian. The woman growled; the Indian
three times assured me that he was so sorry. The prayer
couldn't have been more childish.The air was chilly. It shouldn't be like that in early Sep-
tember if the winter to come wished to be mild. I looked at
the faces of the passersby. They seemed preoccupied with one
thing or the other, though none, I was willing to bet, thought
of the cold winter that would drive many of them into their
graves. They paid no attention to the chilly air. When I was
a little boy in Suffolk and a great frost came, an old woman
stopped me in Thoro'fare Ipswich and said, unintroduced as
it were: "Many a soul will go with this." I stared at her not
a little frightened. "Dead," she said and, satisfied, she walked
on. Now I amused myself picking out likely frightened faces
to which I might go up and say: "Many a soul will go with
this." I was so busy with it that I arrived ten minutes late
at the restaurant.
The bellboy, or whatever he was, asked me whether I had
booked a table. I told him I was lunching with Mr. Farrell.
The bellboy became subservient and said: "He's in the cock-
tail bar. I'll take you there, sir."
Farrell rose as we came in. "There's Mr. Farrell, sir,"
said the boy, pointing to him.
"Thank you," I said. "I wouldn't have recognized him."
Pleased, the boy disappeared. I said I was late; Farrell said
it didn't matter, not at all, not at all. He asked me what I
wanted to drink. I said a Guinness because I hate the words
cocktail bar. The cocktail bar was crowded with businessmen.
Everybody except me wore dark suits. Not a woman about;
the businessmen were outdoing one another in joviality.
"I'm very glad you could come," said Farrell. He was
drinking sherry. "I looked it up this morning. The name is
Mr. Jogglebury Crowdey." I stared at him.
"Whose name?" I asked.
"Why, Mr. Sponge's host."
Amateurs take everything more seriously than professionals.
"Oh," I said. He looked disappointed. "If Surtees," I said,
to humor the amateur, "had cut out all that chatter about
hunting he would have been a giant of a writer."
He was still disappointed. "I love hunting," he said.
The headwaiter danced in and spoke in French to Farrell.
He had a strong Cockney accent. Farrell answered in Eng-lish; nonetheless the waiter persevered with his French. "He's
a good chap," said Farrell, "comes from Birkenhead."
Having done my duty by the cocktail bar I now asked for
a Pernod. "Funny," I said, still thinking of the headwaiter.
"Where did he get his Cockney accent?''
"Must have learned French in London," said Farrell. "You
speak it perfectly, so Rosemary tells me."
"I bummed about in France long enough," I said, wonder-
ing when the man would get down to brass tacks. He was
dying to, yet continued to talk of trivialities.
"
"We ought to go in," he said. I watched him ordering the
food and the wine, and I warmed to him because he did it
unpretentiously. When he had tipped the wine waiter he
turned to me and said: "My son is very angry with me.
That I found disappointing. I hadn't come to those phoney
Edwardian vaults to hear about Roger, whom I didn't dis-
like, but with whom I didn't hit it off. Moreover, I seldom
gave him a thought. I said something about sons being always
angry with their fathers. "I'm afraid I see his point," said
Farrell. Fundamentally he was just a bundle of nerves,
spending his life containing himself. "He has a girl, or rather
had a girl. He was, in fact still is, gone on her, but she broke
it off. He asked her to marry him. He said he wanted his wife
completely to himself. Evidently that isn't her idea of mar-
riage."
"What has that to do with you? I mean that isn't your
worry."
"It is very much my worry. If he hadn't seen the disaster
of his parents' marriage he would have spoken-how should I
put it? more diplomatically to her."
"And you mean to say he told you it was your fault that he
made an ass of himself?" I said, now disliking the young man
thoroughly. At the next table two businessmen were vowing
to flood Canada from coast to coast with some deodorant.
"He never said so," said Farrell, but I could guess it, and
I see very little of him now, which I regret, because he's my
best companion."
"Your trouble is that you're far too sensitive: that is, too
vulnerable.""I wonder," he said, pouring out wine. "I should like
Roger to have a contented life."
I let that pass. Why shouldn't a well-fed young man with a
cushy job, security and the right connections wish to become
more contented than his circumstances warranted? Besides, I
was convinced that Farrell didn't ask me to lunch to discuss
Roger. It was, I guessed, a preliminary. "I like grouse," I
said. He laughed, saying I spoke like one who confesses under
torture. "I love tripe and onions too," I said in self-defense.
The headwaiter asked me comment monsieur enjoyed son petit
oiseau. I hadn't the heart to answer him in English.
"You said I was too sensitive, too vulnerable," said Far-
rell. It's coming, I said to myself. "To be sensitive is a sort
of cowardice," he went on, and waited for me to contradict
him. I didn't. "I won't be a coward this time. I was last
night. It had nothing to do with Rosemary. I always took
for granted that she owed her loyalty to Hilda."
"She spoke to me very nicely of you after you left last
night," I said.
He looked quite moved. "The dear girl," he said.
A woman came in with a businessman looking more busi-
nesslike than he. The headwaiter made a big fuss over her,
madameing her loudly. The other businessmen took no notice
of her whatever. "I imagine you spend half your time giving
or attending business lunches," I said.
"I wanted to ask you last night to listen to my troubles,"
said Farrell.
"You aren't listening to me," I said. He said he was sorry.
"Come out with it," I said, sitting back and refusing a cigar.
He didn't smoke one either. He said it was difficult, and
started off on being a coward again. "You worry too much
about yourself," I said. "That's unhealthy. I never worry
about myself. What's the good of speculating about oneself?
I've heard chaps being told they drank because they were
inverted homosexuals or they were homosexuals because they
were inverted drinkers. Forget about your cowardice and tell
me what you wanted to tell me that is, what you want to tell
me right now."
He told me. I had expected more. It appeared that he hadbeen having an affair with some woman off and on for three
years. He was fond of her, was happy only when he was with
her; but it was all extremely difficult because of his religious
scruples, which apparently made him feel guilty without stop-
ping him from seeing her. He knew it couldn't go on like that
forever. There I agreed with him. "I've fallen between two
stools," he said. "When I get up I fall between them again."
"What do you intend to do about it?" I asked. "Perhaps
you like falling between two stools." He shook his head, then
asked me to have a brandy. I said I should, under the cir-
cumstances, prefer a mirabelle.
"What do you mean 'under the circumstances'?'" he asked.
"It's simple, my dear fellow. I seldom lunch with the rich,
and mirabelle is so much more expensive than brandy." He
smiled and looked uncomfortable. I saw no reason why he
should. "What do you intend to do about it?" I asked. "Or
do you just want to talk about it? I'm sorry if I don't sound
sympathetic, but I've learned from long and wasteful experi-
ence that people tell you of their problems because they want
to speak of them and not because they need advice."
"I should like you to meet her," he said.
"When you like," I said in an offhand though polite man-
ner. I didn't want him to see that I was dying to see what his
mistress could be like. It wasn't curiosity alone: I knew his
wife-that is, my mother-in-law.
"Could you have tea with us this afternoon?" he asked. I
said I should like to very much. "Then come to the Ritz at
five," he said. I said I would be there. "But I don't want to
ask you to come there without your knowing what I have in
mind," he said. So there was a catch in it.
He explained what he had in mind. Some six weeks ago he
had decided to ask old Hilda to give him a divorce. He spent
some time in explaining to me the religious difficulties the
whole thing involved. Nevertheless he was determined to go
through with it, and if she said no, then he would divorce her
for desertion; for every day he was more and more convinced
that a civil marriage and living a normal life with his girl,
even though it would be a sin in the eyes of his Church, waspreferable to moaning between two stools. I wholeheartedly
agreed with him. Then he confessed that he had funked it
when he went to see Hilda. The nuns behind grill and veil.
The man was too emotional. He admitted that he was back
where he was before he took the resolution. The restaurant
began to empty; in fine fettle and goodwill to each other, the
businessmen signed their bills, cracked jokes with the head-
waiter, then made for the door.
"When I see Hilda," said Farrell, "I have a sense of guilt.
It takes two to break up a marriage. I know the subject would
be distasteful and painful to her, so as a sort of making amends
I haven't the heart to broach the subject, although I know
that in the long run she wouldn't mind one way or the other.
Her conscience is and will remain at rest. I'm being as honest
with you as I am capable of being." I bowed. "But if some-
body else told her about it, it would be a different thing," he
said, with his eyes on the tablecloth.
"You mean you want me to put it to her?" I asked. He
said nothing. The headwaiter chose that auspicious moment to
waddle up and ask after Roger. I waited till he left. It must
be admitted that Farrell had admirable manners. "Now I
see what you have in mind," I said. "Tomorrow Rosie is go-
ing with the baby to spend her dutiful fortnight with Hilda,
and the same way as last year I'm going with her to spend
the weekend with them. You want me to tell Hilda in the
course of the weekend that if she doesn't divorce you, then
you will divorce her; your mind is made up, the die is cast,
etcetera, etcetera. I found out by accident; or else you, puff-
ing with grim tenacity and purpose, told me so, and in order
to safeguard my mother-in-law's reputation, I urge her to do
the divorcing herself." I sat back triumphantly.
"How did you guess that?" he asked.
"It's part of my profession," I said. I thought the thing
over. "I am willing to do it if you're really determined, and
I'm willing to do it too because frankly it's the best for all
concerned. Don't forget I've lived in Catholic France and
met plenty of remarried divorced people. But there is one
point we have to consider: namely, I want to do nothing thatcould hurt Rosie in any possible manner. I am grateful to the
girl for having chosen me from among all the drunks of the
British Isles.. Could I have another mirabelle?" I didn't
truly want one but I loathe my voice when it gets moved. It
sounds as if I had swallowed candy floss, God forbid.
"For Rosemary the situation will remain unchanged," he
said. "It matters little to her if I behave badly again. I'm
certain she blames me for everything." I interjected that
Rosie wasn't the blaming sort. By then my mind was made
up: it couldn't affect Rosie in the long run. I began to visual-
ize Hilda's grim face as I broke the unpalatable news to her.
I nearly smiled with anticipation..
"I know it will be hell for a while," said Farrell, "but I
am going through with it."
"I wonder," I said. "Between you and me, old boy, why
don't you do what so many of you Catholics have done
through the ages? I mean solve your Gordian knot by enter-
ing a monastery and turning your back on a world which has
become too complicated for you?"
He looked at me. After a while I had to look away because
it was like watching a bird that no birdwatcher had watched
before. It was more than I was entitled to. Then he said:
"No, dear Jasper, I'm not a refugee. Refugees arrive without
luggage. You can't go without luggage to face the love of
God. I have nothing to take with me if I run from my prob-
lems."
I wished I hadn't joked.
The headwaiter came to my rescue. "Stay as long as you
like, Mr. Farrell, sir," he said in English. Probably he didn't
know how to say it in French, for which he could hardly be
blamed, since in France waiters don't say that, as the clients
take it for granted.
"We're the last here," I said, "and I really have to go."
I left him with the bill and my thanks, and promised to turn
up in the Ritz. Once outside in the nippy air I felt no longer
as an uninitiated birdwatcher to whom a glorious bird had
been revealed. What I felt was that the man was bubbling
over with too much undigested emotion and the quicker hesettled down in a woman's strong arms the better for all con-
cerned. I remembered that I had promised Rosie either to go
back or to telephone her.
I was slightly though not unusually broke on that day. If I
went back to Regent's Park it would mean taking a taxi there
and a taxi to the Ritz. There wasn't enough time for using
public transport. I decided on telephoning her. I assured her
it had all gone off well, that I was on my best behavior and
would tell her all about it in the evening. I walked back to
Soho, my mind full of a spirited dialogue between Hilda and
me. I went down some ladderlike steps into a drinking club
where you could drink for one and four some foul red wine
which came undoubtedly from the sun-kissed vineyards of Soho.
Moura was there and so was Fred, now pale with drink, and
bellicose. I couldn't find my matches. I searched through my
pockets, and lo! the Little Flower fell out. "What's that?"
they both asked as I swiftly picked it up.
"A friend sent it from Lourdes," I said, putting it back
into my pocket.
"I'll go there some day," said Moura, "when everything
else has failed me."
"You haven't the luggage for such a journey," I said. She
was thinking up an answer, but Geoffrey Hale appeared. He
had been her boy friend ever since she had married a Mr.
Atkinson whom I didn't happen to know. Geoffrey became
her lover because she despised Mr. Atkinson for having mar-
ried her. It was as simple as that.
"You saw the stinking review I got yesterday?" Geoffrey
asked us and the Cypriot barman who couldn't speak English
and the two Jamaicans who slept with their heads on a table.
My alarm watch went off. I had wound it up to annoy Fred.
He looked at me as though ready to stab me in the back. That
didn't perturb me, since he had done it and would do it when-
ever he had a chance. "I've got it here," said Geoffrey, tak-
ing it from his pocket. I started for the door.
"Going?" asked Fred. I said I was. "Where are you going
this time?"
"I'm going to have tea in the Ritz," I said loudly, "thenI'm going to have chocolate and macaroons at Rumpelmeyer's,
then if Fortnum's are still open I'll have a sweet sherry there.
Tomorrow morning, elevenses at Woollands."
That put me in such a good mood that I took a taxi after all.
Farrell's woman took me aback a little. I had expected
someone more glamorous, especially more worldly. She was
called Jean. She wasn't on the thin side, but you would ex-
pect a man like him to want to bury his head in an ample
bosom when fear floats around the room in the small hours.
She looked very Irish insofar as her eyes were green-beauti-
ful eyes they were; her hair black, and her complexion slightly
ruddy. She was what nanny would call well-spoken, but what
struck me most was that partly she humored him as you hu-
mor a child, and partly she feared him. When she spoke, her
eyes tried anxiously to catch his in order to see whether he
approved or not; when he spoke, she smiled to herself and
seemed hardly to listen. She was very well dressed in a sim-
ple manner. Farrell wasn't altogether at his ease. He looked
up whenever anybody came in or went out. Suddenly she
stood up and said she had to go back. He said she should wait,
as a cab was calling for her. She sat down again. A porter
came in to say the cab had arrived for madam. So I said
good-by to her. I shook her hand. I found her eyes and her
smile simple and enchanting. "Wait for me; I won't be long,"
said Farrell to me.
"I'm afraid I'm already late for a very important appoint-
ment," I said, and walked off. I had had my fill of him for
the day. The evening was chilly too. I walked as far as Hyde
Park Corner where I took a bus to Regent's Park. Rosie came
flying into the hall with an anxious expression. That decided
me to say nothing unpleasant about the Little Flower.
II
Friday found us at Paddington Station. Nanny was taking
her yearly fortnight off which, however, didn't stop her from
seeing us off. She gave Rosie many instructions about thechild, and stood peering in through the window till the train
pulled out. It gave me some satisfaction that her charge took
no notice of her. "Be a good little girl, Mary," admonished
nanny. Mary started licking the windowpane.
"Stop it," I said, and Mary stopped it; for reasons best
known to herself, the child generally obeyed me. "Now you'll
make a nice, good nun," I said. Rosie didn't know whether
I was trying to be funny or not.
One evening when Mary was about a year old, Rosie, who
had been to visit her late convent school in Essex and was
therefore in a sweet, soulful mood, had said to me: "Wouldn't
it be wonderful if Mary became a nun?" I laughed. "It would
be a great favor," she said.
"Divine, I take it," I said. The point about Rosie was that
because she was neither unctuous nor aggressive in such ut-
terances, I felt usually like a cad for pulling her leg, which
didn't stop me from pulling it. "We look far too respectable,
the three of us in this compartment," I now said. "I'm going
to the buffet car if there is one."
"You'll have nearly a fortnight not to be respectable when
you go back to the flat," said Rosie.
"But think of the next two days," I said, leaving the com-
partment. Mary waved to me. She should have done that to
her devoted nanny. The buffet car was full of soldiers. Now-
adays you see soldiers only in buffet cars. I could have gone
to the first-class dining car next door; but having to buy a
first-class ticket sufficed. When we arrived and the train
pulled out, while Hilda and Rosie were still gazing at each
other with moist eyes, I looked in through the windows of the
dining car. A lot of Hildas were drinking tea in there and
eating tea-cake. I contemplated Hilda on the platform, as a
producer who has to think of the right cast. Not so much a
difficult as a strange nut to crack, strange in that the woman
struck me anew as one who lacked all natural reactions. The
spirited dialogues in my mind didn't fit in with her at all.
She said it was nice of me to have come; I said it was nice
of her to have asked me; then I nearly fell over one of her
dogs. She and Rosie chattered lovingly while we drove toOrchard House, where every time we visited it I had the im-
pression that Hilda thought it shocking that Rosie and I slept
in the same room, same bed. "Your dressing room is next
door," said Hilda. If only, her eyes said, I slept there too.
"We'll have tea in the garden," she said. I excused myself,
saying I had drunk far too much tea on the train. Neither
mother nor daughter minded. But my daughter did because
she waved and waved to me.
"You should have done that to nanny," I said.
As a matter of fact, that soft September afternoon I felt
rather drawn to nanny; for while she packed my suitcase and
Rosie was at the other end of the flat, she whispered to me in
a penetrating stage whisper: "I couldn't stand it even for a
day with Mrs. Farrell."
"But you're not married to my wife," I said properly.
I went upstairs to see Mrs. Walsh. She was glad to see me
and immediately offered me a glass of sherry. "Is there noth-
ing better about?" I asked. Mrs. Walsh said she wouldn't
dare to touch the brandy bottle in the dining room. Mrs.
Walsh still maintained that Hilda nightly caroused in secret
with a bottle of brandy.
"I'll get you some wine," she said. Left alone, I looked at
the photograph of her dead husband. I looked at it every time
I came to Orchard House and into her room, because the man
in the photograph had a poisonous face, a vile expression, and
the mouth of an unsuccessful lecher. Yet it all spelled beauty
to his widow, because it was hallowed by death. She must
have hated him while he lived. Alas, that wasn't certain.
Perhaps she loved everything that face, that expression and
that mouth stood for.
"Your husband must have been a very amiable person," I
said, when she came back with a bottle of lukewarm Moselle.
"You say that every time you come to my room, Mr. Danes-
bridge," she said, gratified. Ah, I thought, but you don't tell
me whether you found him an amiable person. "He died six-
teen years ago," she said. Next year she would tell me he
died seventeen years ago, provided she knew how to add.
"There they are," she said, pointing at the window.
"I'll see them later," I said. "Now, Mrs. Walsh," I saidbriskly, "I need your help." She bristled with curiosity. "I
mean I need your advice. I've come down to discuss a very
delicate matter in great secrecy with Mrs. Farrell. You know
her well. Which is the best time to have a heart-to-heart talk
with her all alone of course-and catching her unaware and
in a benign mood, if the latter is possible?"
"A delicate matter?" she breathed. I nodded. "Have you
decided," she practically whispered, "to convert, Mr. Danes-
bridge? Oh, what joy!"
The room rang with my laughter. She looked offended. I
quickly apologized, saying I laughed because her question took
me by surprise. "I do wish you did," she said. "My husband
wasn't a Catholic and it caused a lot of trouble between us.
But I pray for him daily." She contemplated her shoes for a
while, then asked with avidity: "Then what is the secret?"
"I can't tell you. If Mrs. Farrell falls in with my plans
then you will hear of it. If she doesn't, then you'll hear of it
too. Which is the best time to speak to her? I hardly know
her, and I don't want to speak to her when my wife is present.
I don't suppose for a moment that I could ask her to come and
have a drink with me in the local." Mrs. Walsh laughed
heartily, then suggested I should catch her late at night when
in her cups. "Out of the question," I said. "If she drinks,
which I don't believe, then she won't thank me for catching
her.
"You're very clever, Mr. Danesbridge. One can see you're
an author." I said she should come and have a drink at the
local; she said it was out of the question. I thought it was
time to go into the garden. I stood up and asked her which
was the best time. Poor thing-she suggested after breakfast,
then after lunch, then before tea; in short, she hadn't the
faintest idea. That comes of living too close to somebody for
too many years. I would, I decided, get it over tomorrow.
The evening was duller than I expected. I had a chance be-
fore dinner while Rosie put Mary to bed, but there were too
many dogs in the room. We had some desultory conversation.
"You don't care for the country, Rosemary tells me," said
Hilda. Suddenly I realized what she had reminded me of
from the start a giraffe, as long as you were almost on thesame level with it. I saw myself hanging from a crane in
well-trimmed bush.
"I have very little in common with nature," I said. Her
answer was that she couldn't live in a town any more. Rosie
came in, and soon we dined, eating good solid English food,
including fruit salad. After dinner I said I would go for a
walk in the starlit night. But the stars are nature too," said
Hilda.
"Ah," I said, "but I belong to the night." One does talk
inane rubbish when one has nothing to say. I asked for a
key. Mother and daughter laughed, explaining that the front
door was never locked. I didn't see why it shouldn't be. I
went out into the night, which was quiet; and the stars hid
behind clouds before I reached the pub.
It was heavy with pipe smoke and dart-playing yokels. The
publican's wife suggested in a strong foreign accent that I
should go into the parlor. I agreed because the darts flew
about dangerously, or so it seemed to me. She beckoned to me,
and I followed her through a door. She switched on the light.
I sat down at the first of the two tables and she brought me a
drink, then lingered, trying to find out who I was. She soon
did, without my helping her. A beaming sodden face appeared
in the door she had left open. "Best respects, sir," it said.
That was George, the gardener, who looked like an apple be-
cause of the amount of cider he drank. "Ach," said the pub-
lican's wife, so you're Mrs. Farrell's son-in-law. Here for
some long time?" I told her to give the gardener a pint of
cider, and off she went. She drew it with praiseworthy speed;
then with a gleam in her eyes came rushing back into the
parlor; but I was already closing the front door behind me.
I walked on for a good half hour. At night most things look
alike, even truck headlights, of which I met plenty. Once a
big wolflike black dog came out of a yard to lick my hand. I
had killed more than an hour by the time I got behind the
walls surrounding Orchard House. The dogs didn't bark.
Probably they were dreaming of blue ribbons at Cruft's. I
entered the house. The hall was a large, wide passage running
from one end of the house to the other. Every time I enteredOrchard House I was under the impression that no woman
lived there, that it was the home of a precise elderly bachelor.
Even the flowers, of which there were plenty in numberless
vases, aided the impression. I started for the staircase. The
drawing-room door opened, and there was Hilda with a glass
of brandy in her hand; and for a moment I thought that my
precise elderly bachelor had appeared, to ask me to take a
glass of port wine with him.
"Come in if you want to," she said, "and have a drink of
brandy. Rosemary's gone to bed. She always feels terribly
sleepy on the first evening."
Whether or not that was drinking in secret and sousing
herself, Hilda was her usual forbidding self except for her
eyes, which had become piercingly blue. I sat down facing
her beside the empty fireplace, with only one of the dogs with
us. Was this or wasn't this the right moment? She was look-
ing at her glass, holding it before her eyes, reminding me of a
lonely laird in kilts in a whisky advertisement. "I lunched,"
I said, "with Francis yesterday." She gazed even more in-
tently at her glass. Though she was thin, she had developed two
double chins holding her head like that. "We had a very
long and interesting talk."
"He can be very interesting," she said, and emptied her
glass. There was no gulping or anything similar about it.
It could have been water. She filled her glass. "But at times
he can be very uninteresting too." The glass came up. "Like
most of us."
"To my utter astonishment or should I say confusion-I
heard from him that he intends to get married again. He
seemed grimly determined."
That was taking the bull by the horns: it was starting with
the climax. I gulped down my brandy. Without bothering to
look at the harbinger of portentous tidings, Hilda said: "I'm
afraid he can't do that. He's still married to me. We're
separated but not divorced. You must have misunderstood
him."
"I didn't misunderstand him." For some unknown reason
the sleeping dog woke up, and with his stump of a tail bristlingwalked to the heavily curtained French window where he
stopped and growled. "He wants a divorce."
"That's impossible," she said. "We Catholics can't di-
vorce." Her voice became impatient. "I wish you people
would understand us better. If there are grounds for it, a
marriage can be annulled, but divorce-never."
"According to the laws of the land, he can divorce," I said.
"And he wouldn't be the first who availed himself of them."
She held out her glass: I refilled it. "He wants to. I can
assure you he's determined to. Probably Rosie mentioned too
that I lunched with him." Hilda winced every time I said
Rosie instead of Rosemary. "He told me about it at lunch.
I haven't mentioned it to Rosie, of course."
"That was the proper thing to do," she said. The dog re-
turned, growling absentmindedly, now and then looking back
at the curtain. With his eyes half closed he leaped into an
armchair.
"He can," I said, "divorce you for desertion. But why
should he? You could divorce him; then he'll be the guilty
party, and your conscience won't be ruffled, since you don't
intend to remarry."
"I should divorce him?" said Hilda, her eyes piercing my
beard. "I?'" I felt certain I would find holes in my beard in
the morning. "I to help him to sin, to make himself unhappy,
miserable?" Her attention concentrated on her glass again.
Good thing. "No, I couldn't do that to Francis. He has
done me a lot of harm; he made me miserable, but I won't
help him to make himself unhappy. It would be the most
uncharitable thing I could do."
Frankly, I was becoming annoyed with her. "He feels he's
miserable and unhappy as he is," I said. Hilda lifted her
glass into the lamplight, then examined it as though it were
some sort of revelation. She said he would be far unhappier
if he were outside the Church. "I have known in my time
quite a number of lapsed Catholics," I said. She said none
of them were happy. "We know nothing about other people,"
I said.
She slowly emptied her glass. A brief coughing fit followed.
"I'm sorry," she said. "I smoke too much in the evenings."I saw that she didn't consider my remark worthy of answer
or commentary. "He is very much in love with a woman,"
I said, nastier than I intended, "and he wants to marry her.
He says he could be happy only if he married her."
"Not at the price," said Hilda.
"In that case he'll have to divorce you. I'd have thought it
would be better the other way around. That's the only reason
why I brought up the subject. His life is his, yours is yours,
and I'm only an intruder." I must say I almost warmed to
the old girl, for she nodded vigorously when I used the word
intruder. She was thinking of her daughter and not of our
subject. She held out her glass: I filled it. The dog chased
rabbits in his sleep, whimpering, and his little white body
shook.
"He won't go through with it," she said.
"But a man in love ..."
"A man in love gets over it in time. One more reason why
I must do nothing to help him along the wrong path." An
extraordinary creature. Not for a second did she want to
know who the other woman was. "Francis is a sentimen-
talist," she said. "He exaggerates. He's exaggerated ever
since I've known him. I used to say to him his trouble was
his spate of sentiments. Now he's exaggerating again. He'll
get over it."
"As one gets over mumps," I said with great irony.
"Exactly," she said. Her eyes said she liked me for the
first time.
"Supposing mumps are the only alternative to something
far worse, I said. That went completely over her head.
"Whatever you say or I say, his is a pretty lonely life."
"He could put a stop to it whenever he wanted to," she
said. I waited for her to go on. The grandfather clock chimed
in the hall, the grandfather clock in the dining room joined
in, then the Empire clock in the drawing room added its
chimes. It was a sort of mechanical dawn without cocks and
no day breaking.
"How?" I asked.
Hilda turned toward me. Her eyes were now quite ex-
ceptional. They hardly seemed to belong to the drawn, thinface. "Can I trust you?" she asked, adding: "I hardly know
you." I said it depended, then laughed. Her eyes said it was
no laughing matter. "Can I trust you not to repeat what I'm
going to say now?" I assured her I had the gift of silence.
"I'm not thinking only of other people," she said. "I don't
want you to repeat it to me either. There is nothing more
annoying than having your own words brought back to you."
"Admirable. I won't show them to you again."
"I must let him out," she said. She stood up, pulled aside
the curtain, and the dog hurried into the garden. "Francis
made my life a misery," she said, leaning against the table
under the chandelier. The chandelier wasn't lit. "I did my
duty by him as long as my conscience dictated it. It is said
a boy needs a father more than a mother once he reaches the
age of seven. When Roger was seven we separated. I had to
fight hard for it. He's very insensitive when he gets it into
his head that he is thwarted. But throughout the long years I
haven't given up hope that one day the animal will die in
him-it has to, hasn't it? - and then we shall be able to live
again as husband and wife should after they have done their
duty by bringing children into the world." The dog trotted
back.
"You mean husband and wife to live like brother and
sister?"
Her face lit up, and honestly she looked almost sweet. "How
well you put it," she said. "Brother and sister. Perhaps that
day will come. I always have it in my prayers." I was be-
coming angry with her because she had moved me. Instead of
horror, compassion alone mingled with the brandy I had swal-
lowed. "Well," she said briskly, "I did enjoy our chat. I
never thought I would. Now to bed."
She and the dog came up the stairs with me. I stopped to
wish her good-night outside Rosie's and my bedroom. "Your
dressing room is over there," she said severely. "Good-night."
I undressed as fast as I could. As I opened the dressing room
door I sighed with relief; for I had half expected her to lock
me in-that is, lock me away. As I came into the passage I
nearly jumped. A ghost! but no-in a snow-white dressing-gown, resembling an obese clown who has fallen into a lake
of flour, Mrs. Walsh stood with her broad backside pressed
against the wall, a finger pressed against her lip. I steadied
myself. Down came the finger.
"I heard you both come up," she whispered. "Heard your
voices too-oh, for hours and hours. Has your mission suc-
ceeded, Mr. Danesbridge? Forgive me, but I was so worried
for you."
"Don't worry, it all came to nothing; good-night-I'm in
a hurry." I sighed once more with relief as I came into the
bedroom. Rosie was sleeping, with her rosary on the bedside
table. It was pleasant to know that she was no sister of mine.
In the morning I dreaded meeting Hilda. I had once known
a man who had confessed to me, unasked and unencouraged,
in a drinking club, that he was impotent and his one sorrow
was that he couldn't give his wife a child. That was before
artificial insemination. I listened to him patiently, said the
usual banal words of consolation, then forgot it. Ever since
that day that man cut me, and if he looked in my direction,
he looked daggers. I told Rosie I would skip breakfast, and
stayed in bed till half past ten. When I was dressed I got out
of the house and went to the pub, where the good woman
hovered around me while I did my best to ignore her curiosity.
With my third drink she put the question: were Mr. and Mrs.
Farrell divorced or not? I told her I wouldn't know, since
I was only their son-in-law. That put me into a good mood.
In my good mood I decided to brave Hilda, so I walked back
to the house. Mrs. Walsh said she was in the flower room
arranging flowers. That room was next to the garage.
She was arranging flowers as if she were a sergeant major.
She gave me her little cold smile, said I would find drinks in
the drawing room, and when I was on my way she called me
back to ask me to take a filled vase to the drawing room and
put it on the console table to the right of the door. She joined
me there about ten minutes later. She was equable and not at
all self-conscious. Rosie was somewhere in the garden with
Mary. Perhaps we wouldn't be alone during my stay again.
I said to her: "Did you give that divorce business any morethought?" I think I said that because I began to doubt
whether our chat had taken place.
She laughed. "The more I think of it, the funnier it looks,"
she said. "Poor Francis. I think he was just trying to im-
press you." I said I met his mistress. "I imagine she isn't the
first, and if he doesn't look out she won't be the last either."
Her eyes followed a dog into the garden. "He's so rich," she
said, "and that's such a temptation for unscrupulous
women." Rosie appeared, and I swore not to broach the
subject again.
In the afternoon she took us in her station wagon to call on
some friends of hers. Our host, it turned out, had written a
book on fishing three decades ago. "At least you'll have some-
body to talk to," Hilda said. In the evening she vanished
straight after dinner, saying she had a headache. On Sunday
morning while I had breakfast Mrs. Walsh told me that she
had reached the conclusion that Hilda liked me very much.
They went to Mass with Rosie and little Mary, and I wrote a
review I had promised an editor some time ago.
"Prayers while you work," I said to a small assembly of
bored dogs that had come in to watch me.
"They have a weekly bulletin at the chapel," said Rosie on
her return, putting it in front of me. Dutifully I glanced at
it. There would be a rummage sale next week.
"That'll keep you busy, Rosie," I said.
She turned the paper on the other side. "A rather mysteri-
ous action takes place after the Pater Noster," she read.
"I'll read it later, darling," I said.
"You promise?" she said.
"I don't deserve you," I said. "What about finding me a
drink? You are the daughter of the house."
I was to take a train from Salisbury. Hilda drove me there.
Rosie and the child came too. Hilda remained in the station
wagon with the child. Rosie accompanied me to the platform,
and as the train advanced on us, I said: "Why don't you
leave Mary with your mother and Mrs. Walsh and George and
George's wife and come back to London and have a really
good time with me?""You know I can't let mummy down," she said.
It was only in the buffet car that it struck me that I had
failed Farrell completely. But what else could you expect if
you tackled Hilda? It was characteristic of Farrell not to
have fixed a time for me to get in touch with him on my re-
turn. I telephoned his house from the station: there was no
reply. I didn't feel like going back to the flat. I went at
seven to a club in Soho. They open on Sundays at seven.
Fred was there. "Geoffrey has asked me to come here and
meet Moura," he said. "He can't get here before eight."
If one wanted to write a life of Fred, one wouldn't have any
difficulty in choosing the title. The Third Cat. "You've
been away for the weekend?" he asked. I nodded. "Where?"
"Scotland," I said.

Moura came in, and Fred faithfully delivered Geoffrey's
message. "Didn't you tell me that your wife is going away
she asked. I said she was away already. "Why don't you
ask us to your place?" she said. "You must be lousy with
drink there." I shook my head. "Why don't you?"
"Because it's not my flat," I said. Soon after Rosie mar-
ried me, I had invited Fred and Moura to dinner. Let her
meet my pals. She went out of her way to put on an ex-
cellent dinner. The pals forgot or were too drunk to remem-
ber; anyway, they didn't turn up. Since then I kept the
twain apart.
"Haven't I told you that he's sold his soul to the rich?"
said Fred.
"But not my flesh," I said.
Shortly afterward a sort of journalist I knew turned up
with a well-groomed short girl. He wrote in magazines under
the name of Max Whim. "Oh, you're here," he said to me.
"Now this is thrilling. Virginia," he said to the girl, "this is
Jasper. He's Roger's brother-in-law. Jasper, meet Virginia."
He had genuine Wardour Street manners. "Virginia is
Roger's girl friend," said Max.
"Ex-girl friend," she said. "We fell out."
So that was the girl from whom Roger wanted the lot. She
was a cool little customer, though not unlikable. Max beamedon us. He was the man in the know; the Farrell millions cast
their shadow on him too. "I'm sure you have a lot to say to
each other," he said.
"I hardly know Roger," I said.
"He's a nice boy really," said Virginia, "but he's too much
under his father's influence. The father's a prig."
"Not on your life," I said. "Will you excuse me? I must
telephone."
I went into the boiling hot telephone box at the end of the
room. I dialed Farrell's number. It was Roger who answered.
"Oh, it's you, Jasper," he said. "My father's at the club.
Could you ring him there? He's expecting you to. How's my
mother?" I assured him she was very well indeed. Before
ringing off he reminded me to telephone his father without
fail. I left the box as it was too hot in there.
Virginia met me halfway. "I'm so glad to have met you,"
she said. "I'd like to talk to you so very much. Now why did
you say Roger's father wasn't a prig? Or would you call him
insincere, rather?"
"You must forgive me, " I said. "I've come out of that oven
to get some more change."
"I see," said Farrell, after I, as it were, reported to him. I
didn't say a word about the brother and sister business. "I
expected that."
"I did tell her that you intend to go through with it."
"What was her reaction?" he asked anxiously.
"That's of no importance to you, is it, if you want to go
through with it?" After a brief silence he said I was right.
"So there we are," I said.
"I am going through with it," he said. His voice was now
firm.
"That's the only thing to do," I said. He thanked me for
all I had done for him.
Virginia and Max were gone; Geoffrey had arrived; Fred
was looking and smiling at him and Moura in turn while they
chatted.Chapter Three

I
I was still in bed on the next Thursday when Mrs. Adams,
the daily woman, knocked on the bedroom door. I asked her
what she wanted. She said a gentleman wanted to see me.
Couldn't he come later? She bustled off, returned and said
the gentleman was Mrs. Danesbridge's father. My wrist alarm
said it was twenty past eleven rather the wrong time to say
it was still too early. I shouted to her to give him a drink
and I wouldn't be long in joining him. She said there was
nothing to drink in the house, which on reflection I found to
be correct. When Rosie was away I didn't drink in the flat.
I shouted to her to ask him to sit down and wait. On no con-
dition would Mrs. Adams have come into a room where a man
lay in bed.
When I went into the drawing room Farrell, as I expected,
tried to keep his eyes off my aged dressing-gown: it was an
effort for him. "Would you like some beer?" I asked. "Mrs.
Adams could go out and get some." He said I shouldn't
bother. I did bother because I could do with some beer. In
the Chippendale mirror I saw my beard, which looked a trifle
unkempt so early in the day; my not too many tufts of hair
stood almost at attention. The poor fellow was, I bet, think-ing: and this is the man whom my daughter chose. "It's
very nice of you to have come," I said amiably. "I don't
look my best in the morning." I glanced at my feet. "I
don't like slippers," I said.
"Oh, yes," he said. "How's Rosemary?" I told him I
telephoned her last night and she was well. "Going down
for the weekend?" he asked. I gave him what one calls a wry
smile. "Then you can do it," he said. "Oh, I'm glad." I
asked what he had in mind this time. I wouldn't have been
astonished if he had asked me to see his mistress' mother or
her husband if she had one. "I should like you to come to
Paris as my guest for the weekend," he said. "Please do."
Mrs. Adams brought in the beer. Now that I was no longer
in the bedroom, it wouldn't have worried her even if I had
been naked. "I should love that," I said, pouring out and
spilling beer, my hands being not too steady at such an early
hour. "Really I would. That's very kind of you, very kind,
but I don't see why you should ask me, take me, and spend
money on my seeing Paris again. You should take Roger or
some real friend."
"If you say yes, then you're the one who will be doing a
favor. There's always a catch to it. There's always one when
I'm around. You lunched with me and right away I sent you
on an unpleasant mission."
"What's the catch?" I asked. "It has to be a pretty ter-
rible catch for me to refuse an invitation to Paris." I was
thinking: there were thirty guineas due to me from a na-
tional daily. I would get it out of the editor, however un-
popular I made myself. (The money was due only on date of
publication of the article.) With thirty guineas to blow I
wouldn't have to sponge on him too rigorously. "What is it?"
"I'll tell you, Jasper," he said, sipping his beer as if it were
sherry. I told you on the telephone I'm going through with
it whether Hilda approves or not. Jean is coming to join me
in Paris on Sunday afternoon. The dear girl has never been
abroad. She's so looking forward to it. But I'll go in advance
tomorrow. You see I want to meet her there."
،،
"Where's the catch?""This has been a big decision for me to make. From Paris
we'll go for a fortnight to Auvergne. Know it well. I'll be
on tenterhooks till she arrives on Sunday, so I came to ask
you to share those tenterhooks."
"That's all?" He said yes, that was all; also he hoped that
by taking me he would slightly repay me for the trouble I
took last weekend. "I'd almost do it again for a weekend in
Paris," I said, "but why doesn't Jean go with you tomor-
row?" To my astonishment he said her fortnight's holiday
began only on Sunday. She ran some country club near
Maidenhead. "You mean to say," I couldn't help asking,
"that the woman you want to marry has to work?''
"Oh, yes," he said. "The alternative would be her be-
coming a kept woman. I don't believe in that and I'm certain
she doesn't either." I poured more beer into my glass; my
hands were steadier. His glass was still practically full. "I
wouldn't want to marry a woman if she were a kept woman."
He said that, I suppose, because I said nothing. “I'm de-
lighted that you can come," he said, rising. "Delighted.
We're flying on the Epicure, or whatever they call it. I'll
send a car for you."
I said that wasn't necessary: I would go to his house at the
appointed time. I saw him to the door. Mrs. Adams gave me
a letter that had just arrived: it was from Rosie. Though
we telephoned each other daily, we wrote letters too. In my
letters I was perhaps nearer to her than at any other time.
Perhaps that is because one is a writer. "Mummy," she wrote
among other things, "is really fond of you. That makes me
so happy. She says she finds you understand her and see her
point. Didn't I always say she's the sweetest and kindest
person in the world?"
I rang up Orchard House. "I'm going to Paris for the
weekend," I told her. "Your father invited me. I'll be back
on Monday."
"If you don't look out," she said, laughing, he'll make
you chairman of one of the subsidiary companies."
"You mean the one that makes plastic manure or some-
thing?"She laughed louder, saying it was fertilizer; and I won-
dered after I rang off how she would take her father's mar-
riage if and when it came off. Next day as I took a bus to
Knightsbridge with a not too elegant old Gladstone bag but
with six cool fivers in my pocket, I wondered why Farrell
had marched into my life. One always wants to find a mean-
ing to everything, probably because one searches feverishly
for a pattern in spite of knowing almost for sure that life
contains no such thing. I had seen the man off and on ever
since my marriage; we were neither strangers nor acquaint-
ances; and now suddenly he was either sending me to per-
suade his wife to divorce him or taking me to Paris to be
present when his future wife arrived. I got annoyed with
myself because I sat in the bus thinking it must have some
significance. "Pardon me," said an imposingly corpulent
woman who sat beside me and whose shopping bag, contain-
ing many pounds of potatoes to judge by the weight of it, sat
on my knee. I let her out. I had no wish to sit down again,
so I left the bus too and walked the rest of the way. It was
Roger who opened the door for me.
"Thank God you're here," he said. "My father is on the
telephone trying to get you." I stared at him. "Look at the
time," he said. I looked at my watch. Admittedly I should
have arrived a little earlier. He shouted for his father, then
for somebody called Stevens. Farrell came, followed by a man
carrying two suitcases. "I'll drive you to London Airport
now," said Roger. "I hope we'll get there in time."
"Who is Stevens?" I asked as we rolled along to the air-
port. I sat beside Roger, Farrell in the back. Roger said he
came every morning and looked after their clothes and pol-
ished their shoes. I turned my head around. Farrell was wear-
ing a gray tweed suit; his shoes shone. Mine didn't. I wore
the jacket of a suit which had lost, so to speak, its trousers.
The trousers I wore belonged to another suit which, Provi-
dence be praised, hadn't lost its jacket yet. In the beginning
Rosie begged me to go to her father's tailor. I said out of the
question. Since then she didn't allude to the subject again,
for she respected my integrity.
Of course we weren't late. Farrell and Roger shook hands asif he were flying away for good. Roger looked me deep in the
eye and said in a low voice: "Ring me at the office when you
come back." I left that open.
I never cared for flying. To be bored and frightened simul-
taneously seemed too much of a good thing. Farrell didn't
appear to mind. We flew in a clear sky, and as that conglom-
eration of back gardens known as Kent pulled out and the
wide landscape of France pulled in, I overheard Farrell say-
ing (fear and boredom had kept me from listening properly)
we would go to some hotel in the rue de Rivoli where he had
booked a suite which included a room for me. "Not for me,'
I said. "I'm going up to Montmartre to stay in a little filthy
hotel where I lived for five years in my Paris days." He was
disappointed but didn't remonstrate. I promised to turn up
at his hotel well before dinner.
”
I thought we would take the airline's bus from Orly to the
Invalides. Not a bit. The hotel sent a sleek motor car for us.
Farrell insisted on dropping me first. I made him drop me at
the Pont de Caulaincourt because I didn't feel like hurting
the chauffeur's feelings by letting him drive me to my hotel
door.
Once they were gone I became joyously aware of being back
in Paris. I wished I could meet my self of seven years ago and
tell him boastfully that I was married to a real heiress and
if I lifted a finger I could have enough money to buy all the
jambonneau in the Abbesses market.
"But," he would have answered, his slightly less graying
beard shaking with mirth, "no one wants to eat all the jam-
bonneau in the Abbesses market."
"That's precisely why I don't lift my finger, chum."
I went into a bistro in the rue des Abbesses where I drank a
couple of Ricards. I went into a second bistro in the same
street where I tossed down a Picon. The third bistro I ap-
proached in sprightly humor. The proprietor was a M. Gas-
ton, a heavy drinker, and often I used to roll him for drinks.
The man behind the counter was hardly his shadow, his
cheeks sunken, and in spite of the heat of the afternoon he
wore a muffler around his neck. We exchanged greetings,
shaking hands. I pretended that I had observed no change inhim. I asked him to have a drink with me. He used to wallow
in tomate: that is, Pernod and grenadine. "I'm not drinking
these days," he said. "The doctor told me not to, and you
will agree, maître, that only a fool doesn't follow the doctor's
advice. After all, that's what doctors are for." I agreed with
him. "I'm getting better every day." I assured him he was.
When I left, one of the regulars of the bistro whom I
vaguely remembered followed me into the street and said:
"Gaston has cancer of the throat. He won't last long. Doctors
have given him up."
I walked on. Some of my sprightliness had left me; still,
dear Mme. Jeanne, the proprietress of the hotel where I had
starved and lived, then starved and lived even more intensely,
would cheer me up. I reached the rue des Trois Frères. The
seventh house to the right was the hotel. There was no hotel
sign. In vain I looked for the name. The signboard that had
dangled dangerously above the door wasn't there any more.
Perhaps it had dropped, which would not have been sur-
prising. I pushed the front door open. It couldn't have looked
less like a hotel. I knocked on the concierge's glass door. A
youngish woman came out. It had ceased to be a hotel, she
explained, four years ago. It had been converted into flats.
All the flats were let. I told her I wanted no flat but I did
want Mme. Jeanne's address. She said she knew not who
Mme. Jeanne was. She had come there only two years ago,
and frankly didn't care for the neighborhood. It wasn't re-
spectable. Before I left her she told me her husband was a
policeman.
I walked out of the rue des Trois Frères, my Gladstone bag
weighing me down. Mrs. Adams must have put heavy stones
into it. Now I had to look for a hotel. There in the rue de
Rivoli was a fine room in which I could with the sleeve of my
jacket polish fine ormulu. Yet I had turned it down. Perhaps,
I thought, dragging myself and the bag along, the concierge
was right. Not respectable, I said to myself, shaking my head.
Two old tarts stood quarreling outside a wine and coal shop.
Suddenly I became extremely grateful that over there in Wilt-
shire was a young creature who understood nothing, yet thereshe was. I hailed a taxi, which took me to a cheap hotel I re-
membered near the National Library. Now it wasn't cheap
any more. Still, I comforted myself, old Farrell would pay
for my meals.
I arrived at his hotel soon after six. "M. Farrell is ex-
pecting monsieur," said the concierge. "The page will take
you up." I said M. Farrell should come down. I went into the
bar. Farrell arrived, wearing a dinner jacket. "That's no
good for me, " I said. "I haven't brought one. Besides I don't
come to Paris to go to places where one wears dinner jackets."
He apologized, went upstairs to change, and came down in a
pin-striped suit-only the briefcase was missing. He said he
was completely in my hands. We entered the petrol-scented
opaque dusk of Paris. Dear, dear town. I stopped before a
bistro in the rue de Saint Honoré and said, "Let us go in."
He looked astonished, but followed me in. I asked for two
coups de rouge just to see how he took it. He took it like a
trooper, so I ordered a couple more. I had a long chat with
a man who maintained that French agriculture was doomed,
owing to the government's policy. Then I felt sorry for Far-
rell and took him away.
"Very enjoyable," he said, then, putting on a brave face,
asked me where we would go next.
I hailed a taxi, which cheered him up. We went to a
restaurant behind the Bastille. He didn't mind that. The
meal was the meal one longs for when away from France.
During dinner he said to me: "What did you do in the war?
I hope you don't mind my asking you. No business of mine."
I told him I had been an officer and a gentleman, mostly
stationed in Scotland, till I discharged myself on medical
grounds.
"I had no glory in the war either," he said. "Ministry of
Economic Warfare." I said so what? "When the war came
I said this would be my fulfilment, and dreamed of heroic
deeds. Hilda was already slipping from me. Perhaps, I
thought, fighting would be my compensation. I saw myself
as the last man holding the blazing fort. Then the job in the
ministry was offered and I jumped at it."I took him up to the Butte Montmartre to a sordid cabaret
where in my time an old girl friend of mine sang. Time had
degraded her, and now she foisted one expensive rose after the
other on Farrell. The proprietor, a swarthy, sweating man, sat
down at our table and complained bitterly of the police. The
girl friend came and sat down too. Farrell bowed to her; she
asked for champagne. By then I was happily and silently
drunk. Next morning I vaguely remembered that there had
been an altercation between the girl and the proprietor, owing
to her refusal to do the rounds of the tables to sell flowers.
She must have preferred Farrell's champagne.
He rang me at nine; I promised to see him after eleven.
I went out into the autumn Sunday, sky, sun and the Seine
all pale, the leaves golden. The thought occurred to me that
I would please Rosie if I looked in on Notre Dame. I arrived
there in the middle of High Mass. Two English tourists stood
before me, laughing and yapping. The bell rang at Elevation.
The male tourist said to the female tourist : "Like in a dining
car." She giggled.
"Shut up," I said. That wouldn't have pleased Rosie.
In the hotel I went up to Farrell's suite. It was, as I ex-
pected, all silk, gold and ormulu. I asked him if I could ring up

"Better not," he said. The man was blushing.
After my telephone call I returned to the salon. "Never
seen so many hairbrushes in my life," I said.
A waiter brought in a bottle of champagne. "We'll go out

On the chimneypiece next to the Empire clock was a small

unctuous voice came through. "A sermon," I said.
"Please don't switch it off," he said, coming nearer, a glass
of champagne in his hand.
The priest's voice practically filled the small sitting room."Jésus est mort," it said, "pour tuer nos offenses." The tele-

As far as I could make out it was the concierge he was

He walked up to the radio. "Oh, you stopped it," he said.

"Who?"
"The voice, the priest. How would you translate it into
English My French isn't too good."
"Jesus died to kill our offenses.'
"Jesus died to kill our offenses," he repeated. He sat down,

""
I didn't know what to say. There certainly was a catch to
the trip to Paris. The bubbly stuff nearly choked me-one
reason why I never much cared for champagne. "I can't," he
said. Then he said: "Oh, my Lord, help me." I felt acutely
uncomfortable: he might start crying any minute.
"It's too late now," I said. "Jean's on the way. You can't
let the poor girl down."
"Oh, my God," he said, hanging his head; then, almost
under his breath: "Why hast Thou forsaken me?" That made
me feel even more uncomfortable. I was an eavesdropper; I
had no business to listen to his dialogue with himself. I
wanted to persuade myself the man wasn't sincere; I tried to
tell myself that he was acting. "I'll be all right in a moment,”
he said, like one who has had a touch of giddiness.
"Have some champagne," I said. He obeyed me, though
it was an effort.
"It has always been like that," he said, looking around the
room as if he hadn't seen it before. "I always wanted the
absolute. One can't get it, so I just vacillate."I honestly don't get you," I said.
"You can't; nobody can. I do apologize to you." Suddenly
he laughed. "Do you know what I remind myself of? A
warrior. A warrior who's afraid to go into battle, but, ex-
ercising self-control, he rushes into battle; there fear over-
takes him, conquers him, and out he rushes again. Then
shame overtakes him and into battle he rushes again." He
was looking more cheerful.
"It's all a bit above my head," I said. "You have made
your mind up; you know the consequences; so what's all the
fuss about? Come, old man, you must admit it's all a bit
exaggerated. You want Jean, you want to marry her, you
have it all taped; then I switch on the radio by mistake,
you hear the priest say something you probably heard before
as a child, and you break out if I may use such a vulgar
expression-in a rash. Let's go out and throw ourselves on
the town."
"How simple it sounds," he said.
"It is, and come along."
He came. In order to humor him I went with him to one of
the world's most expensive restaurants, where one moment
your ears tell you you are in the Middle West, the next in
Germany. When we reached the framboise stage he asked:
"You've been married for nearly three years to Rosemary.
How do you feel about being married to a Catholic?"
At it again, I warned myself. "As a rank outsider," I said,
"I feel nothing special about it. I respect her convictions and
leave it at that. What an excellent framboise."
He ordered two more. "I have a cousin here in Paris," he
said, "and if she ever discovers that I was over here without
seeing her, she'll be deeply offended. I think I ought to go
and see her when we leave here.”
"But Jean arrives at four."
"That's it." He asked for the bill. “You'll have to do me
one more favor," he said. "Please go in the hotel car to the
airport, meet her and take her to the hotel. I'll be back by
then." I said all right, but couldn't help looking a bit
suspiciously at him. "I apologize again," he said as we wereready to rise, "for the scene I made before you, but there is
one thing I feel I owe to myself to explain to you. When I
worry as I did this morning, it isn't fear for myself. It's fear
of hurting Him."
"Hurting whom?'"
"Jesus, who died to kill our offenses." Oh my God, I said
to myself. "Don't look so solemn," he said, patting my arm.
"I won't start again."
"Don't you think it would be better for you to meet her?
She'll be so disappointed if you don't go."
"She'll understand; I'll meet her in the hotel. I won't stay
long with my cousin."
It was the same hotel chauffeur who drove me to Orly. With
no Farrell around we had quite a hearty chat. A cousin of his
was in New York earning vast sums. He himself was unmar-
ried because he believed one shouldn't marry before the age
of forty; on the other hand, his brother married at the age of
twenty-three. By then we had reached the airport.
It wouldn't have surprised me if I hadn't recognized Jean,
whom I had met only briefly, yet I recognized her at once be-
cause of that peacefulness of hers, which had struck me before
in the Ritz. The right woman for an emotionally unstable
fellow. She was like an English landscape on a sunny June
day, in spite of her Irish coloring. Among the French she
looked conspicuously a stranger. "You remember me," I said.
"Francis couldn't come because he had to go and see some
cousin of his who lives here, but he'll be waiting for you at
the hotel.”
"Oh, good," she said. "I'm so excited. I've never been
abroad before."
I observed that the chauffeur liked her appearance. In the
car she hardly spoke. She stared out through the window, and
from time to time laughed like Mary would if nanny took the
trouble to take her to the zoo. In the heart of town the laugh-
ter ceased: there remained only awe and amazement. The
chauffeur shook hands with both of us when we reached the
hotel. The concierge informed us that monsieur had tele-
phoned saying he would be a little late, but we should wait forhim in the suite. We went up, and the first thing I did was to
rush into the bedroom. I heaved a sigh of relief: the hair-
brushes were still there. "How pretty this is," said Jean. The
windows overlooked the river. "That's Notre Dame, isn't it?"
she asked at the window. I said it was.
"Are you an R.C. too?" I inquired. She said she wasn't.
Twenty minutes went by without Farrell's appearing. "I'll
telephone down for champagne," I said. "You ought to have
champagne."
"Look here," I said, becoming annoyed and worried after
the clock had ticked another forty minutes, "this isn't fair.
Your first visit, and here you are cooped up in this room."
"Probably he can't get away. He's usually so punctual."
That didn't make me feel any better; but surely he hadn't run
out on us?
"I'll take you to a café," I said. "It's not far from here.
We'll leave a message for Francis. He can join us there."
"I'd love to go to a real French café. Can one still sit
outside?"
I took her to the Weber in the rue Royale. Sunday after-
noon boredom was on. Parents pushed their children on the
pavement, children pushed their parents, and those who had
nobody to push, pushed everybody they came near enough to.
"That's the Madeleine," I said.
"I suppose every church here is R.C.?"
"Roughly," I said. Where was the man? After a while I
began to scan the faces of the passersby like a lover waiting
for the loved one. My eyes hardly left the corner of the rue
de Saint Honoré; I hardly listened to anything Jean said.
Suddenly a taxi stopped before us and Farrell got out.
"Here he is," said Jean.
Farrell kissed her hand, his eyes nearly popping out with
pleasure as if the man of the morning had existed only in my
sick imagination. He sat down beside her; I wouldn't have
been surprised if he had put his head on her bosom.
"I'm glad you've come," I said, "because I have to leave
you. I have a dinner date with an old friend." He said out
of the question, I should dine with them. I said out of thequestion, I couldn't let my old friend down. Luncheon to-
morrow? I would fly back in the morning, for I had an im-
portant appointment. I left them to themselves with my
blessings, whatever they are worth.
I threw myself on the town: that is, went up to the Butte,
getting back to my hotel only toward dawn. I left a note say-
ing if anybody telephoned I had left already. When I paid
my bill, I was told that a M. Farrell had rung at nine. After
paying my bill I had no money left, last night having cost me
a pretty penny. I had no money for the bus fare, so on arrival
I took a taxi to Regent's Park, praying that Mrs. Adams was
still in. She was.

I tried to dodge Roger, but he ran me to earth in the
middle of the week in the French pub. "I knew you'd be
here," he said. I had nothing positive against him: it was
only his company I disliked. He reciprocated that dislike,
which was natural enough. Now, however, he hailed me like
a dear friend with whom he shared a precious secret. The
Farrells, I had noticed, were like that, believing that their
needs and dreams were shared by others. Perhaps that was
the reason why Rosie married me. "How was it all?" Roger
asked. "How did it go? I'm dying of curiosity."
"You'd better come to a filthy drinking joint near here," I
said. "It's always empty at this time." He said yes, let us
go there, and almost led me in his eagerness to a place he
hadn't been to before. "Anyway," I said cunningly, "it's on
my way. I've a date at seven-a publisher-very important."
In the basement joint Fred and Moura were holding the
fort. I took Roger to a table at the end of the room. "I heard
from my father," said Roger; "a postcard. They're some-
where near Aurillac. Naturally he doesn't say 'we,' he says
'I.' '' I didn't make any comment. Moura, I could see, was
asking Fred who the chap was I was sitting with; and Fredwould have to admit he didn't know. "I've never met Jean,"
said Roger. "Do you think she's nice?" My nod implied that
she was. "Was he all right in Paris?"
"What do you mean by that?"
"I mean did he get depressed, did he have remorse, did
he oh, you surely know by now what I mean."
"He did get depressed, he did have remorse, he did talk of
hurting God, but he did fall into Jean's arms the moment he
got near enough to them."
"I suppose it'll always go on like that," said Roger.
"That I wouldn't know," I said, "because I don't cover the
dizzy heights where your father prances. He was practically
a stranger to me till he asked me to do him some trifling favor.
But now that I've seen him at close quarters, it looks to me as
if he's enjoying every minute of his double life. By double
life I mean, if I try to look at it from your Catholic point of
view, that he loves sin as much as remorse; so let us leave him
to both. If he had to struggle to keep himself alive, then he
would probably have time for neither."
"There's far more to it than that," said Roger. "It's diffi-
cult to explain. He really loves God. That sounds rather silly
if one says it like that, but that's the real fact behind it all.
For him-how shall I put it to you?-the life and death and
resurrection of Christ are a near, ever poignant, reality. I
know I'm not expressing myself well, because it's so difficult
to convey that it's like trying to explain why one loves music
or why certain music makes one cry. You see, Jasper, religion,
with so many people, means fear of God or fear of the Devil,
but with my father it's the other way around. It's as if God
feared him; that is, God is afraid that my father will be un-
grateful to him, "ungrateful" meaning that he'll refuse or
turn away from God's love
let's have another drink. One
just can't explain these things."
I went to the counter to get the drinks. Moura asked me
who the young man was. I said a young man, which was pre-
cisely who Roger was. Geoffrey appeared in the door, filling
it. "Look, Moura, here's Geoffrey," said Fred.
"Of course," said Roger, as I sat down, "there's the other
side too. But perhaps that side isn't as different as it looks.I don't know Jean, though he talks of her a lot to me. You
might think it's rather silly to talk of your mistress yet not
let your son meet her because she's your mistress. You might
say he's a hypocrite, but I know it to be pure shyness, and also
a sort of moral belief that I would disapprove of her because
she's his mistress."
"I see," I said, for I saw he had tracked me down not to
ask for news of his father, but to defend the father in case I
didn't think highly of him.
"He's so terribly in need of affection," said Roger. "Heart-
breakingly, helplessly. Jean gives it to him, but he can't be
with her all the time, so he ..."
"Isn't he marrying her any more?"
"I don't think his other side lets him."
"You mean the one that's busy with God?"
"Yes. But I do want you to understand. He's helpless be-
cause he needs warmth. Often when we chat he puts his hand
on my shoulder or my arm. It's to feel there's somebody; it's
to feel he isn't alone. You know how some people laugh or
smile at the dying Nelson for having said to Hardy: 'Kiss me,
Hardy.' Being my father's son I can well understand him.
My father always needs Hardy to kiss him even though he's
no Nelson, he's not dying, and as far as I know, he knows
nobody called Hardy."
"In my opinion he's not entitled to be kissed by Hardy be-
cause, as you say, he's not Nelson, he's not dying and he knows
nobody called Hardy. Now don't shake your head, Roger.
The fewer Hardys around the better for him. A young man
of your age should be thinking of other matters, such as young
girls, dancing and so on. Instead of that you waste your youth
being Hardy to his Nelson, and he can be Nelson because in
you he found his Hardy. It does him no good to be Nelson,
and he is clever, very clever with it. He tries to recruit Hardys
all the time. He's tried it on me too, but he won't succeed.
But I'm thinking of you. You ought to get out of his loving
clutches and lead the life a young man leads. Let your father
and Jean cope with your father. He doesn't distress me in
the slightest. He can deal with his shibboleths since they're
of his making. But you ought to keep away from them. Thatreminds me, I met here a pretty young girl called Virginia
something, came here with a good-for-nothing I know, and she
told me she used to be your girl friend, and immediately
started asking me questions about your father." Roger was
blushing, and looked as defenseless as he believed his father
was. "Now girls don't ask about their lovers' fathers if those
fathers haven't been crammed down their throats," I said
brutally. "You're blushing, so you know I'm right. Go and
make up with her, say you won't speak of your father again;
then dance with her in the moonlight, which is your duty to
your manhood and age. Besides, I'd be delighted if she kicked
out that fellow Max Whim, who's a phoney, and I detest him.
Now I must go or I'll be late for my appointment."
"Couldn't you dine with me?" he asked.
I said I couldn't, though I was truly grateful to him, for in

in that trap of affection with a lot of kiss-me-Hardy. "Go-
ing?" asked Fred. I said I was.
I parted from Roger in the street. "I don't think you un-
derstand," he said.
But I did, and when I went down on Sunday morning to
Orchard House to fetch Rosie and Mary, I told Rosie straight

nant.
"You've no right to judge him," I said. There, I was de-
fending him. "The man is entitled to happiness too."
"But it isn't fair to mummy. There's no need for it." I
explained that there was, as he cared for another woman,
therefore he wanted to be free in order to be able to marry
her. "In a registry office," said Rosie.
"We're on dangerous ground," I said, "and we two never
quarrel, do we?"
That put an end to that. Frankly I admired Farrell. Ididn't think that he would have the guts to start the ball
rolling. Hilda I found in a more cheerful mood. "He'll
never go through with it," she said, "but if he did try-which
isn't impossible, because weak people let themselves be influ-
enced-then I'll go to see him. Francis has never refused me
anything."
"Not even the separation?" I said.
"Not even that," she said. "Why didn't you come down
last night and spend the night here?" Before I could think

Mrs. Walsh is so moved when he comes that she overcooks
everything. So I'll leave you to the drinks; I'm going to
watch over her."
The priest was a friendly old boy with white hair. After
lunch he walked with me in the garden. "What do you make,
sir, of the Farrells? I'm referring to my parents-in-law." I
didn't quite know why I asked him that.
"It's a great pity that they're separated," he said. "A
very great pity indeed. Now that we're here I should like to
ask you a question, Mr. Danesbridge, if I may." I said by all
means. "Do you know how much petrol different brands of
motor bikes consume?'"
"I beg your pardon?"
"Perhaps I had better begin at the beginning. I have a
little old prewar car, a faithful little thing, but it eats too
much petrol and has to be repaired all the time, so I'm afraid
I must get rid of it. I couldn't afford a new car, and old cars
all suffer from the same ailments, so it'll have to be a motor
bike."
"I'm frightfully sorry, but I know nothing about motor-
cycles," I said. He looked so disappointed that I was sorry
for him. "Never rode one in my life," I added.
"Oh well, I'm sure I'll find somebody who has. To judge
by those unfortunate accidents, quite a lot have."
A nice old boy. Mrs. Walsh called us in; it was time for me
and my family to depart. It was only when we were already
all seated in the station wagon that Rosie casually mentioned
that the Sealyham puppy in Mrs. Walsh's lap-Mrs. Walsh
never missed an outing-would come to London and live withus in the flat. "What you mean," I said, "is that you're
going to have a dog."
"All of us," said Rosie. "His name is Smoke."
I could see myself dragging the dog into the basement club;
I could hear what Fred and Moura would say behind my back.
As a matter of fact the puppy caused no trouble, because
nanny adopted it at once and it took its exercise with her and
Mary.
Once we were back in London, what with Rosie's presence,
regular meals, and now the dog too, the last fortnight looked
topsy-turvy in retrospect. I began to doubt I had been to
Paris; Farrell and the hotel radio seemed equally unreal.
A week went by; then one morning Roger telephoned Rosie.
She spoke to him. I was in the dining room, which in the
mornings was my study, when Rosie came in as pleased as
Punch, and said Farrell was back, and had told Roger he
wouldn't proceed with the divorce. I couldn't help snickering
a little.
"Twenty years from now," I said, "you'll come in here to
tell me that your father is divorcing or isn't divorcing-
and all you Farrells will have another emotional orgy."
"I was thinking only of mummy," said Rosie.
She had the same expression in her eyes as she had a week
ago when the puppy was planted into my life. "What is it?"
I asked. "Out with it." She said nothing. “You've asked
them both to dinner," I said.
"Next Thursday," she said. "Please be nice to them."
They came to dinner the next Thursday, Farrell talking al-
most the whole time to me, and Roger giving me conspira-
