either of them any more. But while Farrell was in the nursery
to look at Mary, Roger quickly told me that as far as he knew
his father wasn't seeing Jean. "The other side of the medal,"
I said, unimpressed. They left at eleven; I had to admit they
had been good company. Smoke hadn't been: he was sick
in the living room.
On the following morning I received a letter. I didn't know
the handwriting. At the age of fifty one feels the same way
with handwriting as with faces. One prefers the old to thenew. I played about with the envelope before tearing it open.
As always I forgot to use the paper knife Rosie gave me long
ago. In the hall nanny and Mrs. Adams were chatting; Rosie
was out. From the living room a weird sound came; prob-
ably Smoke was having a try at the carpet or an armchair. I
looked first at the signature. It was Jean Epton. “Ah,” I
said, and read the letter. She asked me to come and see her
at the country club she ran in Burnham Beeches, if I could,
and have a drink with her. She would so very much like to
talk to me. "It is urgent," she wrote, "but only for me."
That rather moved me. Still, if she wanted to talk to me she
could do so on the telephone. I went into the bedroom which
was farthest from nanny's and Mrs. Adams' ears. It was Jean
who answered the telephone.
"I got your letter," I said, "but I don't think I can get to
Burnham Beeches. I'm all booked up." I waited: she said
nothing. "Couldn't you write me and tell me all about it?"
She said she couldn't put it into a letter. It was too difficult.
"Aren't you coming to London some time? We could fix a
date." I hadn't wanted to go as far as that. All I wanted
was not to be rude to her, since she wasn't the sort of person
who deserved rudeness.
"I can't," she said. "One of the bartenders is away on his
vacation, so I just can't get away."
"Let's wait till he comes back."
"But it's so urgent for me," she said in a pleading voice. It
struck me that Farrell might not mind making her miserable
when the urge to be good possessed him. I said I would go to
Burnham Beeches tomorrow afternoon.
It was an unpleasantly windy day, and the wind caught me
nastily as I stepped off the train at Burnham Beeches. I
wished I hadn't come. A man whose expression showed that
he didn't care for the wind either came up to me to say he had
been sent by the manageress to take me to the club in his taxi.
I appreciated that. As we drove against the wind I asked him
what sort of place the club was. "Elegant," he said. There
was an oak in front of the club. My first impression was that
the club was pretentious and boring. I didn't lose that im-
pression. Several businessmen were at the bar; also a coupleof fellows with handlebar mustaches who looked like automo-
bile salesmen. Jean was behind the bar, a morose assistant at
her side; the businessmen outdid or should I say outbid?-
each other in being chummy yet very gentlemanly in an old-
world fashion with her. One who arrived almost at the same
time as I did kissed her hand with a ham actor's flourish.
When she caught sight of me, she detached herself from the
bar, leaving the morose fellow in charge. The businessmen 's
eves followed her to the door. She took me into a deserted-
looking room with a few card tables and some leather arm-
chairs. The fireplace was empty, but the central heating kept
the room warm. It was bitterly cold outside.
"Sit down," she said. "I'll get you a drink."
"I hope I'm not making those fellows jealous."
"They probably think you're representing some whisky
firm. Their imagination doesn't rise above that." She brought
a tray with a bottle of whisky, a siphon and two glasses. I had
spotted at our first meeting that there was nothing mean

felt you were a kind person the first time we met." I bowed.
"I'm utterly wretched about Francis," she went on, after
pouring out two stiff drinks, "because I know he's in utter
misery. He doesn't have to be."
"He likes being miserable. So why disturb him in his
misery?"
"You mustn't say that," she cried. "He doesn't deserve
that from you. He spoke of you with such affection. He's
fond of you and respects you. He spoke of you such a lot
while we were in France."
"I'm very fond of him too," I said, "but since all his mis-
ery is of his own making, I can't feel sympathetic."
"What did he tell you?" I said he had told me nothing
since his return. "Then I'd better tell you about our trip to
France," she said.
They had gone to some place in Auvergne. She couldn't
remember the name but she had it written down in her note-
book, which she would fetch at once. I told her not to bother.
The place was small, the hotel almost deserted, as it was the
end of the season already. The hotel was near a lake and a dam.It rained intermittently. The red cattle were just coming
down from the mountain pastures. Farrell had hired a car in
which they made many excursions. On the seventh day of
their stay they motored to a little town up on a plateau called
Salers. That name she remembered. In the church was an
entombment, very beautiful, with lifelike and life-size figures
in painted stone, except for the dead Christ, who was of mar-
ble. Farrell told her the figures were Burgundian. He stood
there for hours and became very depressed. She had to go

"How ungrateful we all are," and things like that.
"Quite," I said, "though I must admit that I was very
much impressed myself by the Mise en Tombeau of Salers."
"So you've been there?" I said I had. I had been prac-
tically all over France except the north and the east. "That
still wasn't the real trouble," continued Jean. “I don't know
if you know, but when I went to France he was determined to
divorce his wife and marry me. I said it was so unnecessary,
but he insisted. Even after those visits to Salers he assured
me that that was all he wanted and he had weighed the pros
and cons. Then one afternoon as we came down from Salers

garb in front of us on the roadside."
"Mrs. Epton, have we got Campari?" asked the lugubrious
bartender from the door. “I can't find it."
"Excuse me for a moment," said Jean, and went out with
him. I stood up and walked around the room, my eyes unable

probably said Farrell was fond of me because women know
that flattery takes one farther than appealing for pity, which
we males so often try. When she returned, she switched on
the lights. She was decidedly a pretty woman.
"What happened to the monk?" I asked. "Did you run
over him?”
"On the contrary," she said, after pouring me out a big
whisky, "we stopped and Francis gave him a lift. He sat in
the back. I can still smell the moist smell of his rough garb.
He was a cheerful man and talked to Francis in slow but never
stopping French-slow, I suppose, because we were foreigners.I got the gist of it. It was mostly questions. He asked whether
we were married; Francis said yes. I wish he hadn't. Then
the monk asked if we had children and all that. When we ar-
rived in some village the monk got out, and as he took leave

"Ghastly," I said.
"I hoped he would come to me, because I wanted to speak

"Tell him what?"
"You could tell him, because I know you understand that
there's no need for that marriage business, there honestly

myself well, do I?''
One of the businessmen looked in through the open door,

frowned, then went.
"I can't see how I could help you," I said. "I couldn't

"You're a remarkable woman," I said.
She shook her head. "No, but you see I'm self-sufficient-I
think most women are. He isn't; he needs me, and in his need

were in his place."
"Are you sure?" she asked me, as she walked past the bar
with me toward the front door. "I can't sleep at night be-
cause he's alone."
"
"I'm sure, I said.
The taxi had been waiting for me the whole time. I hoped
I had enough money on me to pay it. "Don't give him any-
thing," she said. "I asked you here, so it's on me."
I repeated to myself that she was a remarkable woman.
While I stood at the station waiting for my train and watch-

Virgin Mary and the others were dressed like rich Burgun-
dians of the period. Their expressions were of grief but not

in the wind on the railway platform I couldn't see what I had
meant at the time, which was twelve years ago. A train came
and kindly stopped. I got into a third-class compartment and
of course there were soldiers in it. They smoked like chim-
neys. Some Frenchman who had been standing beside me in
front of the Entombment had said to me, his only audience:
"The dead Christ knows-just look at His face-but in His
mercy He won't let us know."
"Know what?" I had asked.
"The anguish of death," the Frenchman had said.
"If you don't mind," one of the soldiers said to me, as I
tried to open the window of the compartment, "we'd rather
not have the window open.”Chapter Four
I
I went to have an early drink of beer in a pub in Charing
Cross Road where I hadn't been before. I went there because
I didn't feel like seeing Fred. Fred was there. "I didn't
know you came to this pub," he said.
"Nor did I."
"We see less and less of you," he said. "But that was to
be expected. If one puts one foot into another sort of life, the
second foot is bound to follow after a while." He waited. I
said nothing, so on he went. "One doesn't go unscathed even
when one thinks one hasn't surrendered. I can still hear you
telling Moura and me that an ugly little rich girl was chas-
ing you."
"Chaps like you and fine ladies like Moura never forget,
and then you're surprised that I loathe the guts of both of

"I'm sorry, I didn't mean it that way."
"You can mean it only one way."
"You always misunderstand me, Jasper," he said, and

one can't walk in and out of other people's lives without..."
"Does anybody walk in and out of your life?" I asked,
pushing my beard practically into his face."Only myself," he said, so I bought him a beer.
"Pray when did I ever say an ugly little rich girl was chas-
ing me?" I asked. It rankled, for I might have said that in
the beginning. The moral of it is that one should keep one's
trap shut in the beginning. "It was at a party in a basement
in Chalk Farm," he said. "It was given by that girl whose
Christian name we never knew. Everybody called her by her
surname. Sprag."
..
"I've got it at last," I cried. "Sprag Spraggon. Is
there a telephone here?" They said there was. I looked up

Farrell was in conference. I said cut that out and tell him
Mr. Danesbridge wished urgently to speak to him. Family
matter. She said she would try. I said she had better.
She got Roger on the telephone. I told him to get me his

I said "not too well" because there was little air in the
telephone box. He said he was sorry to hear that and we

"I rang you up to tell you you were wrong," I said. "The
man with whom Mr. Sponge got drunk and they didn't find
the ladies when they fell out of the dining room was Sprag-
gon. Spraggon."
"Of course, how right you are; yours is a so much better
memory," said Farrell, much relieved. "It was kind of you
to ring me up and tell me. Could you lunch with me today? I
should like that very much."
I said yes. I saw no reason why I shouldn't see him. Last
Saturday I had a short letter from Jean saying I was right
and everything was all right again. "Well, I must be going
now," I said to Fred, and walked in the messy rain to the
French pub. Fred came five minutes later. I set my alarm.
It went off just as he started on life again.
"A lunch engagement?" he asked.
"With my rich father-in-law," I said.
"You mustn't sneer at them," said Fred. "I've heard a lot
of very nice things about them-I mean your wife's family.Whatever we think, it is our duty to respect believing Chris-
tians. It won't be the fattened, we-never-had-it-so-good I'm-
all-right-Jacks, nor the scientists, who'll take the world out of
the present impasse. It will be Christianity that'll take the

"That's it, Fred," I said.
"Sneering won't help," he said. He said that to Moura
who had appeared. I was on my way out.
In the expensive restaurant Farrell behaved mildly and
soberly, going even so far as to apologize for not having been
as good a host in Paris as I deserved. Jean had told him of
my visit; now everything was going well between them; he
had spoken to Hilda on the telephone the other day and as-

he later admitted, Hilda who rang him up. "If this is my
life, then it is my life," he said. I told him I applauded his
sentiments.
"I think Roger has made up with his girl," he said with the
cheese. I said that was excellent. To reciprocate his confi-
dences I told him the puppy called Smoke would keep the
good juice, even if you walked him for an hour, for the living-
room carpet. "I don't want you to judge me by the ass I
made of myself that morning in Paris," he said with the mira-
belle. (He remembered the mirabelle.) "A real Christian is
a person capable of sacrifice. I never made one in my life.
Some years ago I decided to give up smoking for Lent. That
lasted for three days. Even now I can see my hand going out
as if it were my master to take a cigarette out of the cigarette
box."
"Now you're getting tense again," I said.
"I am fond of you, Jasper," he said, laughing.
We parted as the best of friends, and now I began to see
quite a lot of him. In my benign presence he shed some of his
shyness with Rosie. Consequently she shed some of hers. One
day toward Christmas I lunched with him and Jean. There
were plenty of Pernods in me; I was in excellent form, in my
own eyes at least, and dominated the conversation. I told
them of the time when I was a prep school master. That wasduring my darkest days before the war. I said I admired the
headmaster's wife more than I ever admired a woman before
or since.
"Oh, why?" asked Farrell.
"Because she knew how to feed boys without letting them
die of starvation."
Jean laughed: he didn't. He just couldn't see the point.
She went off shopping after lunch and I walked out of the
restaurant with him. It was a wistful, foggy afternoon with
murderers and elves lurking in the fog. Farrell suddenly said
to me: "How perfect life would be if I could only have a
brother-and-sister relationship with Jean."
For the first time I felt inordinately sorry for the man.
There was Hilda who was praying for her husband to become
her brother, and here the lover was praying that he should
become his mistress' brother. I left him rather abruptly.
Christmas came, and we went to stay with Hilda. Mrs.
Walsh had too much brandy butter and cried her eyes out for
the late Mr. Walsh. Farrell and Roger were spending Christ-
mas with the head of the family, called Matthew, whom I had
met only at Rosie's and my wedding, but it sufficed. It had
been like that since the separation. I accompanied them to
Midnight Mass, which pleased Rosie. On Boxing Day Rosie
went to see an old convent pal of hers, leaving me alone with
Hilda for luncheon. After lunch Hilda reminisced, under the
influence of brandy. She told me in her harsh, matter-of-fact
yet forbearing manner that two years after the separation,
Farrell came down to stay with her for the night. In the mid-
dle of the night he tried to break into her bedroom. Luckily
it was locked. "And just think of it," she said. "The chil-
dren were in the house." I poured her out more brandy.
We left next day in the midst of a mild snowfall, which had
belated Christmas-card pretensions. With a melting snowflake
on her aquiline nose, Hilda said: "Jasper, I thank God that
my daughter has found such an excellent husband." She
kissed me on the cheek, and the snow was my witness that I
nearly cried.
With unimaginative promptitude New Year's arrived. Just
to show Rosie what an excellent husband I could be, I broughtFred and Moura back for a drink on New Year's Eve. They
behaved boringly well and hardly touched a drink. Moura's
eyes were moist when nanny brought in Mary; before leaving,
Fred mumbled something about his faith in mankind being
restored.
"You ought to ask them more often," said Rosie.
"Not on your life," I said. Then one afternoon toward the
end of January Farrell rang up to say he would like to come
around for a drink. I said he should. Nanny poked her head
into the dining room where I was grappling with a review
which was becoming longer and longer-that is, it was getting
out of hand. She had something to tell me. "Yes, nanny," I
said. She told me. In Regent's Park that morning she chatted
with a lady who was an expert on Sealyhams. The lady
examined Smoke, reaching the conclusion that he was all
wrong as a Sealyham; could never be a show dog; and, what
was simply horrible, he had an underslung jaw. I said I was
sorry to hear that, but it didn't change my opinion of Smoke.
"I wasn't thinking of that," said nanny. As I knew what
she was thinking of, I asked her to leave me with my urgent
work. "If one gives a present..." muttered nanny darkly
as she left the room. Clever old Hilda, I said to myself.
Farrell came with a surprising proposal. He and Roger
were going to Bordeaux on business. The firm had interests
there. He suggested that I accompany them as interpreter.
There would be plenty of conferences with heads of vinicul-
ture and agriculture. As interpreter I would get ten pounds
a day for expenses and ten pounds a day for myself. "How
marvelous," cried Rosie.
"Can't be done," I said. They couldn't budge me.
"But why? Why?" asked Rosie, after Farrell left. It must
be said for him that he didn't insist after I said no for the
sixth time.
"You may remember I laid down one rule when I married
you," I said. "That's why I said no.”
She said: "Why did you accept the invitation to Paris?"
I explained that was a private invitation and didn't in-
fringe on the rule. "You have to understand, Rosie, that he
chose me as interpreter because I'm one of the family. If Ihadn't been, then he'd have taken, as he probably will, some-
body who knows all the technical expressions men who sell
machinery and fertilizers use. I haven't a clue. We don't
quarrel, so this argument is over."
"Have you been to Bordeaux?" she asked, to change the
subject.
"Indeed. I was thrown out of a circus there." Rosie bright-
ened up. "It was a blood-curdlingly cold night and the wind
was blowing the Gironde backwards. The streets were de-
serted. In that huge terres vague near the statue of the Giron-
dins there was a circus tent. The performance would soon start.
I went up to the box office. I could see the inside: not a soul.
The manager was standing on my left. The old girl's eyes in
the box office lit up: a customer. I said 'Presse Anglaise; give
me a free ticket.' The man and the woman nearly lynched
me."
Rosie was amused. Smoke insinuated he wanted to lift a
leg. I took him out; nothing happened, so we came back. In
the night I had an awful nightmare in which I was made a
director of Farrell & Sons. But worse was to come. I left the
office in the company's Rolls Royce. The chauffeur was the
Paris hotel chauffeur: that added to the clarity of the night-
mare. I told him to stop at the Palace Theatre. I left my
fur-collared overcoat in the Rolls, and walked along Old
Compton Street feeling clever and cunning, believing nobody
would notice that I had betrayed my past. I went into the
French pub, asked for half a pint of mild-and-bitter so that
all should see I was still the old Jasper. Fred came up to me
like a sneering gargoyle. Moura sneered at his side. "It's no
good lying and cheating any more," he said.
"No good at all," said Moura, who even in nightmares had
to say something banal.
"I don't understand what you mean," I said.
Fred unfurled the Times. "Look," he said. I looked at a
photograph with the caption: DIRECTORS IN SESSION: Farrell
& Sons. I was number ten on the photograph, which meant I
was third to the left of the chairman. I woke up in a sweat.
Smoke was growling a little because he couldn't swallow
whole my left slipper.As far as we knew Farrell and Roger went off according to
their schedule. In the basement club I saw the pretty girl,
Virginia, again. She was with Max. "I should very much
like to talk to you," she said. I told her if she wanted to talk
to me she should come to a pub farther up the street. It was
sure to be quieter there. Max looked daggers when she left
the club with me. That made my evening. She went straight
to the point in the silence of the three-quarters-empty pub.
She had made up with Roger who, so she explained, had pro-
posed to her before they fell out. It was she who brought up
the question of marriage again. On second thought she had
found the idea not unattractive.
"We do live in the age of marriages," I remarked. She
gave me a vague smile, then went on to say that Roger said
he had thought it over too, and though he loved her, and so
on, he reached the conclusion that he couldn't marry for the
time being.
"Do you know the reason he gave me?" said the pretty girl
indignantly. I said I didn't. "He said he couldn't leave his
father. His father depended too much on him." As I showed
no surprise, she became even more indignant.
"It isn't that I don't sympathize with you," I said. "But
the father is made like that. He needs one, and if one is within
his orbit then one is caught by his need; and the trouble is, it
is he who is not aware of his need, only you the victim are.
I'll tell you something else too. Because there's no solution to
his need, the whole wretched thing will go on like that till
Doomsday or his death."
"You put that very interestingly," said Virginia, 'but to
me it looks ludicrous, stupid and wicked. If his father is such
an egoist, then Roger should leave him, if he's a man. But it
seems he's not a man.
"How can you leave somebody whose need is ... oh, you
must forgive me: I'm late for an appointment." I wasn't,
but Fred had come in, his reproachful eyes telling me I should
be ashamed for running after girls when I had such a charm-
ing wife. He was Rosie's devoted admirer since New Year's
Eve. I collided with Moura as I left the pub.
I said nothing to Rosie about my chat with Virginia, sinceshe would have taken the girl's side; and I myself couldn't
help thinking that in spite of the many points he had in his
favor Farrell was a pain in the neck. That was convincingly
shown two days later.
The day started with sleet. Nanny reported that Mary had
a bad cold; that got Rosie straight out of bed. Because of the
sleet I turned on my side and slept till ten, waking in fits,
forcing myself without any resistance back into sleep. By ten
Mary's cold wasn't bad any more: on the other hand, Smoke's
nose was hot. So he was dosed. At twenty past ten I got into
my bath; at twenty-five past ten Rosie came into the bathroom
to talk to me. Nanny had decided to take Mary and Smoke
out, cold, hot nose and all. Around ten thirty-five Mrs. Adams
knocked excitedly on the door. "A phone call from France,"
she called out. Rosie went out to speak on the telephone. I
stretched myself in my bath and lit a cigarette.
"Darling," called Rosie, "please get out of your bath. It's
Roger; it's terribly important." It took me less than five
minutes to reach the telephone. What, I wondered, were the
Farrells up to this time?
"Jasper," said Roger, "my father has vanished."
"I beg your pardon," I said. "Did you say vanish?"
"Vanished, disappeared."
"Can't you make yourself clearer?"
He did. Yesterday his father and some French businessman
had gone to Bayonne on business. They came back late. Far-
rell had looked depressed in the extreme and went straight up
to his bedroom. Roger went out dining with some French
businessman, his father refusing to accompany him though
they had been invited together. When Roger came back the
concierge gave him a note from his father. Roger read the
note over the telephone: "Dear boy, go back to London. I
have no intention of coming back here. I am going away.
Please don't wait for me but go home. Your loving father,
F. F."
"What are you going to do about it?" I asked. Rosie stood
beside me, her eyes on me when I spoke, her eyes on the tele-
phone when Roger spoke. She was following the conversation.
"Are you coming back?" He said of course not, then askedme to come out at once to Bordeaux to help him to find his
father. Even from the distance his voice was pitiful. He
wanted, he went on, nobody to know about Farrell's disap-
pearance. By nobody he meant Uncle Matthew and the office.
It might give rise to scandal, to gossip, might even get into
the papers. I felt he shuddered when he said it might even
get into the papers. The Farrells were averse to publicity.
"You speak French so well," he said, "and you're the only
person who would know how to set about finding him. We
must find him, Jasper. Please come at once.
"
"I haven't the money," I said. Rosie nearly shrieked as she
said she would give me the money. Roger was saying the same
on the telephone. "All right," I said, "but only my fare."
"You are infuriating," said Roger. "Don't bother about
such details."
"Darling, you're doing that for us; naturally we have to
pay all your expenses," Rosie was saying.
"Your sister is talking the whole time, so I can't hear a
word you say," I said. That quieted Rosie. Roger suggested I
should fly to Paris in the afternoon and take the night train to
Bordeaux. I said I didn't fly in winter, but would cross in the
ferry and take the train to Bordeaux tomorrow. Roger asked
me to send Rosie to the telephone.
"I'll fix the tickets," she said when she rang off. "But
where is he?"
"We don't know," I said.
"Has he decided to disappear for good?"
"That's what his letter implies."
"But what does disappearing mean?" she asked, agitated.
"Can one do that nowadays? How does one disappear? What
does it mean?'"
"One does disappear nowadays but usually only behind the
Iron Curtain, but then one has to take some secrets with one.
Your father has no secrets."
"Don't joke," she said. "This is terrible. Disappear?
What will Mummy say?"
"For the moment I'd tell her nothing. I wouldn't worry,
Rosie. Ebullient extroverts like your father don't vanish for
good. I'll find him-don't worry, child.""He had no right to do that," said her mother's daughter.
"Serious people don't disappear. Now I'm going out to get
your tickets."
I told her not to get me a sleeper on the ferry. That started
an argument. She brought back a first-class ticket to Paris,
and in order to repay me for refusing a sleeper she bought a
Pullman ticket for the Sud-Express. Then she wired Roger
saying I would arrive tomorrow on the Sud-Express.
In the afternoon I had a sudden idea and upbraided myself
for not having thought of it before. I was alone in the flat,
so telephoned Jean. She hadn't heard from Farrell since
he left for Bordeaux. Thus I had no cause to upbraid my-
self. I didn't tell Jean that he had disappeared. The Far-
rells' fear of publicity and being subjected to ridicule ap-
parently was contagious. Perhaps Fred was right after all.
I consoled myself with my having refused a sleeper.
Rosie saw me off. "Do find him," she said. "Imagine what
Uncle Matthew will say if you don't find him. It was he who
sent him to Bordeaux."
"I'll find him with the least possible fuss," I said. Yes, it
was contagious.
I dawdled in the customshouse, busy with a far from divert-
ing thought. I nearly missed the ferry. When I came on
board I went straight into the first-class bar. The ferry was
shivering under me. Huddled in a corner of the bar with bot-
tles of French beer before them sat Moura, Geoffrey and Fred.
"Gracious," said Moura.
"What are you doing here?" I asked.
"Geoffrey has an assignment in Milan," said Moura
proudly. "He's taking us with him. Where are you going?"
"Traveling with his wife, of course," said Fred. "She's in
the sleeper, and Jasper has come to mingle with the crowd
before turning in."
"My wife isn't here, and I've no sleeper. As a matter of
fact I'm on an assignment too."
"Oh," said Geoffrey. "Where are you going?"
"I've been commissioned to write an article on Evians-les-
Bains in winter." Evians-les-Bains, according to my geogra-phy, was as far as possible from Bordeaux. "Better have a
drink," I said. Their eyes were focused on the suitcase I had
brought along. It was nanny's fault. Instead of packing my
old Gladstone bag, she shoved my things into a Farrell pig-
skin case. Moura nudged Fred, who then caught Geoffrey's
eye. Geoffrey looked at the suitcase too. They chose to have
French beer. I saw myself sitting with them till the cows
came home: that is, till we reached Dunkirk. Luckily I was
wrong, for once we were under way they became seasick one
by one and rushed off. Now and again one of them staggered
back, only to rush off after a bit of groaning. Geoffrey was
the sickest of the group. They were back together around
three in the morning, pale and green, and lay in deck chairs
with their eyes closed.
By then I was as certain as my mind could admit any cer-
tainty that Farrell had killed himself. It stood to reason that,
chased by himself from pillar to post and back, he had found
life too difficult to master. His religion on one side, and his
need of Jean's safe presence and, presumably, body were a
problem to which he could find no solution; and worse still, it
was limitless, and time couldn't cure it. He must have bruised
himself pretty hard falling continuously between two stools.
I appreciated that nothing, in theory, is more abhorrent to a
Catholic than taking his own life, the direct road to Hell. But
with an exalted chap like Farrell, even eternal damnation
might have a sort of morbid masochistic attraction.
As the ferry pitched, rolled and tossed, or whatever a ship
does when the sea throws it about like a toy it wishes to dis-
card, I thought of one poor Lucie whom I had known in the
days of Mme. Jeanne's hotel. She was a sick old drunk, had
been to a hospital, was discharged as incurable, and one late
morning she simply hanged herself in the lavatory of a bistro
facing the hotel. She wasn't given a religious funeral, which
angered the other old drunks of the street. She must have
overlooked eternal damnation. With such cheerful mental
pictures I dozed off. I felt some weight on my lap; in my fit-
ful sleep I imagined it was Smoke, poor chap, with a chip on
his shoulder too. He hadn't been the same dog since thewoman in Regent's Park declared him underjawed. I awoke
when the ship became quiet. The weight in my lap was Fred's
head. "Really," I said.
We parted company in Dunkirk. They were taking a later
train and would have to change at Amiens, for they bought
first-class tickets only for the boat. An icy wind blew from the
east; I dozed in the train till the Gare du Nord. Funny, I
thought, I was in Paris again.
The Pullman carriage of the Sud-Express was alive with
Portuguese, who quieted down when the Sud-Express reached
the speed of a hundred miles or so an hour. Through the
window I watched the impatient man's ideal landscape; by
the time you focused your eyes on a tree, another tree was in
its place already. With little exaggeration the same could
have been said of wayside stations. Only the dead wintry sky
remained unchanged. The armchair facing me was empty. At
the next table sat two small obese Portuguese, the wife not too
happy with her figure, the husband satisfied with his fat. He
clutched the arms of his chair thinking of business deals and
nudes of Paris; she of dresses, coats and accessories. I decided
they had three slim children.
It was no good. I couldn't keep my mind off Farrell. The
man had killed himself. His letter to Roger proved that.
Though ebullient, exalted and an extrovert, he, like his son
and daughter, had the bashfulness the French call pudeur;
and if you have that you don't write eloquent good-by letters
when wanting to do away with yourself. "Go back home," or
something to that effect, he had said in his letter to Roger.
That was precisely what, under the circumstances, a man of
his pudency would say. The steward asked me if I wanted
an aperitif. I said most decidedly. In fact had three.
After a copious meal I fell asleep and was visited by Fred,
who spoke in Portuguese to me. To his and my surprise I
answered him in Portuguese and in Portuguese we discussed
Farrell's suicide. It was boiling hot in the Pullman when I
awoke. The train was crossing the bridge over the Gironde;
the wind was blowing the river backwards. A steward stood
with my suitcase in the doorway. The stiff luncheon bill lay
on the table; I gasped; I paid; little money was left. Thetrain stopped; I got out into the foggy cold of Bordeaux
station, and there in the crowd stood Roger, but alone. With
that my last hope went. While I became more and more con-
vinced that Farrell had killed himself, the clearer I had seen
him waiting for me with Roger at Bordeaux station. In my
acute disappointment, which surprised me, I didn't bother to
ask him whether his father were found.
II
We had already been half an hour in the Farrells' suite in
the Splendide. I should have expected that they would choose
that hotel. I used to pass it when I was in Bordeaux twelve
years ago staying in a small hole near the station. In the
course of that half hour I became certain that Roger had the
same fear as I. That put up a nice high wall between us.
"Show me that letter," I said. The words were the same
words he read to me on the telephone.
"What does Rosemary think?" he asked, pouring me a
drink. The wind rattled the windowpanes, the same wind that
froze me twelve years ago.
"That's neither here nor there at the moment," I said.
"Does mummy know?" he asked.
"Not if Rosie listened to me."
"He might have gone back to Jean."
"I've told you he hasn't."
"He might have gone today. You spoke to her yesterday."
"Now look here, Roger, we're only wasting time. Your
father has vanished, has he not?" He just nodded. "If one
vanishes, one doesn't go to Burnham Beeches." He shrugged
his shoulders. "You really think your father wrote you that
note, disappeared suddenly, left his business unfinished be-
cause he wanted to get back to Jean whom he would have seen
anyway by the weekend? It doesn't make sense. You have
to change your whole attitude in this case. All you're think-
ing of is that it shouldn't leak out. Like that we'll never get
on his trail."
"I'm not thinking of that.""Have you been to the police?"
"Of course not. That's the last thing we want."
"Supposing your father was murdered?" He looked of-
fended. "Even a Farrell can get murdered. Much better
people have been. Now tell me again what happened on that
night: that is, tell me what happened the whole day."
"I don't know what happened the whole day," said Roger,
who, I was sure, bitterly regretted the impulse that made him
ask me out. "He went off to Bayonne with a chap called
Centenac-he's a big wine shipper."
"Why did he take him to Bayonne?"
"You know the French better than I. It was just to take
him there and give him a huge meal, and they lunched with
some other fellows of Centenac's sort."
"I see," I said. "Very good ham comes from Bayonne.
That reminds me: I don't know what your plans are, but I
want to dine tonight in a small restaurant-can't remember its
name nor the name of the street, but they serve or did serve
wonderful sausages cooked in wine. I've been waiting to go
back there for twelve years." He said he would take me
wherever I wanted to go. "And then your father came back
at seven and said he caught a cold, and you found him look-
ing ghastly?"
"He looked deadly pale."
"Like one who has seen a ghost. He didn't give a lift to a
monk or someone like that?"
"I don't understand you," said Roger.
"Never mind. Now what does that M. Centenac say?"
Roger stared at me. "You mean to say," I cried, "you
haven't been in touch with him since your father's vanish-
ing act?"
"Of course not. We don't want any of his business friends
to know of it. If they did, the fat would be in the fire. Out
of the question, Jasper."
"So all you have been doing, you damned young fool, is
sitting here on your fat arse and doing nothing?" He glared
at me. "All right," I said, getting up, "I'm taking the night
train back. I'm not going to waste my bloody time here withyou." That frightened him. With visible difficulty he swal-
lowed his pride. "You'll do what I tell you to do."
"What is it?"
"First ring up M. Centenac and tell him we're coming to see
him right now." He said that couldn't be done you can't
disturb people, especially important high bourgeois people
like the Centenacs, so near to dinner. It would have to wait
till morning; moreover he, Roger, wasn't good in French on
the telephone. "What's his number?" I asked. "Get it for
me." Roger didn't move. "In that case I'd better ask the
police to interview him, as he might throw light on your fa-
ther's movements on that day."
The telephone number was produced. I rang up; the unmis-
takable voice of a maid answered. I asked for M. Centenac,
saying it was concerning M. Farrell, and M. Centenac came
to the telephone. I was watching Roger as an animal trainer
watches an unruly tiger, while I explained that I was the son-
in-law, and as it was a matter of considerably urgency, a mat-
ter that concerned Francis Farrell, his son and I simply had
to see him right then. If it was inconvenient to go to him,
would he come to the hotel? Being a Bordelais paterfamilias
and a man of immaculate financial standing, he immediately
chose to come to the hotel. "He's coming here," I said to
Roger. He seemed surprised. "The provincial rich receive
only once a year," I explained. "This is the wrong time of
the year."
"What will you say to him?"
"You'll hear. Learn one thing: if one's in a mess, then one
has to take others into one's confidence."
"You mean you're going to tell him that my father dis-
appeared?'"
"If necessary, yes."
M. Centenac was a pallid, thin man who bit his nails. He
wasn't my idea of a substantial wine shipper. He looked more
like an unsuccessful poet with uncontrolled visions. We of-
fered him whisky; he said: "Non, pas de whisky." Even-
tually he expressed a desire for an Ambassadeur "si vous
l'avez." Roger telephoned down for a bottle of Ambassadeur.He drank only one small glass, and the bottle remained with
us for the rest of our stay in Bordeaux. I had decided to tell
him the truth. As a poet he would understand. Poor Roger
looked miserable and blushed while I told Centenac as casually
as possible that his business connection had vanished. "Very
astonishing," was his comment. Then because he sensed that
more was expected from him, he added: "I had an uncle who
disappeared. That was long before the war. We heard rumors
that he had gone to live with a native woman in Indo-China,
but we never succeeded in finding out the truth."
"It's very unlikely that Farrell should have gone to live
with a native woman in Indo-China.
""
"Indo-China as such doesn't exist any more," said the
logical Centenac.
"That's one more reason,
I said. "Now all we want from
you is for you to go over your day with him in the light of
what we've told you. Did you notice anything strange or
suspicious about him during your outing to Bayonne?"
"Nothing whatever," he said. I made him describe their
day. They motored to Bayonne, had lunch with a M. Mack
who, Centenac impressed on us, was of English origin. They
lunched in a riverside restaurant where they had excellent
pike, among other good things which he enumerated. After
lunch Farrell wanted to go for a walk; Centenac had a brief
business appointment; they met later in a hotel where they
had some Armagnac, then drove back to Bordeaux, arriving
in time for dinner.
"Did you find him depressed on the way back?" I asked.
"He was quiet," said Centenac, "but it was quite a long
journey through the Landes." He stood up. "You will for-
give me now," he said, "but I must go and rejoin my family.
They are expecting me for dinner. I am sorry I can be of no
help to you, but may I be permitted to give a little advice?"
"Please do," we said together.
"I have known M. Farrell for years, and my late father had
business dealings with him for many years too. My father and
I, we had the highest esteem for him. Now it appears M.
Farrell has gone away or, as you say, disappeared. I believe,"he said, raising his voice, "that a man who worked hard all
his life, who carried on a substantial imposing business, who
reared a family, is entitled to go his way; and my little advice,
messieurs, is please molest him not, make no effort to find him,
but let him lead the life he wants to lead now. I wish you
good-night."
He bowed, as they say, from the waist down, and left. "Exit
Centenac," I said. Roger said nothing. "The pompous ass,"
I said. "But look here, Roger, perhaps he's right. Your
father might have gone off to start a new life in some soft
valley or on a laughing hillside because he's had too much
of it."
"Do you suppose for a moment that my father would do
any of the things that stupid chap had in mind?”
"Like an Indo-Chinese girl? Never." I thought a bit. "I
wish I knew what happened or what entered his mind in that
hotel in Bayonne."
"What do you mean, Jasper?''
"If we knew, we'd know more than we know now, and
now we know nothing. The next move is the police."
"You can't," he said, going pale. "Not that, you can't."
"He could have had an accident." I didn't want to say
more than that as we were both thinking of the same thing.
"So we could-or I could go to the police and simply inquire
about accidents: that is, tell them we've been waiting for a
certain chap who hasn't turned up yet. But in that case you'd
better burn your father's note."
"Why?"
"Because, dear boy, we're behaving quite illegally if we
don't tell the police the truth we suspect."
"We suspect nothing," said Roger.
"Not at the moment. Officially not, I mean, but sooner or
later, Roger, you'll have to get over your shyness, because if
something has happened to your father then it will come out.
Fear of publicity should be your last consideration."
"Not yet-we might hear from him. I beg you, Jasper,
don't go to the police yet. Please let's wait till morning. We
might have news of him before then. Imagine what fools wewould make of ourselves, and what Uncle Matthew and the
rest of the family would say if we started a hue and cry and
then my father turned up. It would damage him. He's prob-
ably somewhere quite near here just having a rest."
"You know you don't believe a word you say, but I'm will-
ing to wait till the morning. Now to my sausages and plenty
of wine."
The wide glass-covered veranda downstairs looked exactly
as it had looked twelve years ago when on a cold, rainy eve-
ning I sat in there with a Welsh girl who aspired to becoming
a painter. "If we only had a few pennies," she said, “we
could eat pork tonight." She left my life ages ago, yet be-
cause of that one sentence she became for a few seconds strik-
ingly alive.
"We'll take a taxi," said Roger.
"In a taxi I could never find that restaurant," I said.
He followed me into the wet street. The west wind was
pouring into the town the rain of the clouds it brought along.
But I did find the restaurant and the sausages too. "What a
nice little place this is," said Roger, who was trying to humor
me.
"Does your father know anybody in Bayonne?" I asked.
"I don't think so. Perhaps some people connected with our
firm."
"Now let me think. There's Biarritz. Does he know any-
body in Biarritz?" Roger said he didn't know. "St. Jean de
Luz?" Roger's answer was the same. "Hendaye?" Roger
shook his head. "So he knows nobody in Bayonne, yet some-
thing happened to him in Bayonne, or for some reason or
other Bayonne gave him some idea. Can't you throw any light
on it? You were roughly his shadow all your life."
"I can't, Jasper; I wish I could. I'd give anything in the
world to have him back. Anything."
That reminded me of my meeting the girl Virginia. I didn't
mention it: poor fellow, he had enough to think of. The rain
saw us back to the Splendide. We both raced to the concierge's
desk. Nobody had telephoned and there was no telegram
either.At eight in the morning Roger telephoned their house-
keeper in London. It was at my suggestion. "But if there is
a letter from my father, I can't ask her to open it," he said.
With praiseworthy patience I told him that in that case the
housekeeper could take the letter to Rosie who, being also a
Farrell, could open it and telephone the contents through.
The housekeeper had no letters in Mr. Farrell's handwriting.
I took the receiver from Roger. "This is Mr. Danesbridge
speaking," I said. "Mr. Farrell thinks that Mr. Roger is
already back in London. That's the whole point, you see. He
himself is in Paris, so if he telephones, please tell him we are
still here, and if there's a letter then take it to my wife." The
woman said I could rely on her.
"You'd have made her suspicious the way you spoke," I
said. "Now I'm going to the police. Don't come with me or
you'll make the police suspicious too. What are you going to
do? Sit here looking at that bottle of Ambassadeur the whole
day?"
"If he telephones or there's news of him..." said Roger. I
said all right, he should stay in till I came back. I wasn't
loath to find myself alone in the street. The sun, in order to
recompense me, came out and dodged the running clouds. I
took a glass of marc on my way to the police, where after a
short wait I was introduced into the presence of the commis-
saire himself and offered a chair.
I had once spent half a dozen mornings at the commissariat
of the XVIIIe in Paris. A Canadian friend of mine had been
robbed in one of those picturesque higgledy-piggledy streets
of Montmartre, then dragged into a doorway, undressed-
even his shoes were taken from him and naked, poor crea-
ture, was pushed into the street. A policeman took him in,
and he was charged under some Act dealing with public de-
cency. I got him off. Consequently I had no difficulty in
telling the commissaire in Bordeaux no more than I wanted to
impart. He knew of no accident in which an Englishman was
involved in the last few days. He telephoned his colleagues,
and the long and the short of it was that if Farrell committed
suicide he hadn't committed it in Bordeaux or its neighbor-hood. "Monsieur," said the commissaire, when he finished
with the telephone, "your frierd who you say has been de-
layed and causes you anxiety is probably detained for some
obvious reason. You said he was to come from Paris." I
bowed. "Paris is very likely the answer for the delay. You
English are so fond of Paris. Naturally your friend can stay
there or in fact anywhere else in France as long as," he gave
me a piercing look, "his papers are in order. Good-by. I hope
he'll turn up soon." He escorted me to the door.
In a nearby bistro I had another marc. So Farrell hadn't
killed himself in Bordeaux. But he might have in Angoulême.
While I was with the commissaire I had already noticed that
my interest lay, so to speak, elsewhere. The hour Farrell spent
alone in the Bayonne hotel had been preying on my mind since
last night, though I couldn't explain why. The bistro had a
telephone. I looked up Centenac's telephone number, asked
for change and rang him up at his office. After a lot of "ne
quittez pas" I got through to him. His voice implied that he
hoped he had heard the last of us. I said all I asked for was
the name of the hotel in Bayonne where he and Farrell had
drunk an Armagnac. He gave it and rang off while I thanked
him. Still, I was lucky to have got it at all.
"I have a hunch," I told Roger, whom I found glued to the
hotel, 'and it isn't even a hunch yet. I want to go to Bayonne
by the next train. It's probably going to be a wild goose
chase, but we're gloomy enough, so you'll have a good laugh
at least when I come back. But I need money. I've spent my
little nest-egg on the Pullman luncheon yesterday."
If you mentioned money-that is, the lack of it-the Far-
rells became anxious. He produced enough new bills from his
wallet to see me to the South Pole. I took half of them. Roger
was so relieved by my police news that he had a whisky with
me in the suite, then saw me off at the station. He never
asked me what my hunch was. The fact that somebody was
moving even if nothing moved was sufficient for him. I would
be back from Bayonne by nine in the evening.
The Landes I found boring; the bar of the train less so;
and when I got off at Bayonne station and looked at the steepgrass-covered hill on the other side of the track, I became
aware that if I had had a hunch it had deserted me. It was
slightly warmer in Bayonne. Not having been to the place
before, I took a taxi, which whisked me over a bridge with a
river flowing underneath-presumably the Nive-and dropped
me in front of the hotel. I could have walked it in ten minutes.
I sailed up to the reception desk and asked for M. Farrell.
They consulted a list, asked me to repeat the name. I said
Farrell, then spelled it; they said nobody of that name was
staying in the hotel. I hadn't a clue left. "Could I have a
drink here in the lobby?" I asked. "He might be late." I was
assured that I could have a drink.

It was like any smallish hotel lobby, with its couple of
potted palms and an old American couple whispering in a
corner. Yet in this very lobby something happened to Farrell.
The lobby seemed the sort of lobby where nothing ever hap-
pened. Perhaps the something happened in his mind. Roger
was more or less certain that he knew nobody in Bayonne;
therefore it must have been some thought, some notion. I
looked around carefully. The place inspired no thought, no
notion. Only four tables were in the lobby. I took my glass
and moved to the table nearest the door. I sat facing the re-
ception desk. Beside the reception desk a big poster was
stuck to the wall. The caption read: "Visitez l'Espagne." If
Farrell sat here, he would have been facing that poster. If he
sat at any of the other tables, he would have faced practically
nothing. I stood up and strolled up to the poster.
In the middle was a view of San Sebastian, to the right the
beach of Fuenterrabía, and to the left a Roman basilica, which
looked a little out of place on a Spanish tourist poster. I
looked closer. It appeared that it was the basilica of Loyola,
where, so the poster informed me, St. Ignatius was born; and
attached to the basilica was a Jesuit sanctuary and seminary.
The poster added some more information, but I was too ex-
cited to read it. Of course, of course, I said jubilantly to my-
self, how natural. Farrell, like his son, had been brought upby the Jesuits; Loyola was a sort of Mecca for the Jesuits.
In that chair Farrell had been sitting, the poster attracted
his attention, he went up to it, and I knew Farrell well
enough to guess the rest. It had been a sign; his religious
side responded with fervor. In all likelihood he thought that
poster had been put in his way by the hand of God, and with
his emotions all agog, he rushed off to Loyola. Steady, I
warned myself. After all he hadn't rushed off to Loyola:
he let himself be driven back to Bordeaux. Never mind, I
reassured myself, that journey back was only an interlude
between his fervor and Loyola. The man is in Loyola in the
hospice. If he was there, then he hadn't killed himself. I was
relieved, and thinking of Rosie and the relief in store for her,
I promised St. Ignatius right there to send a donation to
Farm Street if I found Farrell in his birthplace.
"How do I get to Loyola?" I asked the concierge, my right
thumb pointing at the poster.
"You must go, I imagine, first to Spain, monsieur," said the
concierge, with that ignorance amounting to disdain that the
French display when other parts of the earth are mentioned.
"How do I get the quickest to Spain?" I asked.
"By train to Hendaye. There's a train in twenty minutes."
"And then?"
"Monsieur will ask there. Shall I get a taxi to the station?"
The train didn't take long in getting me to Hendaye, but in
the train doubts heavy enough to slow down the train assailed
me. Why should he have gone to Loyola? What did I know of
a Catholic's reactions to a poster showing the basilica of
Loyola? I didn't even know whether one was allowed into the
monastery there. Outsiders and ignoramuses always exag-
gerate. His body was probably at that very moment washed
ashore somewhere north of Bordeaux. And why shouldn't he
have told Roger of his intention of disappearing into a Span-
ish monastery? Still, I consoled myself, nobody would have
to know of my trip, so the laugh wouldn't be on me. The
train stopped in Hendaye. I saw that poster again. I had a
good mind to turn straight back."At what time is the next train to Spain?" I inquired.
"Not before nine this evening," was the answer.
"What?" I exclaimed.
"Choses d'Espagne," said the French railway porter, shrug-
ging his shoulders, "but if you take a taxi you can be there
in five minutes. Irun, you know."
Actually it was fifteen minutes. Though my driver was
Spanish he hadn't the vaguest notion how one got to Loyola.
Perhaps he hadn't heard of the place before. In Irun he ad-
vised me to stay in his taxi and go straight on to San Seb-
astian. In San Sebastian they would know. In San Sebastian
he pulled up before a bar. "Come in," he said, "here we'll
find out about Loyola." As we made our entry into the bar,
I realized we were in Spain. Admittedly I couldn't fail to
notice the Spanish customs guard on the bridge, which my
driver proudly referred to as the International Bridge, nor
did my eyes overlook some somber fellows wearing three-
cornered hats, but Spain as such hit me only in the bar. I felt
a little panicky. There I was in an unknown country spoken
to in a strange language, and my sole excuse for being there
was a quest with no foundation. The bartender and the cus-
tomers all had gold teeth.
"What does he say?" I asked from time to time. When they
finished jabbering and pointing at me, my driver, whose
French had become worse in his native land, gave me the
résumé of their argument. Loyola did exist. I was glad to
hear that. Loyola was a mile or so from a town called Azpeitia,
in fact it was known as Loyola-Azpeitia. "How do I get
there?" I asked. Jabbering and pointing at me started again.
Now they were joined by two of the customers. There were,
I observed, no women in the bar. Indisputably I was in Spain.
"There is a coach to Loyola-I mean Azpeitia," said my
driver.
"I'll take that coach. When does it leave?"
He translated my words to the meeting; opinions were
voiced; no vote was taken. Eventually he said: "Coach very
full with many peasants. You'd better go by taxi." I sug-gested he should take me on to Loyola. I wished I hadn't come.
"It can't be done," he said. "I wish I could but it can't be
done. I'm an International Bridge chauffeur, but I can recom-
mend you a chauffeur of confidence who will take you there.
I invite you to have drinks with me while I go and find that
chauffeur of real confidence."
Another argument ensued. I gathered every one of those
present wanted to pay for my drinks. I had a couple of
sherries, remembered I hadn't lunched, felt I could do with-
out dinner too. A customer tried out his English on me; we
didn't succeed in making contact. The driver returned,
bringing with him the driver of confidence, who couldn't
speak a word of French. "You don't worry," said the first
driver, 'he takes you to Loyola. You'll be there in time for
dinner. We'll have one more drink, then I'm returning to
the International Bridge."
I wrote out a telegram and asked him to post it in Hendaye.
It was to tell Roger I wouldn't be back before tomorrow. The
driver and I shook hands and I didn't give the telegram an-
other thought. I knew it would be dispatched, and it was.
The chauffeur of confidence was a quiet Basque who through-
out our journey silently nursed the Basques' grievance
against France and Spain. Of the countryside I saw nothing.
It was pitch dark and the rain never ceased. There wasn't
much traffic on the road; the places we motored through were
badly lit. Don Quixote's shadow brooded in the darkness as
invisible as the countryside. After an hour or so we crossed
a bridge, which was certainly not an international bridge. The
few street lights failed to relieve the night. "Azpeitia," said
the driver.
"Loyola?" I said, pointing at the windshield.
"Loyola," he said.
We were back in complete blackness, but this time it didn't
last long. He pulled up before a building. "Hotel," he said.
"Loyola?"
"Loyola."
I was in Loyola.
The hotel looked and was dismal; the proprietor and hiswife or his sister (because they had the same sad features)
spoke some French. The first thing they told me was that my
driver wanted to get back as soon as possible to San Sebastian.
"How am I going to get back tomorrow morning?" I asked. I
wanted to say late tonight but didn't dare to. Their eyes said:
you are sleeping here tonight and no nonsense.
"You'll take a bus at noon if you want to go back," said the
wife or sister. "Surely you don't want to go back so soon.
There's so much to see in Loyola. We're quite near to the
basilica." The driver was paid. He spoke to them, pointing at
me; they explained that he wanted to buy me a drink before
hitting the high road. We adjourned to the bar next door,
and I had a drink of the land called Izarra which was a bit
sickly. The driver and I wrung each other's hands, and I was
left in the bar. A few peasants and garage mechanics, to
judge by their overalls, were shouting in Basque.
"Can one go to the Jesuits now?" I asked the proprietor.
"It's too late now," he said. The woman who I gathered
was his sister exclaimed: "How very astonishing. This will
be the second Englishman this week who wants to go to the
hospice."
My heart thundered with such force that I thought I would
break out in a rash, or perhaps my beard would drop off.
"Another Englishman," I said. "Who was the other English-
man?"
The Basques are known for being suspicious. "An Eng-
lishman," said the sister.
"Is he still here?" I asked. "As you know, one is always
keen on meeting one's fellow countrymen in foreign parts,
especially if one is an Englishman, but I am sure it's the
same with you Basques. Will you have a drink with me? I
should like that so much."
That broke the ice sufficiently for them to tell me that the
Englishman had been to the hotel but was no longer in the
hotel. A shot in the dark, old boy, I advised myself. Fire, I
added. "Was his name Farrell?" I asked. They said nothing.
"A friend of mine," I went on, "an Englishman, told me in
London the other day," I said London so as to make it soundplausible, 'that he intended to come to the Jesuits in Loyola.
As I am passing through, I don't want to miss the opportunity
of having news of him, perhaps even seeing him."
"You couldn't see him tonight at the Jesuits, that's cer-
tain," said the brother.
"Besides, he mightn't be the same Englishman," said the
sister.
"That can be cleared up," I said. "Is his name Farrell?
My friend, you see, is called Farrell."
Brother said to sister: "Look up the register." That was
a point gained. She went to the hotel next door. The bar
was a separate building.
"I take it very few people stay here in winter," I said
chattily to the brother.
"We have plenty of weddings to keep us busy," he said.
"Oh, weddings," I said. "Very interesting." He looked
like an undersized horse.
"Basque people like coming to Loyola for their weddings,"
he said. "Basque people fancy getting married in the basilica
of St. Ignatius."
"Is this the name?" asked the sister, bringing in the reg-
ister.
It was. There in Farrell's handwriting was Francis Farrell,
date of birth, nationality and the works. "Yes, it is," I said,
strangely deflated. It had somehow been too easy. The golden
fleece had practically come to the Argonaut. "So he's in the
hospice," I said. They said he was. "For good?" I asked.
They stared at me, then at each other.
"A retreat," the sister explained. "Many people come
here to our hospice for a retreat."
"He was lucky to have got in," said the brother.
"It was lucky for him that you know the guestmaster," said
the sister. "First we thought he couldn't get in, but my
brother spoke to the guestmaster and as it happened a guest
cell was available."
"I was under the impression," I said, "that he wished to
become a monk."
"Well, it has happened that a man went in for a retreat andthen decided to become a postulant," said the brother.
"Fancy your friend wanting to become a lay brother. Mind
you, he was a very quiet, well-behaved gentleman."
"There was a case," said the sister, "but I don't remember
whether it happened here or over there in the Benedictine Ab-
bey." As she said over there, she pointed to the left. "A man
arrived and stayed the night in the monastery, then next
morning said he wanted to become a postulant. He was of an
advanced age, I believe, and almost deaf. The abbot or who-
ever it was said they could find no use for a man who was
practically deaf, so the guestmaster told the man it was out,
but if he wanted to, he could stay for a few days in a guest
cell. He was in fact taken to the guest cell. But, being deaf,
he didn't understand a word that was said to him, and in the
morning he turned up in the kitchen and started to help the
others; in the evening he went with the brothers and the
novices to say their office, and he worked very well and stayed
on, and became a brother, and the person who told us the
story said he was one of the most pious and hardest working
monks there ever was."
"I don't think it happened here," said the brother. "It
must have happened over there." He, for a change, pointed to
the right.
"At what time does one dine here?" I asked. Any time,
they informed me, after nine o'clock. I went into the dining
room at a quarter past nine. I was alone. The food, I sus-
pected, was the leftovers of a wedding. There was hardly a
thought in my mind. I went up to my room straight after
dinner. It could easily have been a cell. There was a crucifix
in it too. "I've found him," I told the whitewashed wall. "I
have found him because I'm extremely clever. Rosie will be
proud of me." It had no effect.
Before turning in I went through my pockets and behold!
out came a little picture of a crucifix. It said in English: "The
Crucifix that spoke to St. Francis of Assisi." I knew that
picture because I had seen it in Rosie's missal. St. Francis was
Rosie's great special devotion next to the Little Flower. Her
devotion to those two saints in a way explained Rosie's charac-ter. I lay down on the hard bed, and as sleep approached I
wondered whether it was her St. Francis who guided me to
St. Ignatius' town; but I had to confess my ignorance con-
cerning saints' relation to one other.
In the morning I rushed to the window. There is nothing
like seeing an unknown place first thing in the morning.
Majestic and somberly Roman baroque, the basilica stood
nearby under the heavy clouds. An avenue started, as it were,
from the basilica's steps. At breakfast the sister entertained
me with tales of weddings and tales of woe of high taxation.
The brother buttonholed me on my way out. He had an ag-
gravating manner; even his blinking eyelids proclaimed his
high esteem of himself. "Now it is not for me to give you
advice," he said. Before I could wholeheartedly agree with
him, he said: "Whether your friend is in there just for a re-
treat or for more lasting and solemn purposes, I don't think
he would like to be disturbed by anybody from the outside
world. But you know best."
"I certainly do," I said.
Under the dripping sky solemnity brooded. The trees of the
garden walk were as solemn as the kiosk selling sweets and
devotional pictures. It was a landscape that belonged to the
reign of silence. I was completely out of my depth. Two
Jesuits came down the avenue chatting animatedly, yet I was
convinced that no words left their lips. A coachload of people
arrived: parties to a wedding. They were quiet, and but for
the white dress of the bride they could have been attending a
funeral. In a tight group they went into the hotel, with a
photographer carrying an old-fashioned camera slinking in
behind them. I entered the basilica, which was imposingly
baroque inside too and smelled of stale incense. A man in a
black cassock, a sacristan or lay brother-I wouldn't know-
waddled up to me and said something. I asked him in pidgin
French if he understood French. He smiled, then pointed at
a huge crucifix. A few women were kneeling under the vast
cupola. I left.
A tight group of men appeared from nowhere and walked
into the basilica. A little later a similar group materialized.It was as though there were some cleverly camouflaged under-
ground station from which those groups erupted. By then I
had walked the length of the sanctuary's wall, also of the big
building. Rain fell thoughtfully. In that building was Far-
rell, and there on top of a few steps was a door with a bell.
Twice I mounted the steps to ring the bell: twice I desisted.
I retraced my steps to the hotel bar. The brother was behind
the counter. One of those groups of men I had seen sat
around, most men silent, all of them subdued. A few of them
were scribbling postcards and those who spoke, spoke in
whispers. They were middle-aged men, and you could see
that though they were knit together in a sense, they were
little acquainted with each other. When all the postcards were
written and the coffees drunk, they rose from their tables and
in a close group marched out of the bar. My eyes followed
them to the hospice.
"Businessmen from Bilbao, I think," said the brother.
"Come for a retreat. Now you'll understand why one shouldn't
disturb them when they are in. You haven't tried to com-
municate with your friend?''
"I don't know yet. After all I came here to see him. Tell
me, what do they do during retreat?"
He thought for a while. "They pray," he said, "and there
are spiritual exercises too; and they don't speak to each other,
only to the fathers, but only about spiritual matters and
matters of conscience. I've never been to one. We're too busy
here."
"But my friend doesn't speak Spanish.”
"Oh, yes, he does." I stared at him open-mouthed. "He
speaks it very well, though with a South American accent.
He told us that as a young man he spent some years in the
Argentine."
My jealousy left me. The firm of Farrell & Sons probably
sold hundreds of threshing machines to the pampas, and
young Francis was sent out there because for the moment
there were too many Farrells in London and Reading. So he
spoke Spanish, yet didn't boast of it. "I'm going out again,"
I said. "By the way, at what time does the bus leave?" Hesaid at twelve thirty. "What time will I get to France?"
Seldom had the word France sounded so sweet to me.
"You'll have to change in San Sebastian," he said.
I walked along the road; the wall of the hospice was to my
left. Perhaps it was the wall of the seminary. A polluted
rivulet ran beside the road. A Basque peasant came along
holding an umbrella, leading a cow. It wouldn't be fair, it
seemed to me now, to disturb him. If he had wanted to go
only for a retreat he wouldn't have taken French leave and
would have communicated with Roger before entering the
hospice. It seemed to me too that I hadn't been far wrong
when I thought he had killed himself. To kill oneself is the
materialist's answer to leaving the world. To enter a mon-
astery is its spiritual equivalent. Being very much of the
world, the first possibility came logically first to me; yet my
unconscious mind accepted the other possibility too: otherwise
I wouldn't have come to Loyola. What it amounted to was
that my arguments were right; the means alone were dif-
ferent. Life had become too complicated: life had to be sur-
mounted. To Farrell it wasn't a bridge or pills: it was a
monastery wall to shield him from the world. I turned around.
There was only one thing left to do; namely, not to miss the
bus. The icy rain waited till I reached the bar, then it obliter-
ated from view the basilica too. "I like your weather," I said
to the sister who had taken over from the brother.
"It is a very good climate," she said. "We seldom have any
snow here."
While I waited for the bus I thought of all the others who
were involved in the peace Farrell was seeking and which I
sincerely hoped he would find. Hilda: she would probably
have more generous thoughts of him. Roger: he would miss
him, but after a while Virginia would find no more flaws in
him. Rosie: my dear Rosie would be truly happy. Jean? That
was more difficult, so I drank some Spanish brandy before
answering my question. That brandy was helpful, for it made
me see with surprising clarity that Jean wouldn't mind very
much. She would cherish his memory first with tears, then
with tender smiles; then another man would take Farrell's
place. Jean was a woman made for man, man in the flesh,man in her arms: those arms would become useless if they
remained empty for long. Another brandy brought tears into
my eyes, and since they were all strangers in the bar I didn't
bother to wipe them off. They dried.
The donation, I decided, would be split in two. One half to
St. Ignatius, the other to St. Francis. "The bus will be pass-
ing through in about five minutes," said the sister. "One of
the maids is outside to stop it for you."
I paid and left, and brother and sister forgot me before I
closed the door. Never mind, everything had turned out beau-
tifully. In the bus I had visions of Brother Francis walking in
procession singing Beethoven's Miserere and the smoke of in-
cense hiding him from the world. There were umpteen peasants
and peasant women in the bus, with a sprinkling of doleful
countenances of commercial travelers. The rain kept the
landscape from me. In San Sebastian it didn't rain.
I said to the bus driver as I alighted: "Hendaye?"
He thought that over, then said: "Ah, Hendaya?” I
nodded vigorously. "Irun," he said.
“Si, si, si, Irun," I said. A little longer in Spain and I
would speak Spanish as fluently as Farrell and without a
South American accent. The conductor said something in
Spanish. I didn't understand him.
"Autobus," he said slowly, "para Irun aqui en un mто-
mento."
"Ha ha," I said, “un momento, si, si, si." As a proof of my
knowledge of Spanish, a bus saying IRUN stopped behind us.
I got in, the other driver got in too to shake my hand, and
within a surprisingly short time I was deposited near Irun
station. At the station I found a man who spoke French.
Resting on my Spanish laurels, I asked him when the next
train left for Hendaye. Not before the evening, but if I
wanted he would get me a taxi. The moment I crossed the
International Bridge, Spain, especially Loyola, turned into a
dream I hadn't dreamed but invented.
The first thing I did in Hendaye was to telephone Rosie.
"Oh, I'm glad, so very glad," she said, and I feared she might
cry into the receiver.
The next telephone call was to Bordeaux. Naturally Rogerwas in the suite. I told him his father was with the Jesuits
in Loyola. After a silence he asked me how I knew. When I
finished explaining, he said: "Shouldn't I go there?" I gave
him the reasons why he shouldn't. I was eloquent. "I'm glad,
so glad for his sake," said Roger. "Jasper, it's really won-
derful." Then after a pause: "What am I going to say to
Uncle Matthew?"
"That's your problem," I said. "I for one am taking the
first train to Paris, then going straight back to London.
Mission accomplished. I'll mail you the money that's left to
the office. There's plenty." I rang off.
In Paris it was snowing: the snow was warmer than the rain
of Loyola. I didn't go to my old haunts: I wasn't in the mood
for disillusion. So I went to Saint-Germain and into the Flore.
In a booth side by side sat Fred and Moura. "Geoffrey will be
here in a minute," said Fred.Chapter Five

Twelve days after my return from Spain, a country on which
I now considered myself an expert, nanny put her head into
the dining room. "I'm very busy," I said, and stopped draw-
ing apocalyptic figures on the sheet of paper that should have
been filled with words that made sense. It was late afternoon;
Rosie was out.
"Mr. Farrell on the phone for you," said nanny.
"You mean Mr. Roger?" She said no, it was Mr. Farrell
himself. "From Spain?" I asked, rising.
"Not that I know of," she said.
I pushed past her and went to the telephone in the living
room. Spread out in an armchair, Smoke growled at me. 'My
entrance disturbed his sleep. "Shut up," I said, and lifted
the receiver thinking: stupid nanny. "What is it, Roger?"
I asked.
"It isn't Roger," said Farrell's unmistakable voice, "it's
me, Francis. I got back last night. Roger told me you went
to look for me in Loyola. Jasper, you're wonderful, really
wonderful."
"Go to hell and stay there," I said, and banged down the
receiver.If trembling is a sign of anger, then I was angry in the
extreme. I even laughed in anger. The damned man had shat-
tered more than an illusion; for he had made me believe in
something that I could lift above my sneers, that almost made
me feel a better man. "You fool," I said, meaning strictly
myself. The telephone rang again: I unhooked the receiver
and returned to the dining room. There would be no work
today. Do not disturb the man who has gone to God; let him
be in a world which is not of the world; to the sound of the
organ with bowed head, his hands clasping his rosary-that
was the picture Rosie painted to me the monk was kneeling
before the crucified Christ in the basilica. It was all stuff and
nonsense. Fundamentally, I reflected, only agnostics like my-
self are ever bamboozled. I threw the sheet of paper into the
wastepaper basket and decided to go out.
"Mr. Danesbridge is in here," said nanny, opening and
holding the door open for Farrell. I pushed out my beard
aggressively; nanny closed the door.
"Jasper," said Farrell, putting a hand on my shoulder,
"you shouldn't get annoyed. I can guess what goes on in your
mind. You're wrong. I came here to explain. May I sit
down?" He sat down at the other end of the table. I re-
mained standing. He looked exactly as he had looked when he
left for Bordeaux.
"Before you start on your explanation," I said, "I'm going
to get myself a drink. Do you want one?" He said no thank
you. I found some sherry. "Good Spanish drink, this," I
said. "The right drink to toast a Spanish-speaking monk."
He laughed. That infuriated me. "Now I know where I stand
with you," I said. "You're just a windbag. But I admire
you for having a sense of drama. Writing a good-by letter to
Roger, vanishing in the night, disappearing behind a monas-
tery wall, making one rush to and fro, then calmly coming
back and 'As I was saying' starting where you left off. Not
for me, old boy. I'll have some more of this Spanish drink.
Did you bring me any Izarra? I found it rather syrupy."
"I'm afraid I didn't," he said, "but I believe one can get
it in London. I'll find out from my wine merchant.""Get it from your wine merchant and then I'll tell you
what you can do with it." He rose and came up to me. I
hadn't noticed before that comparatively he wasn't a really
tall man. Dapper was the descriptive word for him.
"I'm going to tell you what happened," he said, "and then
you'll be able to judge whether or not I indulge in histrionics,

don't deserve it."
I sat down: now it was he who remained standing. “Hon-

image; it was a fine one. You shattered it. I don't care a rap
about the debris."
"I'm sorry," he said, "but there was no question of an
image. Now listen to me, Jasper."
It would have been difficult not to listen. I softened a little
when he related his hour in the Bayonne hotel. He had been
bored by Centenac and Mack and they gave him too much to
eat and to drink. In the hotel lobby he was deadly bored;
then, looking up from his armchair, he saw the poster saying
Visitez l'Espagne.
"You sat at the table next to the entrance door," I inter-
jected.
"You sat there too?" he asked. I nodded. "And so you
guessed?" I nodded again. "Only a man with your brain and
imagination could have guessed it," he said. This time I bowed.
Centenac found him still before the poster. (So the busi-
nessman who makes in five minutes probably more money than
I make in a year guessed nothing, observed nothing.) The
notion of going to Loyola, the town where St. Ignatius was
born, the seat of the Society that had educated him, became

age-he twice repeated the word courage to tell Roger.
"Roger and I are so very near to each other," he said, "and
he'd have guessed that I had more than the idea of a retreat
in mind. We'd have had a row."
"So you left a stupid note that could have implied that you
contemplated suicide," I said.
He said he was in a hurry when he scribbled the note. Allhe wanted was for Roger not to know where he had gone.
"And frankly I wasn't giving that note much attention," he
said. "My mind was completely elsewhere."
Also he feared there might be no room in the hospice; in
fact, the guestmaster suggested he should come back next
month for a retreat. The people in the hotel espoused his
cause and he was given a guest cell. "Answer me one ques-

it your intention to stay there and become a brother or some-
thing?"
"I'll come to that presently," he said, "but don't imagine
one simply walks into a monastery and says: here I am,
