say of them. "I have," said Farrell, "a painting of the
Forum by Jolli, Neapolitan painter: rather the Pannini school,
but quite pretty. I'll give it to you. Ideal for a dining room."
"Give it to Rosie. Here, as you know, everything belongs
to her. I don't care for property of any sort."
"How wise you are," he said, and continued his story. He
went in as a guest but with the secret hope that his stay with
the Jesuits would be the beginning of a life dedicated to God.
"There were about fourteen of us guests," he said. "You
weren't allowed to talk to each other, only to the fathers if
you had some spiritual problem or doubt. The silence was no
hardship for me: those people were total strangers; but think
of the others. When I came out I found out that two who were
with me were brothers, yet they never said a word to each
other in there."
"I saw a number of frightened middle-aged men, fright-
ened like schoolboys, go in on the day I was there."
"So you were there on that day? I remember that day be-
cause of those chaps coming in. The other days were no en-
tities... I think I'll have a drink after all." I poured it out,
then he forgot to drink it. "Naturally, I needed plenty of
spiritual guidance," he went on. "I put my problems to two
of the fathers. They said, to put it simply, monastic lifewasn't for me under the circumstances." He bowed his head:
I thought he would burst into tears. "My problems are in the
world and not out of it." He looked at me: I looked slightly
away in order not to meet his eyes. "Going to God shouldn't
be running from the world. It is a positive, definite choice.
With my commitments and problems I couldn't make such a
choice. If I'd stayed in I'd have been a fugitive from my
conscience." He burst into tears, which he had no business
to do before me.
"Cut that out, Francis," I said, then because he was still
crying I said: "Come, come," a little more gently.
"Forgive me," he said, and started blowing his nose. When
he finished he said: "And I knew it wasn't for me. I'm not
good enough for it." I asked him what the old Jesuits advised
him to do. "To try to have a reconciliation with my wife,"
he said with a wry smile.
"Ha, ha," I said, "and you want me to go and talk to
Hilda. Not on your life, old boy."
"I hadn't thought of that," he said, and laughed. That was
a relief. "I don't think she would be willing, but there in
Loyola I thought she might be."
"And where would Jean come in? Would she remain your
mistress? I'm asking because I never had any complications
in my life. But, you see, Francis, I have no conscience."
"Of course you have," he said. "Like the whistling of a
gentle air," he added in a low voice.
"What's that?" I asked.
In the same low voice he replied: "The voice of God."
"What about Jean?" I asked.
"I left the sanctuary resolved to do two things. Firstly, to
make an honest attempt at persuading Hilda to resume mari-
tal relations; secondly, to have only a brother-and-sister rela-
tionship with Jean."

"You amuse me, I said. "Hilda wants the brother-and-

One had to admit that she was a good sniffer. "Have you seen

"Then she came near, and my resolution went out through

"And Jean?" I asked.
"Jean would accept it. The fact is I need her more than

"That bit you quoted," I asked, "about the whistling of a

else to you," he said.
"From Surtees?" I asked.
"Oh, no," he said. "I don't remember precisely, but it
goes something like this: 'renouncing your own will you do
take up the strong and bright weapons of obedience...' I'm
not certain of the rest. Now you understand why I couldn't
stay. My obedience would be neither strong nor bright and
my will is too involved."
"And who said that?”
"St. Benedict." He put his hand on my shoulder. "Thank
you for listening, Jasper. I know it all sounds like gibberish
to you." We went out into the hall. "But I'll make an hon-
est effort," he said.
"With Hilda?"
"With Hilda. Give my love to Rosemary and the baby.
See you soon."
"What does Roger say about it all?"
"I don't know; we were too pleased to see each other."Poor Virginia-or rather poor Roger, I thought, closing the

At seven in the morning I reached the conclusion that I had

In the living room the windows were open and wetness

be accompanied either by sunshine or electric lights. The

I found that astonishing at such an early hour. Luckily it
couldn't wake Rosie, as the bell was switched off in the bed-

I put down the receiver, then unhooked it; then when I

She was the healthiest person I knew. And why did she die just
now? Fred once said to me: "Doesn't being alive strike you
as strange? Look, you breathe and air comes in, you put your
foot forward and you move. That's the wonder. To be dead
isn't." Funny to be remembering Fred's words at such a
moment.
What could she have died of? I scolded myself for such a
second-rate question. A blood clot, heart failure, cerebral
hemorrhage, in short, anything. In fact she could have died
simply of dying. I felt it was time to pull myself together, so

"I couldn't sleep."

"I'm going to the bathroom." Neither of us had tea before

II
"We've always been a very united family," said Rosie's
Uncle Matthew, as we walked out of the cemetery under an
appropriately leaden sky. There were about a dozen Farrells
around. I nodded. He walked on; I waited for Father Byrd,
Rosie and Roger. Rosie had behaved admirably, broke down
in the chapel only after Father Byrd said: "If she had any
sins..." and she piped up with "none." But the person I
watched most was Farrell; for his face, even the way he stood
beside the grave, showed unmistakably true grief. While we
were in the chapel with the narrow long coffin before us, he
was at my side, his eyes glued to the coffin. It wasn't difficult
to follow his thoughts. A remarkable woman who, because of
her lack of temperament, froze a successful marriage to death;
a woman whose admirable qualities were weighed down bysheer lack of comprehension; yet a woman who never faltered
as she marched along without looking right or left. And here
was the end of the march.
The part of the cemetery set aside for Catholics was heavy
with mud. One should have come in rubber boots. Father
Byrd fell in by my side; Roger and Rosie kept together and
apart from us.
"It is very sad, sir," said Father Byrd. "She was one of
my best parishioners. Who would have expected it? I'm not
worried about her. She was a lady of lofty principles, and if
she had any sins they were caused by those principles. They
weren't wilful sins."
"Farrell said exactly the same to me," I said. "I'll miss
her, in a way. She had something."
"She came to like you very much. She often said so." We
were approaching the cemetery gate; above us the clouds
traveled fast; fast too was the rain beating into our faces.
"There's a favor I want to ask you, sir," the corpulent priest
said. "Mr. Farrell very kindly asked me to come to Orchard
House for a glass of sherry. I won't be able to. Please apolo-
gize for me. I am somewhat frightened by all the relatives."
They were frightening because of their number. At least a
dozen Farrells stood outside the cemetery gate with their cars
drawn up. The most conspicuous of the cars, and so it should
be, was Uncle Matthew's Rolls Royce, which at first glance I
mistook for the hearse. The Farrell women were soberly and
expensively dressed; the Farrell men were dressed in the same
manner. It was like the gathering of old girls and old boys of
a coeducational school that boasted the heaviest fees in the
country. "I'll tell him," I said. "He'll understand."
Most of the Farrells had some excuse or other for not driv-
ing the six miles to Orchard House. As they got into their cars
after giving Roger and Rosie soulful kisses or handshakes, and
went off in a bunch, I couldn't help imagining-my imagina-
tion being rather keyed up-a telegraph wire as thick as a sub-
marine cable from which a gaggle of fat geese flies off. Only
Uncle Matthew, another brother called Eddie and his Swed-
ish wife motored to Orchard House, Uncle Matthew takingFarrell, Roger and Rosie. I was asked to go with them but
went instead in the station wagon with Mrs. Walsh. Rosie had
wanted to bring Mary to the funeral. "I don't want mummy
just to disappear into the unknown for Mary," she had said.
Nanny and I dissuaded her. Mary would be taken to the
Requiem Mass in London.
"Well, Mrs. Walsh?" I said, getting in beside her.
"I don't know what to say," she said, starting the engine.
"Mr. Ewart, who's coming to Orchard House at noon, re-
vealed to me the most incredible state of affairs. Very dif-
ferent from what I was given to understand, Mr. Danesbridge,
don't you know."
"Incredible state of affairs?" I asked as the dead hedges
with dead wintry fields whisked by. I shuddered and longed

where in Acton, for the shops in Old Compton Street.
"Incredible indeed," said Mrs. Walsh. "Mrs. Farrell
hadn't a penny to her name." As if I could care. Nonetheless
I made what is known as sympathetic noises. "I was always
given to understand that Orchard House belonged to her. It
didn't: it belonged to Mr. Farrell, and now with her death
both the house and the income she received revert to Mr.
Farrell or the family trust; in short, there's nothing left."
I began to feel gay and relieved. "Yet she always said she
was leaving the house to your wife and the land that goes

"I honestly don't know what you expect me to say. I under-

"I suppose I can start packing my things," she said. "And

"I wouldn't worry, Mrs. Walsh. Farrell isn't the man to let

Not many men will buy houses and land, and give a handsome

"Is that generosity?" she asked, "or just keeping up ap-

ful companion and all that."
In the drawing room were the lot of them; Uncle Matthew,

Not a smile, not even Farrell smiled.
"It's a simple matter," said Mr. Ewart, coming into the

Very delicate. Good-morning." It struck me that I was in

"Eddie he has to be in the House this afternoon," said the

Mr. Vyner opened his briefcase, took from it a bunch of

"The necessity for a decision hardly arises," said Eddie.
"Quite," said Vyner.
"What about all those other Farrells?" I asked. "Shouldn't
they be here too?" I wanted to amuse myself, though I was
hurt. I couldn't care one way or the other about OrchardHouse and family trusts, yet I was hurt because with the other
Farrells my Farrell was very much one of them, almost a

"They have their own trusts," said Matthew. The Swedish

"But some of them haven't a chairman's or director's

"Sit down, Matthew; you've been standing for hours," said

took his time in depositing his imposing behind, sighed, and

"Right, Francis," he said. "Proceed, Vyner. Time is
short."
"What it all amounts to is this," said Vyner. "This prop-
erty, Orchard House, three cottages, outhouses and eleven

I had had it, as the saying goes. I had been taught a lesson :
namely to keep my trap closed when men of property bandy
property about. After my admirable performance on the day
of Hilda's funeral, the worst sort of Nemesis overtook me, like
that big panting dog who lived at the other end of the village
and who out of sheer amiability would race after me and trip
me over. I most certainly was tripped over. Thanks to my
performance I got Orchard House for Rosie, and because of
that the London flat had to be given up. For Rosie could
get no extra income, owing to assessments and trusts and
heaven knows what else.
"Say you don't want Orchard House," I pleaded with her.
"But we can't, after you got it for me," she said. There was
something in that. I even went to see a solicitor, which cost
four guineas, and all I got out of him was that nobody with
Rosie's income could keep two such establishments going.
"I know that," I bleated. "I came for advice, not for facts
which, incidentally, you got from me." He said he couldn't do

quently asked for Orchard House. The only thing to do was to
dissuade Rosie. Her income couldn't be enlarged, as it would

question, though I tried earnestly, passionately."When Roger and I listened at the door," she said, "I

by Regent's Park, three cheers rusticity.

then I got up. "Going to London?" asked Rosie, half asleep.
I said I was.
It was Mrs. Walsh's privilege to drive me to Salisbury

Rosie's insistence. Apparently you aren't supposed to wear
old double-breasted pin-striped jackets in the country.
George lumbered up to the station wagon before we left.His devotion to cider gave his ruddy face an applelike quality.

this car," I said. Mrs. Walsh made no comment. "Naturally
less and less as time goes by," I added. That loosened her
tongue, and she spoke of the late Mr. Walsh and how against
all her efforts his shade lost some of its hue every day. By now
hardly any was left. "Yet my wife doesn't want to meet her
future stepmother, or whatever is the right word."
"I think it's nanny who's put her up to it," said Mrs.
Walsh.
"No, it's still her mother. By the way, could you lend me
three quid? I think I can give it back to you when I come
back tonight, but if I can't it'll be toward the end of the week.
I'm expecting a small check."
"Most certainly," said Mrs. Walsh. "It's a pleasure. Please
take them out of my handbag, don't you know." I said it
could wait till the station. I didn't want to be as intimate as
that with her. We reached the station; she gave me the three
pounds. She said she would go and do some shopping for
Rosie, and waved to me as she drove off. I went into the
station and walked up and down the platform in the company
of eager yet forbidding businessmen in bowler hats, with the
Times under their arms. They got into first-class; I into
second.
I saw them again at Waterloo Station. They hurried; I
sauntered. I had to prove to myself for the peace of my mind
that I wasn't one of them in spite of having nowadays trains
to catch the same way as they. In the middle of spirited con-
versation in London I often had to murmur that I had a train
to catch, and dash. So without any signs of haste I traveled
down on the escalator, immobile and proud, delighted to see
them rushing past me. I went as far as to let two trains go
by. It was only at the third "Watch Your Step" that I
entered a coach. From Piccadilly Circus I sauntered to DeanStreet. At the corner of Wardour Street I was accosted by a
man. I was averse to him. He was a scrounger and was full
of the evil of those who know no remorse. He told me a tale
of woe. Half a crown changed hands. As I left him I couldn't
help but reflect that the half-crown was more self-indulgence
than charity. I reached the French pub before opening time,
so I went to do some telephoning. When I returned the pub
was already open and Fred was at the bar.
"How is Rosie?" he asked. I said she was well. "And that
dear woman?"
"You mean Mrs. Walsh?" He nodded. "You proposed to
her."
"Doesn't surprise me," he said. "It was the anguish of
loneliness."
"Do you propose a lot?"
"Whenever I feel lonely. I always feel lonely."
Moura came in, said "Hullo, squire," which I expected. She
was wearing her winter dress; the heat rose from the asphalt.
"Geoffrey won't be here before one," said Fred.
"Then I won't see him," I said, "not that I mind."
"Lunch date again?" asked Moura.
"Of course landowners always lunch in town when they
come to town." Fred rocked with laughter.
"I'll sell you some of my land,” I said.
Moura took a drink from me, and then said: "I dreamed
of you on Saturday night. That's my middle-class background.
I mean having an erotic dream on a Saturday night. We loved
each other desperately. In my dream, I mean." My alarm
watch went off. I had fixed it when she said she had a dream
of me.
"I must be going," I said. "It's a very early lunch date."
They looked unconvinced. "You'll have to come down for a
weekend, the two of you." Moura inquired why Geoffrey
couldn't come. "I'd be jealous," I said.
I killed half an hour in other pubs; even so I arrived too
early in the restaurant, which was more Cypriot than Italian.
Actually it called itself Portuguese. I sat down in the little
bar. A Portuguese mariner was painted on the wall. Twomiddle-aged women sat at the next table, groaning under
monumental hats, and talked of cookbook food. I ordered a
drink from the Irish waiter and vaguely wondered what it
would all be about. On the wall facing me a Portuguese fish-
wife sold fish. Jean came in. Her expression was serene; her
expression was always serene, so that meant nothing. I was
certain that something had gone wrong in Italy. That was why
I didn't say a word either to Rosie or Mrs. Walsh about her
having telephoned me yesterday, asking me to lunch with her
today. "Hullo, Jean." I said. "You don't look very sun-
burned."
She looked as desirable as any woman has a right to look
in a white dress with a low neck. "One doesn't lie on beaches
when in Francis' company," she said. I asked where he was.
"In Paris," she said. "He has an important business lunch
today. He's flying back tomorrow." That was better. People
were right when they said I walked too often on the shadowy
side of the street. "The day before yesterday he took me to
see a woman cousin of his who lives in Paris, " said Jean. "A
formidable woman; and having guessed at once that I couldn't
speak French, she mixed as many French words as she could
into the conversation. It made it meaningless to me."
"It was probably meaningless anyway. What was Francis'
reaction?"
"He said her French accent was as bad as his. Let's go in,
Jasper. I can't talk to you in this cramped place.”
The headwaiter persuaded us to try a special original Portu-
guese fish dish. It turned out to be sole bonne femme with more
bonne femme than sole. "As Francis' future wife," I said,
"you should have ordered caviar."
"I don't think I'm Francis' future wife," she said. So there
it was. My forebodings, alas, are seldom wrong.
"Why, Jean?" I said. "Did you have a row? Did you
quarrel? Don't upset me, please."
"It would be simpler if we had quarreled. Francis doesn't
quarrel. He's much too gentle."
"Then what happened?"
"Nothing happened.""Not even a monk?"
"Not even a monk," she smiled, "though they're nineteen
to the dozen in Italy. What it amounts to and that's why I
simply had to see you-is that we aren't happy together. It
sounds so negative, but there we are. It was much easier when
his wife was still alive, and even then it wasn't easy."
"His wife has nothing to do with it. I had a long chat with
him shortly before you left for Italy. He told me that on the
day of Hilda's burial he tried to recapture their past, bring
back the early days and so on. In front of her coffin he realized
at last that there was nothing to recapture. That made him
more miserable than her death."
"I wasn't thinking of that," said Jean. "I wish I were an
intelligent woman, then I could explain
I don't think
much of this wine." I said it was quite drinkable. It wasn't.
"He's with me without being with me," she went on. "He's
there, yet he isn't there
I wish I knew how to explain.
One moment he's with me as a man should be when he's with
his woman, the next he's so far that even if I shook him I
couldn't find him. And, Jasper, there's no gaiety in our re-
lations, none whatever. I found that out only now that I've
spent a month with him. Before there were only nights, and
that not too successful outing to France."
"The answer is easy," I said glibly. "It was a repressed
relationship. You got accustomed to it. Now because it's free
and easy, you both feel out of your element. It will take you
some time to appreciate your freedom. I wouldn't worry.
What you told me is one more reason to marry him quickly.
Then it'll all fall into place. Get the wedding fixed for tomor-
row."
Jean laughed; at the table on the right businessmen invoked
jute; and a respectable old lady at the table on the left was
trying to sell a song to an unresponsive music publisher whom
I knew by sight. The score changed hands every few minutes-
that is, she gave it to him, he gave it back to her, then the pro-
cedure was repeated. "You're like Roger," Jean said. "Roger
has set his heart on my marrying Francis, as if everything
depended on it.""Everything depends on it where Roger is concerned."
"I'm quite willing to be the sacrificial lamb," said Jean.
"I've been that in my time for far less worthy causes, but
won't I sacrifice Francis if I marry him?"
"Now we're entering again the world of silly speculations,
of rarified air and lofty thoughts with the organ pealing. I've
no time for that since the unspectacular return from Loyola."
"Loyola," she said, leaning over the table like one who is
going to impart a secret. "I'm convinced, Jasper, that when
he's so suddenly far away from me then he's communing with
God. Is communing the right word?"
"I suppose so. One can commune with God yet be a good
husband. I can't see how one interferes with the other. I re-
peat, remember Loyola."
"Then he wasn't as free as he is now, and there are men and
women who feel that wives and husbands do interfere. Other-
wise there wouldn't be monks and nuns, now would there,
Jasper?''
"Why, did he start playing the old phonograph record of
his again?"
"No, not once, but take it from me that when he's so com-
pletely far away, his thoughts aren't with agricultural ma-
chinery. Perhaps some other woman could bring him back
when he's in the clouds." I shook my head. In a way, I
thought, she deserved a less complicated man. "Of course
he'll do with me what he wants," she went on. "I've never
been really lucky in my love affairs, but from him I get con-
sideration and kindness. I'm utterly earthy of the earth, so
it must be difficult and irritating for him to watch me from
his height. I honestly don't know what to do and you're not
very helpful, Jasper."
The score lay in a sort of no-man's-land between the vener-
able lady and the music publisher, neither of them picking it
up any more. "I'm not helpful," I said, "because I'm con-
vinced that you should go ahead and marry him. Perfect bliss
isn't what marriage stands for. Where are you staying?"
She said she had gone back to the club. That surprised me.
She explained she hadn't given up her job; she wouldn't giveit up till the day before her wedding, provided there was a
wedding. A temporary manageress took over for her while
she was in Italy. "You see how unsure I am," she said. The
old lady and the music publisher left together, forgetting the
score on the table. Jean asked for her bill. “Oh, I know,"
she said.
"Know what?"
She tipped the waiter handsomely. "He'll come out to-
morrow night, and we'll be as we were.' She rose. "As we
were," she repeated, "and that isn't my idea of marriage."
We emerged into the smelly heat. "Can't we go somewhere
else?" she asked. "I haven't yet told you what's preying on
my mind." I took her to the basement club. The regulars
were like slaves chained to a galley. I took her to a table far
from the bar.
"Looks pretty shoddy," she said.
"You're telling me," I said, and went to get drinks. The
chap who took the half-crown from me in the morning cut me
dead as I came past him.
"I only want to know one thing," said Jean. "You have,
haven't you, heard of or even met men for whom women
fundamentally mean nothing?" I asked her to make her-
self more explicit. "What I'm wondering about is this: his
wife was it really her fault? Is there something in Francis
that pushes a woman away from him because he has no real
need of woman?" I pursed my lips. "He told me she was
frigid, disliked physical contact; but perhaps it was he who
made her frigid and dislike sex. It's so easy to make another
person dislike something. Say this drink is disgusting, say it
ten times, and I won't want to touch it any more.'
""
"A woman once called me an iceberg, so I'm no judge in
such matters."
"You're being frivolous," she sighed.
"I am because you probe too much. You two need to adjust
yourselves to the sinless life before you. It'll take time but
that's all."
"You honestly think that Francis wants marriage, wants a
woman at his side?" I nodded. "And you don't think thathe wants something no woman can give him because it isn't in
the reach of any woman?" I said I was certain of it. "I hope
you're right," she said with little conviction. "I must get
back to the club."
I accompanied her to the door.
"Where's Roger?" I asked.
"He's in Ireland on business," she said, "won't be back
before next week. Thank you for listening to my nonsense."
"Why don't you get the wedding over? Why this ridicu-
lous business of decorum? It doesn't fit in. They were sepa-
rated for umpteen years."
"He says a year must elapse, and I don't feel like wanting
to speed it."
I went back to my table feeling a bit glum. They weren't
making matters easy. Poor old Hilda's death was their deliv-
erance, yet they took no advantage of it. A glass of sour wine
improved my spirits. It had become too easy, and Francis
wasn't the man to care for easy solutions. Perhaps Jean de-
served blame too. She should have learned by now how to
treat his ups and downs. All of us, I said to myself, getting
another glass of wine, had our dreams, sought things beyond
our reach, rose to dizzy heights in moments of imagined eter-
nity; nonetheless we continued doing our work and eating
porridge to keep ourselves alive. Of course Francis was rich
and Jean pampered, so they could sniff at the porridge. Basi-
cally they didn't have to admit they were ever hungry. Fred
wandered in alone. He was visibly in a proposing mood. “Do
you like porridge?" I asked.
"Hate it brings back terrible memories of my repressed
childhood."
"Where's Moura?"
"Gone to bed with Geoffrey. She says heat always affects
her that way. You ought to know."
"I don't." He looked at me incredulously. "It always
snowed while Moura and I were together. Have a drink." He
sat down and for about half an hour we sat in silence.
"You don't know how fortunate you are," he said at the
end of the half hour. "Your wife, Jasper. A nice warm crea-ture who wants to be with you all the time. You don't know
how fortunate you are.
I gave that some thought, then I said: "It's because I don't
get any kick out of complications." That showed that I
wasn't strictly thinking of Rosie and me.
"I see," said Fred. "Yes, I see."
It occurred to me that I had no wish to chase after money.
I went to the bar and spoke to Mrs. Basement, or whatever
her name was. She was one of those big-hearted women who
usually weigh sixteen stones. She weighed eighteen. I asked
her to cash me a postdated check for five pounds. "As long
as it isn't too postdated, dearie," she said.
"Friday," I said.
That made her laugh. "If you give me one dated today it
won't be in the bank before Friday anyway."
Moura had arrived and I sat with them till six, then I
started to walk slowly toward Waterloo Station, stopping on
my way in a few pubs. The heat wasn't letting up. I found
myself in the Strand after a while. The crowd pushed
and rushed. Charing Cross Station was the mouth of a huge
sewer, the swirling intake coming from every direction. My
eyes lazily swept the crowd. Suddenly laziness left them. In
the pushing, rushing tumult Farrell was hurrying into the
station, carrying a bulging briefcase, like any fellow who takes
pajamas and toothbrush with him. But it wasn't the briefcase
or the visible fact that he was at Charing Cross and not in
Paris that dumbfounded me: it was his expression, the way he
carried his shoulders, how he moved; in short, the very es-
sence and presence of the man. For the man who was disap-
pearing through the station entrance wasn't the Farrell I
knew. It wasn't that he was prouder or humbler, quicker or
slower: he simply was a different man. You may say: so-and-
so has red hair and green eyes. I may say: I thought he had
black hair and brown eyes. You will rightly say: then we
aren't speaking of the same man. The frightening point, how-
ever, was that it was the same man.
I pushed through the crowd and got into the station. A
woman nearly knocked me over; I fell against a man who inhis hurry took no notice of it. I saw no Farrell. Still it was
a consolation of sorts to read on a green board: NOT STOPPING
AT LONDON BRIDGE. Where was he going? Sevenoaks, Tun-
bridge Wells, Hastings. St. Wilfrids, Dover and Deal. There
were Orpington, Tonbridge and Ashford too. I struggled out
of the station.
My first reaction was that he had come back clandestinely
in order to visit some secret mistress. I shrugged the silly
notion off. My next guess was that he had some illegitimate
offspring somewhere in East Sussex or Kent. That seemed
sillier than the secret mistress. Among my acquaintances the
legitimate children had to stand because the seats were occu-
pied by the bastards. Nevertheless I was displeased. For
wherever he went and for whatsoever purpose he went, his
going was the answer to Jean's qualms. Melodrama is what
you're after, I chided myself. Farrell could have a million
reasons, none of which were my business. Granted, but on
what sort of business was he bent? I decided to keep the whole
thing to myself. The whole thing? Call it a whole thing be-
cause an independent man of very independent means catches
a train at Charing Cross?
I tumbled not too steadily out of a tavern car of British
Railways at Salisbury. The tavern car of course had been
crammed with soldiers. I said to one of them: "Do you be-
lieve in ghosts?" He stared at me. "I mean do you believe
in ghosts taking trains?" The soldier moved away. I fol-
lowed him into a dark corner of the tavern car. "Let's, for
argument's sake, say ghosts are real but trains aren't.
Then..." The soldier left the car.
"Here I am," I said to Mrs. Walsh, who was waiting for
me among hundreds of businessmen's wives, "a bit drunk, as
you can see, so let's go to a pub and have a drink." She
was game. In the pub I gave her back two pounds. We sat
down at a table, as it was one of the listening kind of pubs.
"This is such a perfect new life for me," said Mrs. Walsh,
when I apologized again for being tight. "So gay, don't you
know."
"In that case you ought to take a kinder view of nanny," Isaid. She moistened her fleshy lips as a prelude to running
nanny down. I stopped that by asking her: "Were you a con-
fidante of my mother-in-law?"
"As much as she was capable of confiding in anybody. Why,
Mr. Danesbridge? Can I help you? I so much want to, don't
you know."
“We both know she wasn't too keen on her husband.” Mrs.
Walsh nodded. "Did she say or think he had mistresses!
This isn't an idle question. I have my reasons but I can't tell
them."
"Never," breathed Mrs. Walsh. "In fact Mrs. Farrell's
complaint was that he never moved, so to speak, from her
side."
"Quite," I said. She waited expectantly. "We'd better be
getting back to Orchard House." She wanted to buy me a
drink; I said no, I had enough. She insisted.
"I don't think I told you before, Mr. Danesbridge," she
said with the next drinks, “that I've been twice widowed.” I
tried to look grave. "My first husband was killed in a race.
He was a dirt-track driver. So you see I do know life a little."
"You certainly do."
"I remarried, don't you know-never regretted it, but
didn't regret my first marriage either." I wondered what she
was driving at. I wondered in vain for she was driving at
nothing.
"Well," I said, "there we are and we'd better go back."
In the station wagon she suggested I shouldn't mention to
nanny that she was twice widowed. Nanny had an evil tongue.
It was easier to keep that to myself than having seen Farrell
scurrying into Charing Cross Station. "Anything exciting in
London?" Rosie asked.
I was quite sober by then. "Fred sends you his love, that's
all," I said. After dinner I had to use most of my willpower
to keep myself from the telephone, though I couldn't make up
my mind whom to telephone. In the morning a long letter
arrived from Jean.
"Perhaps I can explain it better on paper," she began, then
went on to describe a long walk they took on the shore of LakeComo. "The more beautiful it looked, the less he saw it, and
when I said, 'What's wrong, Francis?' he looked at me ter-
ribly frightened, as if I were going to hit him or illtreat him
because he wasn't there at all." In Venice of all places he
was so depressed, but gently, uncomplainingly depressed,"
that she suggested they cut their journey short and return to
London. "What for?" he asked. "Then, realizing that he
wasn't good company, he rushed into the first shop and bought

wish I knew. I really feel like a hospital nurse whom the
doctor forgot to warn of the patient's disease." She added a
postscript. "I know you'll say bosh. It isn't." I put the
letter into my pocket, turned to Rosie who was facing me at

that your father is an ass?"
"I think he's very unbalanced," said Rosie. "If he hadn't
been he wouldn't have wrecked his marriage."
"That's your mother talking. Forget the wrecked marriage
for a moment. Anyway, two are needed to wreck one. That's
the minimum. It's usually three. With your parents it was
only two. However... What do you think of him, apart from
his marriage?"
"I don't know what to answer. He's my father, but you
know, darling, we've never been close to each other. It sounds
silly, but I always had the impression that he was afraid
of me."
"And you afraid of him. We know that. Two are needed
for that too. What I want to know is this: do you think him
capable of any double-dealing?''
"Oh, no."
"Let me explain. Do you think him capable of having
some secret life of his own? Women, gambling, blackmail, lit-
tle bastards, or anything like that?"
Rosie burst into laughter. "And you say I'm romantic,"
she laughed. "He can't say boo to a goose."
"Talking of goose," I said, getting up, "the geese are in the
garden." I went out to drive them back into the orchard.
But I was relieved. The phantom of Charing Cross had prob-ably a simple explanation. The gander turned on me; I held
my ground. Being the bigger coward of the two, he hurried
hissing into the orchard.
I saw no reason to go up to London, so I sat in the garden
and tried to write. Bees disturbed me, dogs disturbed me,
nanny disturbed me; then ants climbed up my leg. In the
afternoon I sat in the garden again.
Next morning was Friday morning. My check arrived; I
deposited it in the bank, and ceased worrying about Mrs.
Basement. I took my seat in the garden; then I smelled onions,
which meant that George's wife was near. "Wanted on the
phone, sir," she said. I went into the house. Davy was bark-
ing at the unhooked instrument. He was a bourgeois dog that
liked everything to be in its appointed place. "Shut up," I
said to him, "Hullo," into the telephone.
"My dear Jasper," said Farrell's voice. "How very nice,
Do you suppose I could come down on Sunday? Only for
luncheon. I must get back to London in the evening. Will
that be all right?"
"Why don't you come on Saturday and stay the night?"
"I'm staying with my brother Matthew. Haven't seen him
for over a month. We've a lot to discuss." I didn't care for
his voice.
"Have you seen Jean?" I asked. "I lunched with her the
other day." There was a pause for which I didn't care
either.
"Yes, she told me on the telephone," he said. "We're
lunching tomorrow," he went on.
"Only tomorrow?" I said.
"Today I'm at the office and tonight I'm staying with my
brother Eddie in Sussex." Another pause. "You met him
and his Swedish wife." I was thinking. "Are you there?"
he asked.
"I've a capital idea," I said. "We're going to ask Jean
down for the day on Sunday. High time Rosie met her.
We'll pick her up at the station."
"Excellent. I'm coming over from Matthew's place, so I
can't bring her down. See you on Sunday, and thank youagain for letting me come." I replaced the receiver and went
upstairs to Rosie, wondering why I had complicated matters.
That, however, was of secondary importance. What worried
me was why I was taken aback by Farrell's voice on the tele-
phone. But no: I wasn't taken aback: I was made uneasy. If
only I could forget the alien Farrell pushing his way into
Charing Cross Station. Rosie sat at her dressing table; the
sun on her face which reflected in the glass was completely
thought up by Rubens.
"Listen to me, my woman," I said. "I'm going to give you
an order which has to be obeyed." She smiled into the mirror.
"You won't smile, I can assure you. I am asking Jean down
for lunch on Sunday." The smile went, as I expected. "I
repeat, it's an order. I'm not being funny either, darling. I
have my reasons why don't you speak?"
..
"I've been waiting for you to finish."
"I haven't finished yet. Your father rang me up. He wants
to come down for lunch on Sunday, so I thought it was just
the right occasion for you to meet Jean.”
"Have you already asked her?" I said I hadn't, but Far-
rell knew I was asking her. "I won't let you down over it,"
was all Rosie said. I told her I was certain she would like her.
"I'll have to," she said, then stood up. "Perhaps that's the
best. You once told me what one needs most in life are faits
accomplis."
"I said that because I'm a lazy chap."
"Perhaps I am lazy too," she said.
"You're a very good girl."
II
Sunday morning dawned with windowpane rattling, west
wind and sheets of steady rain. Wet cows lowed for dry calves
outside the shed that housed the calves; and I lay in bed with
the blanket up to my beard watching Rosie getting ready for
Mass. "I won't get up before you come back from church,"
I said."And if they arrive early?" asked Rosie.
"They'll wait," I said, adding almost in an aside, "Jean
is coming by train-arrives at twelve-so I'll get Mrs. Walsh
to take me to the station to pick her up."
"I didn't know that," said Rosie. I said I forgot to tell
her that her father stayed the night with her Uncle Matthew.
"Mrs. Walsh can't go," said Rosie. "She can't leave the
luncheon to Eileen." That was George's wife. "I'll go get
her after I've brought them all back from church."
"I'll come with you," I said, rather touched. When they
left I jumped out of bed. I wasn't in the mood to stay in
bed in the now comparatively empty house. I looked out
through the window: wind and rain had driven summer away.
Summer would come back, but in my mood I was convinced it
could not. My life, I reflected after I got into the steaming
bath, had consisted of chasing after work, money and, from
time to time, women. My only angst was the fear of starva-
tion, thirst, and my pen being struck dumb. But since the
rich entered my life, my fear became impersonal; for feeling
uneasy over Farrell, Jean and Charing Cross Station had
nothing to do with starvation, thirst, occasional lust and the
writing business. I cursed the rich for having lifted my beard
from the grindstone, for having implanted a fear of things
that weren't within my competence.
George's wife hammered on the bathroom door. "Mr. Roger
on the phone," she shouted. I shouted back I was in my bath;
she should ask him what he wanted. That took her some time.
"He's just got back from Ireland," she called out. "He
says Mr. Farrell left word he is coming here for lunch. He
wants to know if he can come."
"Definitely. The more the merrier. Now don't say the more
the merrier to him: say definitely."
"Yes, sir," she said. She was one of those enviable people
who, owing to their inability to laugh, escape the vice of laugh-
ing at themselves.
While I stood with a pair of scissors attending to my beard
I suddenly started having regrets. The coming of Jean and
now of Roger would make it weightier and more irreparable.Without witnesses one goes less off the deep end. You idiot,
I said to the beaver in the glass, you idiot. What's all this?
What are you fretting about? As a punishment I snipped off
the few remaining dark hairs of the beard. An idiot with gray
beard stared into my eyes. What I needed was a glass of beer;
but not in the house. I wanted to get out of the house.
In rain and wind I marched to the back entrance of the
pub. The pub-keeper's nosy wife was called Bella and hailed
from Bolzano. She took me into the parlor, which was still
curtained, and brought a pint of beer. We communed in a
sense with one another; for she would hang around, and I
wouldn't speak. Her curiosity and my silence kept us to-
gether in the pub. I looked at her now and then; her eyes
dilated; I looked away. Her husband was polishing the taps
in the bar. I stood up to go. "I hears Mr. Farrell is coming
down today," she said. "The first time since poor Mrs. Far-
rell's funeral."
"How right you are," I said. Even that didn't improve my
mood. Nor did the smoking log fire in the drawing room.
"More cheerful like this," said George's wife.
They came back from church. I told them Roger was com-
ing too. "That doesn't worry me," said Mrs. Walsh. "I've
made two steak-and-kidney pies." Nanny looked daggers.
"I'm leaving," said Rosie. "Will I recognize her? I wish
you could come, but of course you have to stay."
"I'm sure I'll recognize her, I'm very good at recognizing,"
nanny said avidly.
"Jean will recognize you, darling," I said.
That didn't please Rosie, who would have preferred to re-
semble her mother. Rosie, nanny and Mary left. I sat down.
The dogs steamed all over the room. Then I heard Mrs.
Walsh's best social voice larded with don't-you-know, so some-
body had arrived. It was Roger. "How is he?" he asked,
almost before crossing the drawing-room threshold. "I
haven't seen him since his return and he wrote very seldom."
"He sounded very cheerful on the telephone," I said, gaz-
ing at the smoke curling from the fireplace. I felt like adding:
as cheerful as the fire."And Jean? Have you heard from her?" Poor anxious
boy, I thought.
"She's coming to lunch," I said.
He brightened up. "There he is," said Roger shortly after.
"You didn't bring us fine weather from Italy, Mr. Farrell,"
boomed Mrs. Walsh. His voice said he was sorry, and how
were they all?
"Getting on, getting on in every accepted sense of the
word," boomed Mrs. Walsh. She was at her best on Sundays
because in deference to the tradition established by Hilda on
Sundays she lunched with us, and that was being one up on
nanny every Sunday.
Farrell came into the drawing room, resembling not in the
slightest the Farrell at Charing Cross. I had been having
hallucinations, I said to myself, without believing myself.
"Roger, you're here too," he said after shaking my hand
affectionately. He could be very affectionate. "How splen-
did, Roger." While I got them drinks, father and son spoke
to each other with the scarce words of intimacy, each finishing
the other's sentence in his mind.
"And Jean?" asked Farrell. "Isn't she coming?" I
thought I detected relief in his voice. Dreaming again.
"Rosie's gone to pick her up in Salisbury."
"Oh, good,” he said, "so we'll be quite a party." Then
father and son reverted to their private chat. Mrs. George
poked her head in; the dogs made a dash for the door. "Ah,
feeding time," said Farrell.
I studied the man. Putting fancy aside, I still couldn't per-
suade myself that all was well with him. He wasn't on tenter-
hooks, he wasn't uneasy, he wasn't depressed. In short every-
thing about him was negative. He hardly touched his drink,
but he wasn't much of a drinker at best. When I heard the
unmistakable rattle of the station wagon I went into the hall.
Mrs. Walsh had preceded me, her cheeks flushed more by ex-
pectation than by the heat of cooking. As an anticlimax nanny
was the first to appear on the scene. "Still raining hard," she
said to me. Next came Mary. I told her to go and see her
grandfather. At last Mrs. Walsh savored fulfilment. She wasface to face with Jean. Rosie brought up the rear. I was glad
to see that Rosie was at ease with her. Ease departed when
she greeted her father. My lynx eyes were on Farrell and
Jean. He smiled at her in the same affectionate manner as he
had smiled at me. I found it understandable that they dis-
played no emotion; nonetheless it was too casual for my liking.
Again my liking: I was becoming sick and tired of my likes
and fears. Nanny came to get Mary. The child kissed Jean
and said: "You're so very nice." Farrell smiled like an im-
becile; Jean was visibly moved. Mrs. Walsh joined us for a
glass of sherry. She started with gay chatter, but trust Mrs.
Walsh. She soon felt the tension. That increased her enjoy-
ment.
"You'll have to go over to Dublin one of these days," said
Roger to his father.
"I'm devoted to Dublin," replied the father. Now why
couldn't he say: yes, I'm going soon to Dublin, or certainly, or
even fix a date?
"While you were in Milan, did you see our man there?"
Roger asked.
"We hardly spent any time in Milan," said Farrell, looking
at Jean, who was sitting in an armchair near the smoking log
fire, her left hand gripping her right wrist.
"I did love that restaurant we went to," she said. "What
was its name?"
"Campari," said Farrell. "How do you like country life,
Jasper?"
"He pretends he doesn't like it, but he does," said Rosie,
speaking to Jean.
"Mr. Danesbridge is genuinely devoted to the slop-hounds,"
said Mrs. Walsh. "They just adore him."
The slop-hounds came back; Mrs. Walsh brought out a filthy
rag with which she wiped their beards. "One day you'll have
to wipe mine," I said to Mrs. Walsh. She burst into ringing
peals of coy laughter, and stopped only when she realized
that nobody else was laughing.
"Did Francis take you to churches all day long in Italy,
Jean?" I asked."We visited many," she said. Why couldn't she look at me
when she spoke to me?
"I should have asked Rosie how Father Byrd is," said Far-
rell. "How is he?" Rosie said he was very well and he and I
were becoming fast friends. "I'm glad to hear that," said
Farrell.
"But we only talk of secular matters," I said.
"Well, he's a secular priest," said Farrell, and once again
I didn't care for his voice.
So I said in defense of Father Byrd: "Don't overlook,
Francis, that as a secular priest he's in the world, but not
of it."
Rosie beamed on me. "That's what he's told me umpteen
times.''
"Oh, yes," said Farrell. He walked to the window. "Ter-
rible weather."
"Suits us all," I said drily. "Have a drink, Roger. No-
body here seems to want a drink.”
"You've forgotten poor little me, Mr. Danesbridge," gig-
gled Mrs. Walsh. "A little sherry, if I may."
The clock said it was ten to one. Another ten minutes to
kill, but I wouldn't help. Rosie talked to Jean of Mary; I
held my peace; Mrs. Walsh laughed to herself. That was her
third sherry. Roger took his father to the French window and
spoke to him of Ireland. Farrell listened to him intently. At
last George's wife brusquely flung the door open. She wore a
starched white apron which was too small for her. There were
plenty of such aprons about, relics from parlormaid days.
"Lunch is served," she said, adding "
she said, adding "mam" as an after-
thought. We went into the dining room. It was no easier
in there.
Rosie didn't bother to catch my eye: we knew each other
too well for that. Jean looked not once at me; Farrell at
everybody; Mrs. Walsh at me. Roger alone appeared be-
wildered. I swallowed a lot of wine. Farrell's glass remained
untouched; he ate little. The steak-and-kidney pie was fol-
lowed by an apple pie. Mrs. Walsh's culinary gifts could besummed up in two words: more pies. They were good pies.
George's wife's apron string burst when she brought in coffee.
The apron was now like a flag at half mast in complete calm.
She removed it before she put the port decanter on the table.
"Are you going to show Davy?" Farrell asked. It had
taken him some time to think the question up. Rosie said we
wouldn't, but plenty of people wrote wanting him at stud.
"Davy doesn't need that," I said. "Davy gets all the
bitches he wants round here, isn't it so, Davy? Of course it is
so. Besides, Davy doesn't like being paid for his enjoyment.
Isn't it so, Davy? In fact Davy told me only yesterday that
he's going to give his accumulated stud fees to the first de-
serving bitch he meets."
Rosie rose and took Jean with her. I moved up to Farrell, as
he showed no sign of coming to my end of the table. Roger
moved up too. "How is your writing, Jasper?" asked Farrell.
Rain hit the window so hard that I half expected the pane to
shatter.
"My writing? I'm not thinking of writing," I said.
"I secretly hope whenever I meet you," said Farrell, "that
you'll tell me that you have started at last on your master-
piece."
"Cut that out, Francis," I said. "I want to know how you
are. To quote nanny speaking to Mary: 'I'm not pleased with
you today.'”,
Farrell smiled. This was his first sincere smile that day. "I
admired your acumen for a long time," he said, "but when I
arrived here and saw both Jean and Roger, my admiration
rose to even greater heights. You've saved me so much trou-
ble. Now I can say what I want to say to you all at the same
time." Who said I wasn't prescient? "Could we join the
ladies?" He stood up.
"Mr. Sponge wouldn't think much of that," I said. “Go
and join the ladies, but I want Roger to stay with me while I
drink my port. I hate drinking alone. Don't start before we
come." I gave him a huge grin which froze at once. "What's
he up to?" I asked Roger as the door closed."That's exactly what I'd like to know," he said. Gracie
scratched on the door: I let her in, took her on my lap, then
forgot about her. "He's not marrying Jean," I said. “Sticks
out a mile. Just a new tantrum, of course, sheer masochism;
then there will be plenty of suffering and moaning at the bar,
go-betweens and messengers will daily be employed, and then
he'll come crawling to her. I can assure you he can consider
me a non-runner."
"Makes no difference to me," said Roger, helping himself
to more port. "Virginia is marrying that fellow called Max
something."
"She has no business to," I exploded. “Max? He's bogus,
sham and phoney."
"But he's free," said Roger.
"You poor fellow," I said. " I did warn you, didn't I?"
"I had no choice, and if he can't get himself to marry as
charming a woman as Jean, then ... well, you know, Jasper.
Don't you think we should go in?"
"Just a second," I said, and told him about Charing Cross
Station.
The boy looked frightened. "I can't make it out, just can't.
Damn it, I know his life. There are no secrets in it." He
shook his head. "You have frightened me." I said it fright-
ened me too, opened the door, and we went into the draw-
ing room. Mrs. Walsh had retired already to her estates.
The drawing room was a long but not too wide room. The
French windows faced south. The fireplace was in the middle
of the east wall, with an armchair on each side of the fireplace.
A low-backed sofa faced the fireplace; thus if you sat in either
of the armchairs you could talk over it to anybody sitting or
standing on the west side of the room. The middle of the room
had only one piece of furniture, a low round table under the
chandelier. Farrell sat on a chair against the west wall; thus
he could talk to Jean and Rosie, who sat on each side of the
fireplace, practically without moving his head. I pulled a
chair up to one of the two console tables against the north
wall; thus I had them all in view, including Roger, who stoodleaning with folded arms against the escritoire near his
father's chair. Smoke sat in mute adoration at Rosie's feet.
(Rosie had to turn her head a little sideways if she wanted to
look me in the eye.) The other four dogs were in the room
too. There was no inducement for them to go out into the
angry rain. We were five souls and there were five dogs. That
surely had some significance. It escaped me.
"This rain will never stop," said Jean, to say something.
"It won't do much good to the herbaceous border," said
Rosie.
Enough of this, I said to myself. The scene was set, so let us
get on. "Francis has some momentous announcement to
make," I said, then laughed.
"Oh, yes," said Jean. "He's going to be a Benedictine
monk."
"What?" That came from Roger.
"Remember Loyola, Francis," I said. "I remember it."
"I remember," he said, "but then my hands were still
empty. You can't go to God with empty hands. A monastery
isn't a refuge from one's problems. It's a positive life to the
glory of God." It was a pity I couldn't see Rosie's expression.
I shifted in my chair but it didn't help. "But now I can go,"
continued Farrell in a pretty listless voice, "because I'm not
running from the world, I'm going from it. Jean knows that
for me to leave her dear presence is proof enough that I want
to go. And I am going."
"Never heard such highfalutin' nonsense in my bloomin'
life," I said, my cheeks-that is, the little of my cheeks the
beard didn't cover-pink with anger.
"Darling," said Rosie, very reprovingly.
Archie, the stupidest of the Sealyhams, took to chasing his
tail. "Stop it, dog," I said sharply.
"Why the Benedictines?" asked Roger.
"It is an Order after my heart, and I'm glad to say the
Lord Abbot of the Benedictine Abbey of St. Wilfrids-on-Sea
is willing to accept me as a postulant if I return there in a
month's time.""Ha," I said. "In a month's time? So his lordship must
feel a bit like me about it." I felt I was irritating Rosie. Far-
rell stood up. Now father and son were roughly side by side.
Gracie had already begged herself onto my knee. I stroked her
with such fury that the poor thing whimpered.
"The Benedictines don't accept you at once, Jasper," said
Farrell. "It isn't that easy. They tell you to come back after
a while if you still feel like it."
"Like what?" I interrupted.
"Like seeking God, like accepting a conventual life of
prayer, work, obedience, vowed to stability."
"I say," I said. "Stability? You, Francis?"
"My father means that Benedictines aren't friars, they're
monks who belong to their own monasteries. Stability means
they stay there. You've missed the point, Jasper."
"Thank you, Roger, for explaining," said Farrell.
"I know I miss many points," I said, "but I don't think I
miss the main point, which is..."
"You aren't here to excuse yourself to Jasper," said Rosie
to her father. "It's for him to listen to you." She turned
half around in her chair to look at me. "Now, Jasper," she
said.
"I understand Jasper," said Farrell quickly. "Indeed I
do." He took some sheets from his pocket. "Please let me read
a few words from the Holy Rule of St. Benedict, Jasper." He
peered at the paper. The fellow ought to start wearing specta-
cles. " 'So that never departing from His guidance, but perse-
vering in His teaching in the monastery until death, we may
by patience share in the sufferings of Christ, that we may de-
serve to be partakers of His Kingdom.''' Rosie began to cry
quietly, Roger was staring at the carpet, serenely Jean smiled
at Farrell. I pushed Gracie off my knee. "Perhaps now you'll
understand, Jasper." He put the sheets back into his pocket.
"Or you won't," he said.
"I'll try to," I said, because I didn't want to hurt Rosie.
Farrell put his hand on Roger's shoulder. "You're so quiet,
Roger. I do want you to say you're glad that I've found myvocation. Roger, I want you, boy, to share in my happiness."
Roger couldn't speak. I got out of the chair, for I knew I
had to leave the room. It had come home to me, practically
wounding me, that I loved the man. "While you all have a
nice cry," I said, "I'm going to have a drink."
I hurried into the dining room, got a bottle of Hilda's
brandy out of the sideboard, uncorked it, and poured out a
stiff drink. By then Rosie was standing at my side. "You're
a beast," she said. She sat down on a chair and tried to smile.
"Now you can see," she surprisingly said, "that it never was
mummy's fault. He'd have made Jean just as miserable." A
few days ago she didn't want to meet her. "A man whose
vocation is monastic life," went on Rosie amid her tears,
"can't give happiness to a woman. Darling, I'm sure mummy
has prayed for him that he should find his vocation."
"If you don't stop I'll become an old monk myself. Never
been so neck deep in an emotional quagmire before." It didn't
sound too convincing.
"But we must rejoice," she said in her best convent-school
manner, "that he has found it."
"You said the same when he was in Loyola. Now go and
wash your poor face, then give them tea or something." She
started for the door.
"Take this," I said, and almost poured the brandy into
her mouth. That didn't stop me from having an even stiffer
one after she left. Trying to ooze bonhomie, I went back to
the drawing room. "St. Wilfrids," I said. "Some horrible
place on the southeast coast. Candy floss, bingo, fish and chips,
palmists, more bingo, busloads of smelly people: just the right
place to retire to from the world."
"A man in the train told me you could smell St. Wilfrids
from the distance of ten miles," said Farrell, laughing like a
silly boy. Silly boy was wrong: a simple boy.
"I saw you on your way into Charing Cross Station," I
said.
"That must have been the evening when I went to spend a
night in the Abbey and to speak to the abbot," he said. Ilooked at Jean. She was still in the same armchair, her legs
crossed, her little friendly smile the same too.
"I told nobody," I boasted. "When did you tell Jean?"
"Francis told me when we lunched together. Somehow I
knew it was coming. I'm so glad, Jasper, that he's happy at
last."
"This is all too beautiful for words," I said. "All we need
is organ peals and doves circling the room." At last Jean left
her armchair. She walked slowly to the window; then as an
afterthought turned around and sat down again. It was
hardly worth getting up for that. "In a few years' time," I
said, "Francis will be the General
no, not General,
Superior-General of the Benedictines. You'll make a very
elegant one."
"I want to be only an ordinary humble lay brother," said
Francis.
"With those cuff links?"
"Without them."
"I've run out of matches and my lighter doesn't want to
work today," said Jean. Francis went to light her cigarette.
He leaned forward as he held his gold lighter to her cigarette;
and it occurred to me that it would be a good thing to leave
them alone for a while.
"Roger, I need your help," I said. "I've a little job to do
in a shed here. Can't do it alone. Will you come?" He was
glad to leave the room.
In the vast hall you could feel the silence of the house.
George and his wife had long since been swallowed up by
Sunday afternoon; Mrs. Walsh probably lay Indian fashion
flat on the floor of her room with her ear glued to it. Her
room was above the north end of the drawing room. Two of
the three clocks began to chime in the hall. The third came
on at their third chime. "So we're off again," I said to Roger.
"Come and have a drink in the dining room." We went into
the dining room. "So we're off again," I repeated. "What do
you make of it? Do you believe it?"
"What do I know?" said Roger desperately. "If I say yes,then I can see him coming back after a month. If I say no,
then I know it's just wishing he stayed or found some other
solution." He took a glass of brandy. "But he seems far more
serious than ever before. He's told Uncle Matthew about it."
"And what did Uncle Matthew have to say?"
"Uncle Matthew reminded him that he wanted to be a monk
at the age of seven."
"What does that mean or imply?"
"It means that my father wanted to be a monk at the age
of seven."
"But whichever way we look at it, your father isn't seven
years old now."
Roger said: "Precisely." After that we said nothing and
both went and surveyed the dismal world from the window.
The dining-room window was next to the front door, and in
the torrential rain two Perpendicular-Gothic figures swayed
toward the door. Though they were partly obliterated by the
rain, I recognized the two straggling creatures at once.
"That's all we need," I said, and went out into the hall.
The rain must have half blinded them. They groped for the
doorbell, their hands touching the panels of the door. I grimly
let them, and opened the door only after they found the bell
and rang it. Fred and Moura tumbled into Orchard House.
"We thought we'd never make it," panted Moura.
"This is a surprise," I said. "A real one."
"We were staying with Eric in his cottage," said Fred.
"You know Eric." I shook my head.
"Everybody knows Eric, darling," said Moura. "But Geof-
frey had an awful row with him before luncheon and stormed
out. Fred and I had nothing to do with the row and we
wanted to be polite, so we stayed behind."
"We wanted to show Eric that we took no sides," said Fred.
"But he kicked us out. We didn't want to go straight back
to London," went on Moura, "so we thought we'd look in on
you, but we didn't dream it was so far. We nearly perished,
Jasper."
"Now you're here. This is my brother-in-law, Roger Far-rell. You'll flood this house." They weren't dripping: water
poured from their raincoats. "Put them in the cloakroom.
It's that door to the right." Being a gentleman and. not know-
ing them, Roger helped Moura to peel off her raincoat, which
in better days had been some man's macintosh. I tried to
ignore the traveling bag but Fred put it on a chair. "Put
that in the cloakroom too. Now come into the dining room

"A country house is your perfect background," said Moura.
"It makes you look so lordly and somber. Lordly and somber,
Fred."
"Yes, Moura," said Fred. "Is your wife away?" I said
she wasn't.
Roger asked anxiously: "Shouldn't we go back into the
drawing room?''
"With these?" I said. They looked hurt. "The point is,"
I said, to unhurt them, as it were, 'that you've come in on a
family drama. In the drawing room sits my widowed father-

monk. I'm sure you don't understand a word of it. I don't
blame you, I'm just warning you."
"Oh," said Rosie, appearing in the door, "what a pleasant
surprise. You'll stay and have tea. You must." She had put
on fresh makeup, but traces remained of the tears she shed
after she went upstairs.
"They'll stay for tea, don't worry, Rosie. Now drink your
brandy and come into the drawing room. I think God sent
you to cheer us all up. In the drawing room behave as if you
knew nothing. I beg you to mention neither wedding bells
nor abbey bells. The best is not to mention bells at all."
"I just don't understand a word of it," complained Moura.
She wore a green tweed skirt and a black jumper, no stock-
ings, but (for reasons best known to her) golden Italian san-
dals, out of which peered her toes, caked with mud and what-
ever else you pick up on a country lane in the rain. Farrell
was charming in the grand manner and did his own best to
keep away from bells; and as happens to those who wish tobe polite under every circumstance and to all and sundry, he
soon brought conversation to a standstill. However, there
could be but few standstills with Moura in the room. “I've
known Jasper for years and years," were the words with
which she smashed the silence. "Knew him before he had a
beard. He had no personality without a beard. He looked like
a withered calf." Farrell smiled like one in pain. "I met
Fred when he was writing his epic poem."
"There's no future for epic poems in this benighted coun-
try," said Fred.
"When I said writing I meant you said you were writing an
epic poem, but you never were," she said.
Mrs. Walsh opened the door, caught sight of Fred and
blushed like a girl. "Oh," she squeaked. Fred blushed too.
"Who is she?" asked Moura. Rosie introduced her.
"I'm going to prepare tea, Mrs. Danesbridge," said Mrs.
Walsh, looking at Fred. "I'm sure you're dying for tea," she
said to Fred.
"Not for me, Mrs. Walsh," said Farrell. "I have to be
back, alas, in London by seven. I have to leave you, Rosie;
such a pity. Shall I drive you back to Burnham Beeches,

"Jean is staying the night," said Rosie, to my and Jean's
surprise. "Oh yes, you are, Jean. You don't want to go back
in this horrid rain. You look so tired."
"You are kind," said Jean, "very, very kind, but I can't.
Brought nothing with me."
Rosie said that didn't matter, she would lend her a night-
gown and Mrs. Walsh would get her all she needed for make-
up and washing in the morning, adding she had a couple of
toothbrushes she hadn't ever used.
"One will be for me," chimed in Moura. "Fred can put
toothpaste on his finger. Done it a thousand times in my life."
I looked at her pained and surprised. "Don't look at me like
that, you withered old calf," said Moura. Farrell winced.
"I'm certain your sweet wife will let us stay the night even
if only on the sofa here. I just can't go back into the rain."Unenthusiastically Rosie said of course we would put them up.
"Thank you so much," said Jean to her. "I should love to
stay the night." I always said Rosie was a good girl, and now
she had proved that she had understanding too.
Roger declared he would follow his father to London in his
car. "I'm glad you're coming," said Farrell, who shook hands
with Jean, saying he would telephone her some time tomorrow.
I accompanied him and Roger to the door. Rosie he had kissed
rather tenderly, which brought tears to her eyes again.
"I am afraid, Francis, I can't take it seriously," I said.
"You don't have to, Jasper," he said. "Thank you for my
excellent luncheon, and please come and see me before I go.
May I telephone you, and then could you come up to Lon-
don?"
"Don't exercise humility on me," I said. He smiled. "Any
day you like." I heaved a sigh of relief when the two cars
disappeared in the rain.
I asked Jean after tea to come to my study. "I don't want
to hurt your feelings," she said, "but that woman is horrid."
"Moura is all right," I said, "but one should see her only
in bed and in bars. Well, what do you make of it? I mean
Francis, needless to say. I don't think he's fair to you."
She sat in the only armchair of the study, a leather arm-
chair, deep and dark; I sat at the desk, and it was a little as if
I were interviewing her for a job. Though it was still early,
the rain had melted the light outside. I switched on the table
lamp. Jean remained half in shadow and when she spoke to
me it was like listening to a disembodied voice, but a warm
one.
"I failed to explain it to you the other day," she said. "I
don't know whether it is God he needs. I can't judge. God
is too vast a concept and I'm far too small, but he is seeking-
he's been seeking since I've known him and it isn't me. I
thought perhaps it was his wife that caused him to be there
without being there, but it became even more marked after
her death, and when he told me yesterday that now he can go
to God with a calm conscience, I believed every word of it."She stopped speaking. I waited for her to go on. Gracie whim-
pered outside the door. I opened it for her. She got on my
lap. "Do I still believe it?" continued Jean. "I don't know.
I haven't the faintest idea what monastic life is like, though
he spoke of it in detail yesterday. Prayer and work, obedience
and the rest, a very hard life-I just don't know. But what I
do know is that if he discovers that it isn't the life for him,
that he's unfit for it, I honestly can't see so finicky a man
leading a strictly communal life. As I say, if he finds it isn't
for him and he comes back to me, he won't be happier than
he has been and he will tear his heart to pieces again."
"A cheerful prospect; but you ought to think of yourself.
Where's your pride? Why should you wait for him? Jean,
you're one of the most likable women I have met and you
ought to find yourself a man who deserves you, a man to whom
you are the answer."
"I've always been a doormat," she said. "Poor Francis, he
said yesterday leaving me is his great sacrifice. I think it is.
No more Jean to hold him when he wakes frightened from a
nightmare, no more Jean to keep him warm in bed... I must
stop or I'm going to bawl." I went over to her and kissed her
forehead. "You must allow me to remain your friend," she
said in a small voice, "and Rosie's too. How wonderful to
have understood that it would have been sheer suffering to
drive back with him. Do you know why it would have been
sheer suffering? Because having taken such a big decision he
shouldn't be worrying about my vanity-I mean woman's
vanity. I have none, yet he worries about it. It's heartbreak-
ing. If only he could understand that I'd be a far happier
woman without him and knowing he has found peace at last,
than with him, and he never being completely with me."
"You're a great woman, Jean, but frankly, do you believe
he's going to stick to it?”
"Not for a moment," she sighed.
"We'd better go back," I said.
Moura drank steadily, her face paler, her brow darker with
every drink. First she talked obscenely, for she guessed sheshocked Jean; then morose silence followed. Then she said:
"I can't stay here any longer. The country always gives me
the creeps. I want to get back." She opened her bag. "I've
got enough money for a taxi. Get me a taxi, Jasper."
"I'm not coming with you," said Fred. "I like it here."
"I don't want you to come with me," she said. "A taxi. I
want to get out of this God-damned place."
"I'll show you your room, Jean," said Rosie, and they left
the room. The village cab driver lived only two hundred yards
away. The rain had slowed down. I raced to the cab driver,
who luckily was in, told him to get moving; that is, pick up
Moura and take her to the nearest railway station or to hell or
anywhere, as long as he took her. I went on to the pub.
Bella came to me with piercing eyes. "Did your two friends
find the house?" she asked. "They stopped my hubby-he
was outside the garage. Just think: walking in so much rain."
"If they were friends of mine, then they missed me," I
said. "I was out in the rain myself. A pint of your best bitter,
please."
I would bring Jean to the pub in the morning. That
would worry old Curiosity. On second thought I decided
against it, for by tomorrow she would certainly hear that Mr.
Farrell was entering a monastery. Therefore Jean's entry in
the pub would be stale news. The rain had stopped: it be-
came stale news too. The stars appeared, first singly, then
en masse.
It was Fred who had the last-but-one-word. He sat up with
me well into the small hours. I accompanied him to his bed-
room to make sure that in his loneliness he didn't burst in on
Mrs. Walsh. He sat down on the edge of the bed and said to
me: "You don't see it, Jasper. I do. It is the grand finale if
there ever was one. That man loved that woman dearly, and
I can assure you she's one of the most lovable women I've
seen for years. Some men are lucky. When the urge to leave
the world and take himself to God seized him he wanted to
make a worthy sacrifice. So he gave up that woman because
she was his most precious possession. The great sacrifice. Andnow he can go to God because he has shown God that he loves
Him completely."
"Yes," I said. "Yes." Then added: “Yes.”
"Can't you see it? Are you as blind as that?"
"It isn't that," I said. "What I witnessed was a splendid
third act. I was the spectator who now and then had the bad
manners to interrupt the actors; but still it was only the
third act: that is, the last act.. Now you know and I know
there's no such thing as a last act till one dies; or if there is
one, then the actors still have to go home, eat, sleep and, most
important of all, get up next morning. The reason I refused
to be impressed is because there is a next morning."
"That's because you're a Philistine."
"I am," I said, and closed the door behind me. The late
moon swept the wide hall with her eerie beams, and I wished
with the impatience reason fails to explain that it were already
morning and I didn't have to go to bed. Why should I? So I
started for the stairway; the study beckoned to me; I turned
back on reaching the stairway and went straight into our bed-
room. Rosie was awake. "How could you ever have cared for
that awful woman?" she asked, before I could close the door.
"That's a very secular question," I said.Chapter Seven

​I
Father Byrd's housekeeper was a poor man's Mrs. Walsh. I
counted four warts on her pallid face: to my best knowledge
Mrs. Walsh had only one. She took me into his study saying
he was out but she was expecting him any minute now. "Is
it very important, Mr. Danesbridge?" she asked hopefully.
I shook my head. "I just want to have a chat with him."
She left me, to heat up yesterday's roast. There was a slight
smell of burned cauliflower. I sat down in the study, which was
small, a little untidy and sprinkled, so to speak, with devo-
tional goods. A crucifix stood on the desk and an oleograph
of the Blessed Virgin hung above the chimneypiece. The smell
of burned cauliflower became dominant. The scent of Rosie's
roses in the Woolworth vase was swamped with it.
I had seen Fred and Jean off an hour before. I didn't feel
like telling Fred that his words of the night before had im-
pressed me more than I showed, though not in the way he
would have expected. Farrell was a tragedian, a man who
overindulged his emotions, a lover of the spectacular. Yes, I
nodded, spectacular was the word. The rich man giving up
his worldly goods, the devoted father leaving his family, theman of luxury reaching out for the hair shirt weren't good
enough for the tragedian. He had to give up his beloved too.
Logically you have to give up the beloved if you become a
monk; but for Farrell that was the crux of the matter; for the
tragedian, if he is a good tragedian, knows that giving up
one's woman is the finest sacrifice of all. Since the woman
happened to be Jean, he could always go back to her after
the curtain had dropped on the last act.
"I'm sorry to have kept you waiting," said Father Byrd.
"It's that motor bike again." He lit his pipe and I related
the grand scene of yesterday. The friendly, fat priest twice
relit his pipe while I told the story. "We must pray for his
intentions," he said when I finished.
"As you know, Father, I'm not the praying sort," I said.
"I'm saying this with all respect and admiration for those
who believe in prayer. Besides, I don't believe for a moment
that he's going to stick to it."
Father Byrd shook his head. "This pipe will ruin me in
matches," he said.
I waited for him to light it again. "I've come to know Far-
rell well," I said. "Recently only, but well. I can't see him
giving up completely all those little things which are luxuries
for you and me but necessities to him, owing to habit. He
wouldn't use all those matches: he'd use his gold lighter.
What I mean is, the thought of matches wouldn't even occur
to him."
"Pipes should always be lighted with matches," Father
Byrd said.
"Do Benedictine monks eat caviar often?" I asked, laugh-
ing.
"I very much doubt it," he smiled.
"There you are," I said.
"St. Benedict laid emphasis on his monks keeping aloof
from the world. The distance between them and the world
should never be lessened. And as regards your caviar," he
said, pointing with the pipe, which had gone out again, "the
Holy Rule of St. Benedict says: 'Not to seek after delicateliving'. So you see, sir, that Mr. Farrell will have to give up
all those pleasures. But of course he knows that."
"I didn't mean it literally," I said.
It was his turn to laugh. "St. Benedict," Father Byrd
went on, becoming thoughtful, 'laid the greatest emphasis on
conventual life, which is communal life, and that is com-
munal prayer and communal work even if your own work isn't
communal. I think that it is the acceptance of communal life,
especially of communal prayer, that may prove the biggest
obstacle for a man like Mr. Farrell to surmount." My expres-
sion showed that it wasn't familiar ground for me. "Man is
proud," went on Father Byrd, "and if a man is intelligent,
then he is mighty proud of his brain. Now Mr. Farrell will
have to accept not being proud of his brain, culture and in-
telligence, and that the prayer of the simplest monk at his
side is worth his, however beautifully his mind rises in prayer
and however simply the mind of the simple monk rises. To
accept that will be very difficult for a man like him. And it
will be like that in everything else too in that communal life."
From beneath the layer of recent and tabulated memories a
yellowish face emerged. "I knew a man when I lived in Paris.
He had been a monk but left his monastery. I can't remember
the name of his order, probably I never knew it: I wasn't yet
interested in such matters.
"The man lived in a dismal hotel in the rue des Trois
Frères, on the top floor where rooms were dark and narrow,
but very cheap. Summer and winter he wore a dirty old rain-
coat, and French raincoats when dirty look more dirty than
any other raincoat. [Father Byrd laughed patriotically.] He
kept to himself, seldom left his lightless room; but one after-
noon he came to have coffee in the bistro across the street. I
asked him to have a brandy with it; then he had another, and
with the fourth brandy he told me why he left his monastery.
"'I couldn't take communal life,' he said. He shuddered as
he said that. 'I live here almost a monastic life,' he said, 'but
alone. Alone of course one can't lead a monastic life.' In
spite of the thundering traffic I could hear his sigh."'If you left it,' I said, 'then accept it. Live in the world
now.'
"'I don't care for the world,' he said. He put up the dirty
collar, though the sun shone gaily, then said: 'My pride was
my undoing.' He turned back from the open door. 'It still
is.' Evidently he afterward regretted speaking to me, for he
darted into his room whenever we came face to face on the
landing."
"There you are," said Father Byrd. "One more reason
to pray for Mr. Farrell's intentions."
"I'm certain he won't stick to it," I said. "It sticks out a
mile." I looked at the clock. "Good gracious, it's past one.
I'm keeping you from your lunch." He accompanied me to
the door. "If Farrell makes the grade or whatever you call it,
Father, then my ideas will undergo a profound change. But
he won't.”
In the village street I was saluted by a tiny old man who
was as near to being the village idiot as was humanly possible.
"Good day, major," he said knowingly. I had never been a
major in my life. What a grim prospect, I said to myself,
thinking of brains.
I reached Orchard House in a depressed state. Nanny was
on the stairs, poor old nanny who had been left out of yester-
day's excitement. "What do you make of it?" I asked.
"Oh, it'll be like Loretto... pardon me, Mr. Danesbridge, I
mean Loyola. Get mixed up with those names." Heartily I
agreed with her, and when on Wednesday of the following

"Therefore I should be very grateful if you could come and

"Business?"
"Well, family matters. May I expect you?"I said yes indeed. "Now what sort of business does he want
to discuss with me?" I asked Rosie at lunch. "Surely he

"But he hasn't," she said. "Mrs. Walsh is going up for the

Certainly Rosie was a good girl, and off we went in the morn-

She told me, among other things, that a friend of hers had a
cousin. No, not a cousin: the young man was a nephew who
became a monk. He left the monastery after two years, mar-

It was the first time I had entered my father-in-law's office,

Farrell's room tried to impress one as a gentleman's study

"Like the Copper Horse in Windsor Great Part was Queen

My need took me into the nearest pub, which was suffo-

pensive food in restaurants, but I would refuse his invitation.
To my surprise the thought of his returning cheered me up.
Perhaps I would accept his invitation.
