II
On the following Saturday afternoon the enormous Rolls
Royce I once mistook for a hearse stopped outside Orchard
House. I was outside the house too at the time, listening to a
long rigmarole by George, who in deference to his private
Saturday protocol was half drunk. The other half he kept for
the evening. The chauffeur got out and opened the door. A
stick appeared, followed by a cautious foot; then slowly but
inevitably Matthew Farrell got himself onto the ground. He
wore a green pork-pie hat and a checked tweed suit. He lifted
his stick in salutation. "My dear fellow," he said, "just the
man I've come to see."
I took him into the house. He said the countryside looked
very pretty. I said Rosie was shopping with nanny and Mary
in Salisbury; he said he was fond of Salisbury. I opened the
drawing-room door for him.
"Do you want a drink or tea?" I asked.
"Perhaps a drink will be less trouble," he said as he
dropped like lead into an armchair.
He wasn't satisfied, heaved himself out of the armchair,
took a step forward and from there dropped back into it.
"Better," he commented. I went to get whisky. He looked
like a whisky drinker.
"So you have lost a brother and found a monk," I said.
"Get out, all of you." That was for the dogs, who, excited by
the new face, took to playing hide-and-seek all over the room.
"Nice fellows," commented Matthew.
"Two of them are bitches," I said."These used to be poor Hilda's dogs," said Matthew. “Re-
member them. It was decent of you to take them over. Let's
plunge into it because Rosemary will soon be coming back, I
take it, and what I'd like to discuss with you is between you
and me. How's the little girl?" I said Mary was fine. "Oh,
good," he said, "excellent. Could you push that little table
nearer?" On the little table stood his whisky. "My fallen
disc or whatever they call it nowadays." I pushed the little
table nearer. He lost himself in thought or whatever he called
it, so neither of us spoke. Now and then he lifted his stick as
if to strike the nearest vase.
"It's difficult," he said. "It looked simple before I came
here. Now look here, my dear fellow, what I want to say is
we need your help."
"My help? You flatter and surprise me." How could a
stocky man with that stick and the Rolls Royce need my help?
"My help?" I repeated.
"Yours. May I say at once that Eddie and I were very
much impressed by your abilities on the day of the funeral.
The way you presented your case was masterly."
"And landed me with the dogs."
"The dogs
but you should have foreseen that. I have
dogs too. One gets along with them if one knows how to treat
them." I wholeheartedly agreed with him. "Back to brass
tacks," he went on with his stick in midair. "Eddie and I
talked it over last night. It looked simple. I wouldn't say it
does now. One's always more sanguine at night." Down came
the stick, only to rise again. "Here goes. Francis' strange
delusion is a real disaster for our firm; a real disaster. He has
to be stopped."
"You call it a delusion?"
"Well, yes. If a young man feels he has the vocation or
inclination for monastic life, then well and good; but for an
important businessman to take such a step is.
is... very
extravagant. And it's a disaster."
"Pray why?''
"Because Francis is a financial and commercial genius."
My hairy jaw dropped. "He's made our firm with his genius.It was a nice, quiet, humdrum, old-fashioned house, but we
expanded, thanks to him; all our subsidiaries were his idea.
How often my brothers and I literally trembled when he came
up with one of his grandiose plans. Yet they were all suc-
cessful. His going is a calamity, an irreplaceable loss to the
nation's economy."
”
"You amaze me, was all I could say.
Matthew held out his glass: I poured whisky into it. “He
has to be stopped. Farrell & Sons need him; he has no right
to give us up. You have responsibilities you aren't entitled to
throw away if you have his gifts."
"Francis a financial and commercial genius," I said. "Stop
chewing my hand." That was for Gracie. "I thought he sat
in his office when he sat there because he was born your fa-
ther's son. Well, I never."
"The sun is in my eye," complained Matthew. I went to
pull the curtain. You wouldn't have expected him to shift.
"It has to be stopped," said Matthew, "and there's only one
person who can do it and that's you."
"I? Not I. Why I?"
"You have the gift of persuasion. We saw that and were
struck by it on the day of the funeral. And you have another
advantage: you're no blood relation. We, the brothers, failed
because we are, but you are a breath of fresh air, and with
your power of persuasion. . . .”
"May I speak? I have come to know Francis very well. He
has all the cussedness of the weak. If I went and tried to use
what you call my powers of persuasion he would start for the
monastery even sooner than he intends. The reason is obvi-
ous. It's better for all of us to humor him, especially as we
can do little else, and when he comes back, he'll be a wiser if
not a better man." Matthew nodded several times; the stick
was now quiet at his side.
"That's our last hope," he said. "Eddie thinks too that he
won't stay. He has met the lady Francis wants to marry...
I mean wanted to marry. He believes he'll come back because
of her. He must." He began to push himself out of the arm-

couldn't pull it off. A great pity. We're willing to make him
chairman if he's willing to stay. I don't see why I, the elder
brother, should remain the chairman forever. I'm more a
farmer than an executive. If you speak to him just mention
that en passant. You never know, do you?"
I asked him to wait till his niece came back, but he said he

"As a Catholic, why are you so shocked by your brother's
going into a monastery?" The vast man smiled diffidently.
That smile could have been Farrell's or Rosie's or Roger's.
"If it happened to anybody else,” he said, "I would shout
bravo and be touched by it. But you don't accept something
so fine from your own brother."
His chauffeur stood glumly beside the huge limousine, car-
ing little for George's vivacious talk. "If you have an oppor-
tunity..." said Matthew, entering the tonneau. He called

Eddie are right."
I pushed my head into the tonneau. "His woman thinks
the same. I said that to cheer him up. He beamed.
"
"That's a real big car, sir," said George.
The next car to burst in on us was Roger's, on Monday. The
fast, low red thing was still throbbing when I opened the

"I took him there myself," said Roger. I bowed my head,
for till the last moment I had hoped that Farrell would re-

preferable not to embark on it. In the morning nanny had

"London," she said coldly.
I was certain it could only be Farrell. "Hullo," I said
eagerly.
"Oh, it's you," said Fred's voice. "I want to speak to
Priscilla. I'm in a call box; get her quick." Nanny was a
vengeful little woman.
Roger lifted two suitcases from his racing car. "I'm sureRosie and you won't mind if I stay here for a few days. I'll

"Orchard House can take two commuters," I said, taking

They had left at eight in the morning. "How many suit-

"It's five past eight," he complained.
The journey was comparatively long and jerky. After

"Did he chat?" I asked.
"He was mostly silent, but suddenly he turned to me all

Cenobites, the strongest race of monks.' He hit his knee and

"Esprit de corps," I said.

"Very significant," I said.
"Did you go into the monastery with him?" asked Rosie.

There are dozens of them."
..
"I'd lose them. Didn't he wear a watch at all?"
"He said the bell and his superiors would keep him pune-

Roger played for a bit with Smoke, then said: "Perhaps that

"Did he mention me?" Roger thought he did. "What do

He was a creature of habit, and if it was sleeping time then

I didn't broach the subject to him; nor did I speak to Rosie

"Why don't you have another try at Virginia? Max isn't

After a bit of hemming and hawing Roger confessed he hadceased to be interested in Virginia. I was the last to blame

"You're beginning to develop your own personality," I
said; 1; but I knew I didn't get it right.
He left us on Monday and for a few days we waited for his

jealous of the baby.
Soon Christmas came and Rosie wanted Jean to stay with

might she come down for the day on the first Sunday after

a car, even if the car is at a great distance, I start waiting."
We ceased to be strangers.
After luncheon I took her into my study. The wintry sun
was pleasantly shining, and there we sat till Mrs. Walsh called

couldn't help remarking: "He could just as well be here."
"He isn't," she said. "I think of him so much at night,"

plained that my bed was too hard, so he bought me a softer

"I don't," I said.
"And there's something else, too, Jasper." She passed her

She wiped her eyes, and we went to have Ceylon tea and

You win, Jasper."

"Caked with mud and dung, which my dogs bring in from

"Love; and now buy me a drink. I'm broke." I bought him

One of the rigid young men collapsed. "You can't do that

"Now you understand perhaps why I'm entitled to know how

"What did Roger say?"

There were, I thought, no cheerful warm fires in the abbey.

She had little to say. Their meeting was like a luminous,

"When are you going to see him again?"
"In a year's time; I said I'd go and see him every year."George's wife announced that dinner was served. The dogs

I was in the middle of Mrs. Walsh's veal-and-ham pie when

"But that's cruel. To go and see your father, or even an old

She nodded again. "Cruel. Why can't you go and see him

"He has left the world, so it's not fair to disturb him."
"But the world will disturb him when he comes back

"He won't."
"That remains to be seen. Look at those bloody dogs."
I stood up and let them out of the dining room.Chapter Eight
I
Though it was noon, mid-August, and the sun blazed, I
alighted from the railway train at St. Wilfrids-on-Sea like a
thief in the night. For I had come on an errand Rosie would
have disapproved of if she had known. She didn't. I wasn't
only the thief in the night: I was the wolf that had prowled
around the sheep pen and now would gain admittance. Sheep,
beware.
Father Byrd alone knew of my errand. I told him one eve-
ning over port that I had promised Farrell to go and see him
once he was a monk. But I didn't want to see him just in a
waiting room: I wanted to see him in his monastic existence,
because that would settle the question one way or the other.
"I'd see whether he's kept there by his pride. I'd see that at
once. I know my Farrell. He's gone too far and he doesn't
dare turn back. Losing face, and all that." Father Byrd
observed that in monasteries there was no room for pride.
"Let me see it for myself. I know it's vulgar naked curiosity,
but perhaps there's something far deeper behind it. I won't
know until I've seen for myself."
"I think you should go," said Father Byrd. He wrote to
the abbott, and the guestmaster wrote he was expecting me onAugust the 16th, which was a Tuesday, and would I let him
know when I would arrive. I said afternoon. Then before
starting off I had cold feet. "There's not a catch in it, is
there?" I asked Father Byrd. He assured me I wasn't the
sort of person who could be caught. It was a two-sided com-
pliment in a way.
Farrell was wrong. St. Wilfrids could be smelled as far
away as Charing Cross Station, and the train was jammed
with day tourists and vacationers, and the older they were
the more foolish they looked with paper hats and Tyrolese
hats and other stupid hats. Whistles were blown, candy floss
was wafted on the air thick with the smell of potato chips.
The only quiet ones were those who were going on a week's or
a fortnight's holiday and were already adding up their ex-
penses. "Kiss me quick," said a cap worn by an old maid
blinking behind spectacles. "I love Lucy" was another fa-
vorite. The noisy mass streamed out of the station carrying
me along. I managed to escape into a pub. The stench of fish
and chips and of roast lamb and two vegetables was every-
where. "Down for the day?" asked the pub-keeper.
"More or less," I said.
"Makes a nice change," he said.
"Yes indeed," I said politely. It was a pleasant pub;
the customers were railway men and commercial travelers.
The influx went farther afield. Well, there I was, and in the
afternoon I would see Farrell. I felt annoyingly excited. I
could and probably would tell him of the many changes since
Rosie's visit, the most startling of which was Mrs. Walsh's
leaving in March to marry Fred. We feared that nanny would
have a nervous breakdown or give us notice. She did neither.
A Spanish cook was engaged, and her culinary art consisted
of smuggling, as it were, fried eggs into any dish she cooked.
The days of pies were gone forever.
With the new influence I acquired with the Farrell family
I got my pal Matthew to give Mrs. Walsh the thousand
pounds she would have received if she had left Fred-less a year
ago. Fred and Mrs. Walsh established themselves in CamdenTown. Since his marriage Fred had developed a quality I
wouldn't have suspected him of possessing: namely, benignity.
Kind and benign, he pontificated in the basement club; he also
worked on his memoirs. His more practical-minded wife went
out cooking for five days a week. Rosie and I dined in their
small flat on an exceptionally good steak-and-kidney pie. "It's
a pity," I said after dinner, "that we can't ask you down to
Orchard House. If you came, nanny would leave, so we must
wait till the children are old enough to do without her."
"I fully appreciate that," said benign Fred.
I left the pub and looked down on the seaside resort. In
a harbor of sorts rusted an old tug. I pushed past three
youths, then a pram was pushed into me. I got off the pave-
ment and was nearly run over by a crowded bus. I took a
street to the left: it was a chain of fish-and-chips shops: I
backed out. Shortly after, I found a quiet pub. A fat woman
served me. "Business isn't good on a sunny day like this,"
she complained. "They're all on the beach."
"Is the Benedictine Abbey far from here?''
"It's in Abbey Road," she said. "Not far from the prom-
enade. Down for the day or are you staying here?"
I answered her to the best of my ability. Two women came
in, their hefty arms a disgusting pink. They drank baby
cham. In the door stood a small boy calling mommy. "You'll
'ave to wait," called back one of the hefties.
Surely Farrell was dying of curiosity to hear about his
daughter-in-law. For in June Roger had married a big, lanky
girl who was a distant cousin of his, two years older than he
but very competent. Theirs was quite a grand wedding; I had
to hire a morning coat and top hat, and mislaid the top hat
irrevocably at the reception, which meant that I had to pay
for it. The poor always pay. Now they were in Italy. Roger
had acted swiftly. The wedding took place six weeks after the
engagement, the Knightsbridge house was sold, and on their
return they would move into a large flat near Lowndes Square.
In short, Roger was liberated.
I looked around the pub: a young man and his girl sat ata table fondling and kissing; at another table sat three blind
men, and in the corner an ugly couple drooped, tired and
disheartened, hating every minute of their holiday. And who,
I asked myself, would liberate Farrell? As I asked the ques-
tion, I already knew that the answer had for some time been
waiting at the back of my mind. I knew too that it wasn't
curiosity that brought me to the hideous town. Farrell the
tragedian had overacted his part, as a result of which he had
become his own prisoner in the abbey. It had been a fine thrill
to give up Jean and his earthly goods. It had been splendid;
it had majesty too. But the aftermath had already lasted for a
year because he saw no way out. It was up to me to show it
to him. It stood out clearly. It wasn't only pride: it was
shame and fear too. Enclosed by the monastery walls he saw
himself closed in for life. He was a volatile man, a senti-
mentalist, and I did not for a second doubt his faith. To
believe and pray on an elegant prie-Dieu is one thing: to lead
a hard life a navvy would buckle under, quite another. I
had read a certain amount about monastic life since his
departure. Those books were further evidence for the case I
was making out. "A large gin and bitters," I said to the
woman, who served me promptly and with a smile. A good
client, her smile said.
I was in a smiling mood too. Good old Farrell; and old
Jasper to the rescue. I walked out of the pub and started
toward the harbor, where, I suspected, gaiety was centered.
My steps were sprightly till I remembered that I had left my
small suitcase in the last pub. I went back. "I've kept it
for you," said the good woman; so I ordered a drink, then
asked her where one should lunch. She reeled off a list of
restaurants and cafés. I forgot the names before I set out
again. Past trolley buses I strode to the promenade. The
smell of fish and chips burst through restaurant and café
doors. Winkles and rock were sold in stalls. I passed the door
of Madam de X, Palmist; then came the entrance to Professor
Tolstoi, who apparently was a palmist too. I looked into a
pub: the noise of the piano drove me out. Gaiety certainly
was centered around the harbor.In a side street I found a quiet pub, large and almost
empty. The man behind the bar was so fat that I thought he
was asleep on his feet. "Do you sell wine by the glass?" I
asked. He stared at me. "A pint of bitter then. What
kind of sandwiches have you?"
"Bread and cheese," he said.
"That'll do me, " I sighed.
I sat down at a table with beer, stale bread and hard cheese.
Farrell needed my support. If necessary I would tackle the
abbot. I had after all no inhibitions where abbots were con-
cerned. Besides, there was little time to waste if Farrell still
wanted Jean at his side on his return. That at least was the
impression I gathered yesterday.
Rosie was under the impression that I was going to Paris on
an assignment. (As a matter of fact I had telephoned an edi-
tor I knew asking him whether he wanted a cute article on
St. Wilfrids-on-Sea. He thought I had gone off my head.)
It was better for her not to know of my errand till my return.
I left Orchard House early in the afternoon, and traveled to
Maidenhead, where I took a taxi to Jean's club. I walked into
the bar: she wasn't there. For a moment I imagined that she
had left. I asked for her. The bartender said she was resting
in her flat; he couldn't disturb her, but she would be back
at six. I insisted it was an urgent matter that brought me
there; therefore he should disturb her. I gave him a drink.
"I'll see what I can do, sir," he said. Jean appeared before
he had time to return to the bar.
"Jasper," she said, "how very nice." She looked anxious
and a little ill at ease.
"We haven't seen you for ages," I said. Now she was less
ill at ease, for she saw I came not as a messenger.
"I was afraid to see you," she said.
"Afraid?" I asked.
She nodded vigorously. "Because I ratted the day Rosie
came to get me."
"I understood your reasons," I said. That was a lie, but it
made her look less anxious.
"Let's go into the lounge," she said.I couldn't fail to observe a youngish, immaculately dressed

Bob and I bowed slightly, and Jean moved on. "He's such a
nice boy," she said, as we entered that big dead room.
"Madly in love with you?" I asked.
She laughed. "He's so sweet and so useless, but I like to
have him around."
He looked, I thought, the sort that suffers from nightmares.
"I'm going to see Francis tomorrow," I said. Jean became
anxious again. "I want to find out for myself," I added.
"Oh," she said. "It wasn't he who asked you to go?"
"No, Jean." I pushed out my beard, though gently this

"What is it?" she echoed. "Everything and nothing. I
can't explain." The bartender brought a bottle of whisky and
three glasses. He hovered for a while in the background;
then, as neither of us spoke, he left the room.
"Yes you can," I said, my eyes on the third glass.
"I can't go and see him," she said, "yet I know I ought to.
If I were certain that he means to stay a monk and has left
everything that connected him with me behind, then I'd go
with you tomorrow. But I'm not certain." She rose to pour
out two drinks; the third glass seemed to ask to be filled too.
She left it empty. "I've told you before, Jasper, that I'm
just an ordinary everyday woman, and if I saw him I would
guess at once whether he remembers me as the woman who was
his mistress; and I know, because he was my lover long enough,
that if he remembered, I'd stand before him as his mistress."
She laughed mirthlessly. "This sounds very stupid and makes
no sense, but that's how it is."
"What you're afraid of is that you might reawaken it all."
"It isn't quite that," she said. "I wish I could put it into
words. When he left he was in a sort of ecstasy, but now that
a whole year has gone by, it can't be ecstasy any more. And
he hasn't seen me for a year, and if he saw me in the flesh..."She jumped up. "That's it, Jasper. If he saw me in the flesh.
Now you understand me? Of course you do. If he saw me in
the flesh
it was his flesh and mine. I can't explain it any
better."
"You'd feel you were placed in an equivocal position if you

"Brother Jerome?" It was almost a cry. "Brother Jer-

"That's his name now," I said, without looking at her, "and

"One more reason why I couldn't go," she said. "I can't go

looked at his woman. You're a man, so you probably don't
see my point. A woman knows when a man, even though it's
buried in the back of his mind, contemplates her as a woman,
which in the long run means one thing alone."
"It's the prerogative of woman to think that she knows," I
said, bowing from the waist. She didn't laugh with me.
"And if he thought that I still had that sort of appeal for
him," she said, "then he would stay in the monastery even
if it broke his heart. I know my Francis."
"In that case you certainly mustn't see him," I said.
With the lukewarm beer and the stale bread before me I
repeated: Jean certainly mustn't see him.
The young man called Bob came to join us. Jean poured
him out a drink before he reached the table. He was a pleasant
young man. Jean said he was an architect but for the moment

generally apologetic. Jean told him I was on my way to see
Francis Farrell, who was now Brother Jerome. Bob blushed.
Poor chap, he surely had to listen day in and day out to her
speaking of Francis and the monastery. I explained to them I
would stay a day and a night, and if necessary another day
and night, because I wanted to know for myself."Can one ever?" Bob said. I assured him one could. "I
think one should pander to one's sense of incertitude," he

"It isn't curiosity alone that takes me into the monastery

that he gave me a thousand pounds as a good-by present,

"Excuse me," said Bob, "I have to make a telephone call."
"A man of tact," I said. "A nice chap."
"He needs guts." With her left hand she massaged her

I explained I didn't cash the check because it could be use-

In the silence of the vast empty pub, with the untouched

"No France with Rosie," I said and tore the check up.

Bob came back. I set my watch; the alarm went off. I said

"With the greatest of pleasure," he said, and went to his

"Would it surprise you," I asked her, "if I came back with

long seconds to answer me."If he came back, then I'd be here for him if he needed me.
I couldn't do otherwise. But frankly I'd be so much happier
if he stayed and his life remained dedicated to God. For me,
a simple, earthy woman, it would be a sort of glory. It would
make me feel a better person."
That was it, I said to a strange beetle that alighted on the
cheese. Let Francis lie on wooden boards, eat his poor heart
out; with us in our viewing chairs getting uplift not from
television but from Brother Jerome.
Bob spoke in the same vein while he drove me to the station.
"It is deeply moving to think of a big business executive, a
rich man who has tasted every comfort and luxury, giving up

was one of the weakest men I knew.
"Your last orders," said the indecently fat publican to

"Where's Abbey Road?" I asked.
It was the second street to the left; you can't mistake it; the
buses went through it and the abbey was toward the top.
Walking slowly I could reach it in five minutes.
Not yet, I decided, as I reached the promenade. There was
plenty of time till four o'clock. Plenty of time too to lock
myself up in a monastery. I would, I frowned, control my
excitement. On the promenade, gaiety was in full swing.
Women with bulging breasts and bellies, their legs fat, red,
and full of varicose veins, frisked in tight swimming suits; a
band played; children shrieked; an airplane buzzed in the
hot sky like a wasp in a bathroom; and lovers shrieked like
idiots in amusement arcades. The old Benedictines had evi-
dently chosen to separate themselves from the world in the
middle of the world at its rawest. A girl of sixteen and well
developed for her age stood hitting a boy of her age. He loved
it. Farther on a mother was telling a father off because, so
she shouted, it was his fault that the howling child chokingwith candy floss had wet its pants. The amusement park
loomed up. I turned into a side street.
I was hungry. In the comparatively quiet street I found a
Cypriot café. The Cypriot asked: "You please want some
Cyprus specialty?"
"Most certainly." He brought the menu. "Bacon, eggs
and fried bread," I said, without glancing at it.
"With some chips?" I said no chips.
It stuck out a mile that Farrell, an expert in histrionics,
fully appreciated what Rosie, Roger, and especially Jean felt
about it. He saw himself on a cloud rising to Heaven wafted
on incense. But only through their eyes. In order not to let
their eyes down he led a life of sheer misery and wretched-
ness. Didn't even his own brother Matthew say if you were
thus inclined then you left the world young? A caprice, an
impulse, honest and sincere, and a man lifted himself out of
the life he was meant to lead; and because of his pride, which
was not to disillusion others, he kept away from everything
that was as natural as air to him.
"Some Turkish coffee?'"
"By all means."
I hadn't been married to a Catholic woman for four years
for nothing. Rosie had faith; Rosie could be a martyr if the
need arose. She went to Mass on Sundays and holydays of
obligation, to confession and communion once a month; but
all that didn't disrupt her life. Yet she was deeply religious.
If a rose in the garden was of extraordinary beauty, she would
rather take it to the chapel of the Augustinian nuns than put
it in a vase at home. And Rosie loved roses. If she wanted
something or hoped for something to happen, she sank on her
knees and prayed. She had prayed for my conversion prac-
tically since we met, and as she was a good Christian girl, she
went on with it instead of giving it up as a bad job. Farrell
could, in fact should, give the same sort of outlet to his own
faith, but stop indulging in excessive enthusiasm. I changed
that to the excess of enthusiasm. It wasn't for him and he
wasn't for it. Besides, his own life was still waiting for him.He hadn't erased it because it hadn't run its cycle. "The
gold cigarette lighters are waiting to light cigarettes," I said.

Abbey Road was to the left. It was broad, and mounted
toward the station. I walked slowly. A big black cloud passed
overhead; a trolley bus whined past, then there was the abbey.
It was a vast, ugly Victorian house, the bricks dark, and it
could have been an oversized almshouse or a smaller peniten-
tiary. On each side of the abbey ran a high wall built with
the same bricks, and facing the abbey on the other side of the
road stood the church. Same bricks. A number of riotous
tourists milled between church and abbey. As I came near I
saw a figure in a black habit and cowl dart across the road.
For a second I thought it was Farrell. It wasn't. I ap-
proached the door.
"Please do not ring if no answer is needed," read a legend
above the bell.
But I needed an answer.

The lay brother who opened the door was small and elderly;
his eyes were surprisingly vivacious. "I think the guestmaster
is expecting me," I said.
"I'll take you to the waiting room," he said, "then I'll find
Father Paul for you. This way, please."
We went along the whitewashed cloisters, his sandals mak-
ing no noise, my shoes an infernal racket. So it sounded to
me. Sacred and profane noise, I said to myself, snickering
silently into my beard. From then on I walked practically ontiptoe. The cloisters were long; the monk shuffled along sound-
lessly, and soundless it all was in front of and behind us. “Is
one allowed to smoke?" I asked.
He turned his little head toward me. "Not in the cloisters,"
he said, "but we're nearly there. You may smoke in the
waiting room."
"I'll wait till we get there," I said. He said nothing.
Pursued and preceded by silence, we entered the waiting

crucifix and a colored picture of the Blessed Virgin. Above the
door was a picture of a bearded man. St. Benedict, I presumed.
First I made sure there was an ashtray, then I lit up, half ex-

"Good afternoon," he said. "I'm the guestmaster."

"We're all very well," I said. "Roger's marriage must

"Is she well?" I said she was. "I am glad. She suggested
she would visit me with Rosemary, but then couldn't make it.
It is such a tiring journey. Now we're entering the laboratory.
It's very up-to-date, don't you think so?"
"I know nothing about laboratories. Mrs. Walsh has mar-
ried. Surprising, eh?"
"She made such excellent pies. Now I'm going to show you
the gym. That'll impress you."
We went along a corridor. He suddenly stopped. "You
know the life of our Holy Father? If you don't, I could write
a short sketch for you and put it in your room, with Father
Paul's permission."
"Four Hundred Eighty to Five Hundred Forty-Three-those dates aren't certain. He was the patriarch of Western
monks, born in Nursia near Spoleto...”
"I'll let you off," he said, opening the gym's door. He be-
came loquacious pointing out the gym's wonders. I inter-
rupted him:
"Are you happy within yourself? Answer me. I came to
find out." Saying it was a little stuffy in there, he flung the
windows open, then turned to me.
"Father Abbot often says: what is happiness? People
bandy the word about and give it the meaning that suits
them. He also says: happiness is something one feels perhaps
in childhood. But there are so many who didn't have a happy
childhood. Yet they are all alike before the Father."
"I see," I said. "I'm dying to meet the abbot."
"You'll meet him before supper; then ask him to see you
alone. You will see then for yourself how good Our Lord is to
our house in giving us such a splendid abbot. Come to the
window, Jasper. You can see the tennis court for our boys."
I joined him at the window, and like a rotten bridge player
who throws away the winning trump, I said to him: "I have
the impression that Jean contemplates marriage."
Turning around, he almost made a pirouette. "You should
have told me at once. I am pleased, oh I am pleased. I am
sure she'll make a perfect wife. I am so pleased." I could
have kicked the trump.
A small rusty-looking tractor thundered slowly past the
window. Farrell burst into his childlike laughter. "And to
think that I sold those things for decades." He shook with
laughter, then drew himself up. "I am ashamed; I apologize
for all this inane laughter. You won't mind if I leave you
now? I really must. If you want to go out, you can go out
through a door in the wall. It's over there. If you want to,
you can go and have a drink at a hotel which is farther up the
road. It's called King Edward. After the potatoes, I sup-
pose." He laughed again. "Oh, you are a wicked influence,
Jasper. Permit me to withdraw. High Mass is at six thirty."
He hurried off, probably to make an act of contrition or say
fifty "Our Fathers"; and to my annoyance I felt guilty. I lita cigarette in the garden and found the door in the wall. A
small bunch of teen-agers pushed past me blowing whistles.
The King Edward was full of French students who drank lit-
tle but made plenty of noise. "Aren't they the limit?" asked
the waitress. I wondered how the abbot would define the word
limit.
I had a good mind not to go to High Mass. I hadn't come
for that. At six twenty-five I walked to the church. There
weren't many worshippers in the church. I sat down in a
pew well to the back. I heard the patter of feet, looked
around, and there were the brothers with three young civilians
going to their altar. I couldn't pick out Farrell. At six thirty,
High Mass began. The abbot was a long, thin man with rubi-
cund cheeks and snow-white bushy eyebrows. A silver crucifix
hung on his chest, held by a thin silver chain. The male voices
burst into Mass and they seemed to lift the floor, the pews-
hence me-with their voices. I watched their faces. I was
struck by the male strength and male joy of it all. I who had
expected mealy-mouthed girlish creatures praying in rapture
before flower-clad statues found myself among men in the
strongest sense of the word man. They weren't apologizing
for being different from us; they sought no reassurance: they
were there in the ripeness of their joy, their voices rising to
the God who made them capable of feeling that joy. When the
bell rang to Elevation, my knees preceded my thought: un-
derstandably, since one had to partake of their joy.
"Qui manducat meam Carnem et bibit meum Sanguinem:
in me manet, et ego in eo, dicit Dominus."
Vespers followed the Mass immediately, and I was a trifle
dazed when I left the church. The guestmaster was waiting
for me. "We're going in to supper," he said. The other
fathers filed in through the abbey door.
"The singing was very moving, father," I said.
"Liturgy is our great joy," he said. As we moved along
the cloisters, we reached a door, which he opened. There were
chairs around the wall, with a purple armchair like a throne
in the middle. "Here we hold our Chapter meetings every eve-
ning after supper. Father Abbot addresses us till compline onspiritual matters or on business. It was in here that he took
the decision, after listening to us, to buy that school which
the sisters are leaving."
"There is nothing nunnish about you Benedictines, father,"
I said as we moved on.
The corner of his mouth trembled a little, then entering the
grounds he said: "No, Mr. Danesbridge, and you won't see
many flowers in the abbey."
There was a sort of small anteroom through which one en-
tered the boys' dining hall. The abbot came up to me extend-
ing his hand. "Mr. Danesbridge," he said. "How are you?"
"Very well, thank you, Father Abbot," I said. That was
the end of my independence. "I have already seen my father-
in-law-I mean Brother Jerome." He nodded. "May I be-
fore I leave have a talk with you?"
"That will have to be tomorrow morning," he said. “I am
going out at eight in the morning we'll make it ten o'clock,
Mr. Danesbridge."
...
"I was very much moved by..." I began chattily, but the
guestmaster pulled me away from the door saying: "Father
Abbot is going in to supper."
That suited me. Before my eyes floated that million-times-
reproduced picture of the jolly monks in their refectory, the
table strewn with pheasants and studded with Chianti flasks.
I went in with the guestmaster who motioned me to a table.
The white room was dark with the black habits. They all
stood and Grace was intoned. It was quite long. When the
abbot sat down at the longest table in the room, a monk ap-
peared on a rostrum with a lectern and read the Gospel for
the tenth Sunday after Pentecost. "At that time Jesus spokę
this parable to some that trusted in themselves as just and
despised others..."
The supper consisted of sardines, plain lettuce, butter and
cheese. Only the reader's voice was heard. The dishes were
passed soundlessly, and I observed that no glances were ex-
changed either. Farrell served with three other monks, never
looking at me even when putting a plate before the monk whosat facing me; but from time to time he and his serving
brethren cast anxious glances in the abbot's direction. The
three civilians sat at a table not far from us. Probably guests
too. I couldn't take my eyes off the abbot's table. The other
fathers sat beside him as though they were there to protect
him and if need be die for him. There was something heroic
about the way they sat clustered round him. One of the
servers dropped a plate. The abbot wanted to frown but re-
strained himself.
"... because every one that exalteth himself shall be hum-
bled, and he that humbleth himself shall be exalted."
Now perhaps conversation would start. The reader took a
book, opened it and read some story about Irish saints, and
once he got mixed up and the sentence came out senseless. I
thought I saw the suspicion of a smile on the lips of the monk
who sat facing me. I quickly caught his eye: the eye vacantly
ignored me. The three civilians were young fellows and when
we left the dining hall, I asked the guestmaster who they
were. "Postulants," he said. They looked so sure of them-
selves.
"Shall I see Brother Jerome again tonight?" I asked.
"Oh, no, they have to clear up, get the tables ready for
breakfast after Compline, and then they go to bed. We rise
early, have a lot to do, so we go early to bed." We had
crossed over to the abbey. He opened the door of a room
which contained three armchairs. “We call this our senior
common room," he said. "Sit down, Mr. Danesbridge. One
of the brothers here made the armchairs." A bell rang loudly.
"Father Abbott is summoning us," he said, and vanished. I
sat down. There was an ashtray on a small table, so I smoked.
I wondered what would happen next.
Shortly afterward the door opened. "Compline," said the
guestmaster. That was the moment to assert myself. Instead
I followed him over to the church. The voices lifted me out of
myself again; but this time differently. It was as though a
dark cloak had descended on the voices: the dark cloak of
man's mortality. Soon a shroud covered the cloak, wet withthe tears caused by man's sins. A monk in a pew behind mine
tapped my arm. I looked around. He gave me a Missal opened
to the page of the Office of Compline.
"Et averte iram tuam a nobis."
Ah, miserere nostri; and in deep utter silence, silence with-
out end, I fell with them on my knees; and the silence per-
sisted. It was in that silence that I comprehended that in
death the proud brain dies too. No wisecracks, no repartees in
Paradise. My mind dissolved into the silence and a couple of
heavy tears fell into my beard. Miserere nostri; and as the
proud brain wasn't there I had no shame for my tears.
The monks were filing back into the abbey when I emerged
from the church. The guestmaster was waiting for me. “Nine
o'clock only," he said. "We're going to bed but it's too early
for you. You see that door in the wall?" I said I did. Some
monks came past, I thought with Farrell among them, but I
wasn't sure. "The lock will be left on the latch; please lock
it when you come in, and come into the abbey through the
door we use going over to the school. Number 26, don't forget
it, Mr. Danesbridge. We'll have you called rather late, say
half past six?'"
"Suits me fine," I said.
A drink. I hurried down Abbey Road, came into a small
square; at the corner stood a pub, and in I went. A noisy
pianist was playing hot music; the place was crowded. I hur-
ried on, eventually finding the deserted pub with the inde-
cently fat publican. Two old men were playing bar billiards.
I drank whisky at the counter, my eyes surreptitiously glanc-
ing from time to time at the empty table where I had sat with
beer, cheese, flies and my thoughts. I had a frantic desire to
talk, to hear my rasping voice. The publican seemed useless
as an audience, and the Jasper Danesbridge who had sat at
that table was just as useless to me.
"Very warm night," I hazarded.
"Seasonal," he said.
"Quite," I said, and there was nothing more to say.
My plan was to go back after closing time. At ten o'clock Iwas already in the monastery garden. I found the back door,
fell over a broom before finding the light switch, mounted
the stairs, didn't know which way to turn on the landing, and
stood there helplessly. I heard a door open. Footsteps ap-
proached, and in heavy woollen pajamas the guestmaster came
around the corner. "I guessed you wouldn't remember your
room number," he said, and took me to the door, where he
wished me good-night. In the monk's cell," I said half
aloud, as I went into the room. I sat down at the table and
sat there smoking and facing the crucifix for an hour, if not
more. From the street, noise, and the sort of laughter that is
prelude to mating, rose into the night but broke against the
wall. In the abbey, silence was complete, and I thought of
the beginning of the last war when I started my military
career as a rifleman. In barracks and Nissen huts there was no
complete silence at night. They talked in their sleep, whined,
sighed, dribbled and snored. The monks around me slept in
peace.
When I ended my tête-à-tête with the crucifix, I undressed
and got into bed, thinking I wouldn't sleep. Sleep came as
soundlessly as the monks slept. A bell woke me: dawn was a
mixture of opaque and night. A knock came on the door next
to mine.
"Benedicamus Domino," said the knocker.
"Deo gratias," came the reply.
"Benedicamus Domino,” on the door after next. "Deo
gratias," and down the passage "Benedicamus Domino," and
again and again "Deo gratias."
I got up and sat at the table, and when it was half past six
I heard footsteps approaching my door. I was ready to say
"Deo gratias."
"Good morning," said the voice. "It's six thirty."
I looked out into the corridor. It was empty. I took the
towels the monastery had provided and went to the nearest
bathroom. The water was hot. "A monkish bath," I said, but
found nothing funny about it. I returned to the cell and
shaved. While I shaved, it occurred to me that I didn't knowwhether the bed had been hard or not. A bit of lather dropped
on the crucifix. "I am so sorry," I said, wiping it off.
In the cloister I heard my name called. "Am I late for
breakfast, Brother Jerome?" I asked. Farrell's cheeks, I ob-
served, were as red as a freshly scrubbed baby's. Evidently
early to bed and early to rise had its effect.
"Breakfast is over," he said, "but never mind, Jasper.
Come over to the dining hall and I'll get you bacon and eggs.
Father Gregory-he has just finished saying Mass-will be
breakfasting too."
"I never have bacon and eggs,” I said. “I mean for break-
fast."
"But you don't usually get up so early, old Jasper," he
laughed.
We walked over to the school building. It was a gloriously
fine day no clouds, only a stupid helicopter in the sky. At the
other end of the table where I breakfasted sat a father. He
had sat with us the night before too. He was a broad man. I
bowed to him; he bowed to me. I sat down, said it was a fine
day. He bowed, then we sat in silence. Farrell brought me a
plate of bacon and eggs. "You cooked them?" I asked. He
nodded, then went away.
After a while the father rose, came over and bent over
me. "Jasper Danesbridge," he said, "I recognized you last
night."
I stared at him for a moment. "Good gracious,
"' I ex-
claimed, "the colonel." I wanted to get up. "Fancy meeting
you here, sir."
'Father Gregory," he said. "Very glad to see you." He
bowed and returned to his place. He had been my rather
dashing colonel for three years during the war, a gay, chatty
fellow devoted to gossip. Now he was smiling to himself and
smearing jam on a piece of toast. He finished breakfast before
me. As he came past me, he put his hand lightly on my
shoulder. "I wouldn't have allowed you to wear that untidy
beard," he said, patted my shoulder and was gone.
Farrell appeared again. "I'll have to chase you out,'
" hesaid, picking up plates and cups. "Father Abbot is expecting
you at ten."
"I don't think I need to see him," I said. "I had so many
questions to ask him. But now I have none."
"You must see him. Are you going for a stroll?"
"I don't want to go out yet. I'll go to the library, I think."
"You do that. I'll call for you at five to ten. You see I'll
be working in the refectory." We came out into the sunshine.
"What's all that hammering?" He said the carpenters'
shop was next door. "You employ carpenters?"
Farrell laughed. "We employ nobody," he said. "The
carpenters are monks like myself."
"Do I get a bill or how do I pay for my stay?"
"Give Father Paul an offering," he said. "I'll see you then
at five to ten."
Slowly I walked back to the abbey. The fattish hen was
roaming in the grounds. "Beware of Brother Jerome," I
called to her. The abbey was like a busy beehive. Typewriters
clattered; one monk was cleaning windows, another mixed
whitewash, a third flew past me carrying a saw. I unlocked
the library door, took down a life of Gregory the Great, and
sat down. The sun streamed in. I didn't open the book; then
to my surprise Farrell turned up saying it was five to ten.
He took me upstairs to the abbot's waiting room. He
knocked on the door. There was no answer. He looked in,
then said: "He isn't here yet. Perhaps we're too early." A
clock chimed ten, and the abbot appeared. Farrell disap-
peared.
"Come in, Mr. Danesbridge," he said. It was a small room
with a big writing table. A photograph of the cloisters hung
under the crucifix. "Sit down, Mr. Danesbridge," he said,
sitting back in his chair. I sat down and I didn't know what
to say. He was waiting.
"Father Abbot," I said at last, "I came here yesterday
with some stupid preconceived idea. I honestly thought that a
monastery was a sort of refuge for neurotics and those who
are tired of life.""A life of prayer and work is not an easy life, especially as
you have to give yourself completely."
"I see that," I said. I was trying to think up something
else to say. "Father Abbot, what is the procedure of becom-
ing a monk?''
"Six months postulant, six months novice, then lay brother
for one year, then if he wishes to stay he undertakes to stay
for three years. At the end of those three years, if he wants to,
he can leave and he can go in peace."
"He'll never go."
"Who?”
"Brother Jerome."
"Oh yes, you're married to his daughter. He is a good
monk. With him you have had the opportunity of seeing how
God manifests Himself."
"Yes, Father Abbot." Now think of something. "If a monk
leaves, why does he leave?" I deserved to get first prize for
stupid questions.
"You should put the question the other way round: why
does a man want to come?" I nodded. "If a man wants to
leave, he leaves because he is proud. Man is very proud and
many men can't conquer themselves." A shape in a dirty
raincoat outside a bistro door flitted past me. "In monastic
life there can be no pride and no arrogance. St. Benedict said:
kill your will."
"They have to obey you completely, don't they?"
"Yes, Mr. Danesbridge, because I am responsible for them
before God."
Now one more question, I commanded myself, and then you
can go in peace. "Because of that life of prayer and work you
lead, Father Abbot, do you consider yourselves above us who
live outside in the world?"
"That's a very personal question," he said. The bushy
white eyebrows frightened me. Then he saw my confusion and
said in a kinder voice: "I'll answer you from a different angle.
If a man has pushed aside the life outside in the world and
dedicates himself to prayer and work, then it is much easier
for Christ to enter him."I stood up. "You have answered my question perfectly,
Father Abbot. There's a lot I could say, in fact want to say,
but... oh well. I want to thank you very much. Believe me,
my stay here will be unforgettable for me
oh, well, may
I say good-by now?"
"I'll see you at dinner."
"I'm leaving now."
"Then good-by, Mr. Danesbridge," he said, taking my
hand. I let it go abruptly.
Farrell materialized as I came out into the corridor. “I
want to see the guestmaster," I said. "The abbot's a remark-
able man," I said as we went along the corridor.
"Oh yes," said Farrell. "Here we are; I'll wait for you."
I went in. The guestmaster sat at a table littered with papers
and he was writing in a ledger. I gave him my offering and
the library key.
"Not staying for dinner?" he asked. I said I wasn't.
"Good-by then," he said, and returned to his ledger.
In the corridor Farrell came flying toward me with my
suitcase. "With your permission I brought your suitcase
along," he said. "I'll see you to the door, then I must rush
back to work."
We went downstairs and along the cloister. "Give Rose-
mary my blessing, and the children too, especially the little
fellow. How big is he?" I stopped to show him how big he
was. "Splendid," he said. We reached the door. Through
here I had entered yesterday. "And if you see dear Jean,"
he said, "congratulate her on my behalf too."
"It isn't certain," I said.
"Let's hope it is."
He opened the door for me. A crowded bus went by; vaca-
tioners in shorts and silly hats thronged Abbey Road. I
looked at Farrell. He smiled, but I saw he was impatient to
go. I took his hand, squeezed it, then stepped across the
threshold. I turned back.
"Pray for me," I said.UNIVERSITY OF MICHIGAN
